# IdeoLab Marketing Source of Truth (Public Safe Final)

Version: 3.0 - Public Marketing Master Prepared: September 22, 2026 Purpose: Authoritative marketing source for people and AI agents creating IdeoLab articles, videos, social media, emails, landing pages, comparison content, FAQs, scripts, campaigns, product education, and sales-enablement content.

Public-marketing standard: This source is limited to customer-facing product truth: capabilities, benefits, outcomes, use cases, buyer questions, content opportunities, and competitive context.

### 0. How to Use This Source of Truth

Use this source to answer four questions before creating content:

- What can the customer do? Understand the capability in plain customer language.
- Why does it matter? Identify the pain, opportunity, time savings, consistency, leverage, or business outcome.
- What story should we tell? Select a relevant use case, marketing angle, buyer-stage question, or search intent.
- How should we position it? Compare the customer job honestly against current alternatives without exaggeration.
This is a marketing knowledge base, not a technical manual. When a content idea requires details that are not stated here, do not invent them. Use only verified public capabilities and customer outcomes.

#### 0.1 Content hierarchy

Use the following order when deciding what to emphasize:

- The customer problem or desired outcome.
- The IdeoLab capability that addresses it.
- A concrete use case or workflow story.
- The broader IdeoLab advantage when several capabilities work together.
- Competitive context only when it helps the buyer make sense of the category.

#### 0.2 Buyer journey framework

| Stage | Buyer mindset | Content posture |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Awareness | The person feels the problem but may not know the solution category. | Teach the pain, the old way, and a better approach. Keep the product mention light. |
| Consideration | The person is evaluating AI, software, or workflows. | Explain capabilities, alternatives, questions to ask, and workflow differences. |
| Decision / Acquisition | The person is actively evaluating IdeoLab or a named alternative. | Use IdeoLab-specific use cases, fit, objections, comparisons, demonstrations, and a clear next action. |

### 1. Marketing Constitution

#### 1.1 Core IdeoLab narrative

IdeoLab is an AI business creation workspace designed to help people move from business information and ideas to finished marketing work without constantly rebuilding the project in separate tools.

The primary marketing story is:

Research → Understand → Create → Repurpose → Publish → Measure → Improve → Automate

IdeoLab can begin from many kinds of real business material - a website, document, video, image, audio recording, customer review, research finding, written brief, existing social post, or a simple idea - and help turn that material into useful marketing outputs.

The important customer benefit is continuity. The work that informs a project can stay with the project, so the next article, presentation, graphic, social post, image, site, or campaign does not have to begin from zero.

The second important story is review and refinement. IdeoLab is built for ongoing creation rather than one-shot output. People can review direction, improve the work, create variations, and continue using strong assets in later marketing.

The third story is one business idea becoming many useful assets. Research can become a presentation. A video can become social content. Real photos can become posts. Customer reviews can become messaging. A website can become research for a campaign. The value comes from turning existing business material into more useful work.

#### 1.2 Primary audiences

- Business owners and founders who need more professional marketing output without building a large internal creative team.
- Marketing operators responsible for producing content across multiple formats.
- Agencies, consultants, and freelancers managing research, client context, creation, and repeatable delivery.
- Local businesses that need stronger visibility, research, content, lead pages, graphics, and follow-up.
- Creators, educators, coaches, authors, experts, and course businesses that need to repurpose knowledge into multiple formats.
- Ecommerce and product businesses that need product visuals, campaigns, presentations, graphics, and social content.
- Sales and business-development teams that need prospect research, presentations, proof assets, and relevant outreach angles.

#### 1.3 Canonical public marketing terminology

| Term | Public marketing definition |
| --- | --- |
| IdeoLab | The overall AI business creation workspace. |
| Business | A workspace for a company, client, brand, or operation. |
| Agency account | A way to organize multiple Businesses for multi-client or multi-brand work. |
| Lab | A visual project workspace where business material, conversations, research, and creations can live together. |
| Canvas | The visual working surface inside a Lab. |
| Card | A movable item on the canvas representing source material, a conversation, a note, research, or a creation. |
| Source | Business material brought into a project so it can inform thinking or creation. |
| Chat | A conversation area used to think through material and create from it. |
| Connected context | Source material kept available to a project conversation so it can continue informing the work. |
| Assistant | A major creation area such as Images, Social, Graphics, Slides, Mindmaps, or Sites. |
| Skill | A specific creation job within an Assistant. |
| Creation | A finished AI-produced output or organized set of outputs. |
| Creative Brief | A review checkpoint used in selected creation experiences to confirm direction before the finished asset is produced. |
| Studio | A dedicated workspace for reviewing and refining a finished creation. |
| Automation | A repeatable workflow that can handle recurring business work with less manual effort. |

#### 1.4 Public claim rules

- Lead with the customer problem, job, and outcome rather than a product control.
- Describe customer-visible capabilities and benefits; do not speculate about behind-the-scenes technology.
- Do not make absolute superiority claims unless they are independently substantiated and specifically approved.
- Do not present dynamic counts, network lists, limits, or availability as permanent without re-verifying them.
- Acknowledge that specialist competitors can be excellent at their core job; position IdeoLab around the connected business workflow where that is the real advantage.
- Do not present unreleased capabilities as available.

### 3. Current Competitive Landscape - September 2026

This section exists to prevent future content agents from using outdated straw-man positioning. It is not a ranking of the products. Each competitor is strong in different jobs, and current comparison content should acknowledge those strengths before explaining the IdeoLab workflow difference.

#### Gamma creation modes

Gamma supports Generate, Paste, and Import modes for presentations/documents/webpages, with AI refinement after creation.

Official source: https://help.gamma.app/en/articles/7838093-how-do-i-create-a-new-presentation-document-or-webpage-in-gamma Research date: September 21, 2026.

#### Gamma presentations

Gamma can build a deck from a prompt, outline, or document and supports editing, presenting, sharing, and export.

Official source: https://gamma.app/products/presentations Research date: September 21, 2026.

#### Beautiful.ai AI presentations

Beautiful.ai uses a staged AI presentation workflow, accepts supporting files/links, and emphasizes shaping the story before design.

Official source: https://support.beautiful.ai/hc/en-us/articles/12885226948109-Creating-a-presentation-with-AI Research date: September 21, 2026.

#### Pitch Agent

Pitch Agent can create and edit presentations from prompts, uploaded files, and brand context.

Official source: https://pitch.com/use-cases/ai-presentation-maker Research date: September 21, 2026.

#### Adobe Express AI presentations

Adobe Express can generate presentations from prompts and source files, provides an editable outline, and exports presentations.

Official source: https://www.adobe.com/express/create/ai/presentation Research date: September 21, 2026.

#### Canva Visual Suite

Canva combines AI-powered social posts, presentations, video, graphics, websites, editing, templates, and broader visual design tools.

Official source: https://www.canva.com/ Research date: September 21, 2026.

#### Canva Content Planner

Canva supports social creation, planning, scheduling, publishing, and performance analytics in Content Planner.

Official source: https://www.canva.com/solutions/content-planning-scheduling/ Research date: September 21, 2026.

#### Buffer platform

Buffer combines social content creation/AI assistance, publishing, community management, performance workflows, and broad social-channel integrations.

Official source: https://buffer.com/ Research date: September 21, 2026.

#### Buffer AI Assistant

Buffer AI Assistant helps draft, brainstorm, repurpose, and adapt social posts in the composer.

Official source: https://buffer.com/integrations/openai-ai-assistant Research date: September 21, 2026.

#### Buffer analytics / Insights transition

Buffer analytics brings cross-channel performance data together; in 2026 Buffer is transitioning from Analyze to Insights.

Official source: https://support.buffer.com/en-us/articles/using-buffer-analyze-nHvyBMdqz8 Research date: September 21, 2026.

#### Sprout AI

Sprout uses AI across content creation, trends, social intelligence, publishing, and strategic analysis.

Official source: https://sproutsocial.com/ai/ Research date: September 21, 2026.

#### Sprout analytics

Sprout supports cross-network analytics, paid/organic analysis, custom reports, and AI-assisted insight summaries.

Official source: https://sproutsocial.com/features/social-media-analytics/ Research date: September 21, 2026.

#### Hootsuite AI

Hootsuite’s AI assistant supports content generation, trend discovery, and social insights tied to live social conversations.

Official source: https://www.hootsuite.com/platform/ai Research date: September 21, 2026.

#### ChatGPT Projects

ChatGPT Projects can hold chats, files, instructions, shared context, and history for ongoing work.

Official source: https://openai.com/academy/projects/ Research date: September 21, 2026.

#### ChatGPT Deep Research

ChatGPT Deep Research can research the web and connected sources, track progress, and refine research interactively.

Official source: https://openai.com/index/introducing-deep-research/ Research date: September 21, 2026.

#### ChatGPT Images

ChatGPT can create and edit images, use reference images, work across aspect ratios, and support iterative image refinement.

Official source: https://help.openai.com/en/articles/11084440 Research date: September 21, 2026.

#### Miro AI

Miro AI can create mindmaps and diagrams on a collaborative canvas and can use existing canvas content as context.

Official source: https://help.miro.com/hc/en-us/articles/28782102127890-Miro-AI-with-Diagrams-and-mindmaps Research date: September 21, 2026.

#### Framer AI

Framer’s AI agents create editable websites and work with the visual canvas, CMS, SEO, analytics, and publishing.

Official source: https://www.framer.com/ai/ Research date: September 21, 2026.

#### Wix AI website and marketing

Wix combines AI website creation with business tools and an AI marketing agent spanning SEO, social, email, and paid ads.

Official source: https://www.wix.com/ai-website-builder Research date: September 21, 2026.

#### Zapier

Zapier connects apps, data, workflows, and AI/agents across a very large integration ecosystem.

Official source: https://help.zapier.com/hc/en-us/articles/37518970271245-What-is-Zapier Research date: September 21, 2026.

#### Make

Make provides visual workflow automation and AI agents across a large app ecosystem with transparent orchestration.

Official source: https://www.make.com/en/ai-automation Research date: September 21, 2026.

#### BrightLocal

BrightLocal focuses on local-search audits, Google Business Profile audits, rankings, reviews, citations, and local-search intelligence.

Official source: https://www.brightlocal.com/platform/ Research date: September 21, 2026.

#### Semrush Local

Semrush Local combines Google Business Profile management, listings, reviews, map-rank tracking, and competitor intelligence.

Official source: https://www.semrush.com/apps/local-seo/ Research date: September 21, 2026.

#### Adobe Express generative design

Adobe Express supports AI-generated editable templates and design workflows for social graphics, flyers, posters, cards, and presentations.

Official source: https://helpx.adobe.com/express/web/create-with-templates/text-to-template.html Research date: September 21, 2026.

#### 3.25 Competitive positioning principles

- Against general AI workspaces: emphasize the business-production system: sources on a visual canvas, connected context, multiple creation types, Studios, and automations. Do not claim general AI cannot retain project context or create images.
- Against Canva / Adobe Express: acknowledge their deep visual editing, templates, and expanding AI. Emphasize research-to-creation continuity, source context, and multiple business workflows rather than claiming they are “just template tools.”
- Against Gamma / Beautiful.ai / Pitch / Adobe Express presentations: acknowledge strong AI deck generation and editing. Emphasize Concept vs Exact workflows and the ability to create the presentation from research/context already living in the broader project.
- Against Buffer / Sprout / Hootsuite / Canva social: acknowledge AI, scheduling, and analytics. Emphasize the range of source-to-post workflows, real-asset repurposing, cross-format creation, and the IdeoLab content-intelligence loop.
- Against Miro: acknowledge Miro’s broad collaboration, diagramming, and AI canvas. Emphasize the direct bridge from business source material into native marketing creations and Studios.
- Against Framer / Wix: acknowledge mature site editing, CMS/SEO/analytics/business tooling. Emphasize campaign continuity, purpose-built site workflows, and the connection to the same research, graphics, social content, and automations.
- Against Zapier / Make: acknowledge far larger automation ecosystems and agentic capabilities. Emphasize built-in content creation and results delivered into IdeoLab Labs for continued marketing work.
- Against BrightLocal / Semrush Local: acknowledge specialist depth in local rankings, listings, reputation, and GBP management. Emphasize rapid research-to-marketing use: reviews, listing intelligence, lead discovery, sales angles, and creation from the same context.

## 4. Platform & Labs

### System-level marketing definition

IdeoLab’s workspace layer brings source material, research, conversations, and finished creations together on a visual canvas. Instead of treating AI as a sequence of disconnected chats, a Lab keeps the working context and outputs side by side so a project can move from source to reasoning to creation without repeatedly rebuilding the brief.

### System-level customer problem

Business work is scattered across browser tabs, documents, AI chats, design tools, folders, and notes. Every switch forces the user to re-explain context, relocate source material, and reconstruct what happened before.

### System-level differentiation

The marketing advantage is not simply an infinite canvas or persistent project memory; other platforms now offer those concepts. The differentiation to emphasize is the combination of business bringing business source material into the project, multiple independent AI conversations, reusable connected project context, and built-in creation options for many finished marketing outputs in the same project surface.

#### Discover & Remix Templates

##### Public definition

Discover is the template gallery - “curated and community-built starting points.” Each entry is a ready-made lab (tagged LAB) you can use as a head start instead of building from an empty canvas. Entries range from official templates (e.g. Secret Webinar Builder Lab Template) to community-shared labs (e.g. COVID-19 Learning Lab, Helix Beta Initiative). Reached from the Discover item in the left sidebar (or View All in the Discover strip on the dashboard).

##### Customer problem this solves

Business work is scattered across browser tabs, documents, AI chats, design tools, folders, and notes. Every switch forces the user to re-explain context, relocate source material, and reconstruct what happened before.

##### Why this feature is valuable

This feature is valuable because it reduces manual handoffs around customer jobs such as: start an AI project from a proven workflow instead of a blank canvas, preview a reusable Lab before copying it, learn how a complete workflow is structured. It also matters as part of a larger system: the source, conversation, or finished output can remain connected to the broader IdeoLab project instead of being treated as an isolated one-off task.

##### Why this matters in the IdeoLab experience

The marketing advantage is not simply an infinite canvas or persistent project memory; other platforms now offer those concepts. The differentiation to emphasize is the combination of business bringing business source material into the project, multiple independent AI conversations, reusable connected project context, and built-in creation options for many finished marketing outputs in the same project surface. At the feature level, the most defensible advantage is how the capability contributes to a connected project rather than existing as a separate utility.

##### Detailed use-case library

###### Derived marketing scenario 1: Start an AI project from a proven workflow instead of a blank canvas

Situation. A user needs to start an AI project from a proven workflow instead of a blank canvas but the work is slowed by context switching, repeated setup, or manual handoffs.

What the feature enables. Discover & Remix Templates gives the user a direct way to perform that job inside the IdeoLab workspace using the customer-facing capability described above.

The marketing opportunity. Show the feature in the context of a complete project, not as an isolated button. Connect the job to the broader workflow where the source or work can continue into the next marketing activity.

#### Labs: Connected AI Workspaces

##### Public definition

A Lab is the core workspace in IdeoLab - a canvas where sources (URLs, docs, videos, social posts, research-leads) and AI-created outputs (images, slides, graphics, social posts, mindmaps) live side-by-side as cards. Each lab belongs to a Business and has chats, creations, and uploaded resources scoped to it.

##### Customer problem this solves

Business work is scattered across browser tabs, documents, AI chats, design tools, folders, and notes. Every switch forces the user to re-explain context, relocate source material, and reconstruct what happened before.

##### Why this feature is valuable

This feature is valuable because it reduces manual handoffs around customer jobs such as: keep research, source material, chats, and creations in one workspace, build a visual AI workflow around a project, return to a project without reconstructing the context. #### Canvas Design Elements

##### Public definition

Opens the Design elements picker - a dropdown panel (not a modal) with a search bar and four categories of decorative, drag-and-droppable items that live on the canvas. These are decoration/annotation items - they are not chat-created; you place them by dragging them out of the panel onto the canvas, where each becomes a normal movable, resizable, deletable card.

##### Customer problem this solves

Business work is scattered across browser tabs, documents, AI chats, design tools, folders, and notes. Every switch forces the user to re-explain context, relocate source material, and reconstruct what happened before.

##### Why this feature is valuable

This feature is valuable because it reduces manual handoffs around customer jobs such as: sketch a flow on the canvas instead of opening a whiteboard app, annotat a created asset for feedback, label and sectioning a busy canvas. It also matters as part of a larger system: the source, conversation, or finished output can remain connected to the broader IdeoLab project instead of being treated as an isolated one-off task.

##### Why this matters in the IdeoLab experience

The marketing advantage is not simply an infinite canvas or persistent project memory; other platforms now offer those concepts. The differentiation to emphasize is the combination of business bringing business source material into the project, multiple independent AI conversations, reusable connected project context, and built-in creation options for many finished marketing outputs in the same project surface. At the feature level, the most defensible advantage is how the capability contributes to a connected project rather than existing as a separate utility.

##### Detailed use-case library

###### Use case 1: Sketching a flow on the canvas instead of opening a whiteboard app

Situation. A founder or product person is mid-session in a lab, ideas coming fast, and needs to map out a sequence - a user flow, a funnel, a decision tree - right where the work is happening. What they want to add. Boxes, arrows, and labels dragged straight onto the canvas to turn loose thinking into a structure they can see. The problem this feature solves. The usual move is to break flow, open a separate whiteboard tool, rebuild context there, then screenshot it back in. The switch kills momentum and the diagram ends up living somewhere disconnected from the actual work. What it enables. The Regular Shapes and Sketch Shapes categories drop rectangles, diamonds, and arrows directly onto the canvas, where each becomes a movable, resizable card. The diagram is built in place, next to the chats and creations it relates to. The opportunity. The thinking stays visual and stays in one workspace. The flow sits beside the assets it describes, so anyone opening the lab later sees both the plan and the output together. ##### Use case 2: Annotating a created asset for feedback

Situation. A designer or marketer has a created image or graphic on the canvas and needs to point out what to change - this corner is off, move that element, try a different colour here. What they want to add. Arrows, callouts, and short text labels placed right on top of and around the asset, marking exactly what they mean. The problem this feature solves. Describing visual changes in words is slow and ambiguous, and exporting the asset into a separate markup tool just to draw an arrow is overkill. Feedback gets vague, and revisions miss the point. What it enables. Doodle Icons, arrows, and text cards drag straight onto the canvas over the asset, so the feedback is spatial and unmistakable - pointing at the actual thing rather than describing it. The opportunity. Whoever picks up the revision sees precisely what was meant, the first time. Review cycles get shorter because nothing is left to interpretation. ##### Use case 3: Labelling and sectioning a busy canvas

Situation. A strategist or researcher has filled a lab with many cards - research pulls, notes, created assets - and the canvas has become a wall of stuff that’s hard to navigate. What they want to add. Headings, dividers, and grouping labels that carve the canvas into readable sections so the layout makes sense at a glance. The problem this feature solves. Without visual structure, a busy canvas turns into clutter where nobody - including the person who built it - can find anything a week later. The knowledge is all there but the map is missing. What it enables. Text cards and shapes from the panel act as section headers, frames, and dividers placed around clusters of cards, giving the canvas a clear visual hierarchy. The opportunity. The workspace becomes self-explanatory. Collaborators orient themselves instantly, and the person who built it can return weeks later and still know where everything sits. ##### Use case 4: Pinning a note or reminder right where it matters

Situation. Someone working in a lab needs to leave a quick note - a to-do, a question, a “don’t forget this” - attached to a specific spot on the canvas rather than buried in a chat. What they want to add. A sticky note or short text card dropped exactly next to the thing it refers to. The problem this feature solves. Notes typed into a chat scroll away and lose their connection to whatever they were about. A reminder that isn’t anchored to the relevant card is a reminder that gets missed. What it enables. The Text & Media category drops a Sticky Note or Text card onto the canvas at the exact point it’s needed, where it stays visible and tied to its context. The opportunity. Important reminders stay in view, anchored to the work. Nothing critical drifts off into chat history. ##### Use case 5: Mocking up a layout before committing to creation

Situation. A marketer or designer wants to rough out roughly how a piece should be arranged - where the headline goes, where an image sits, the overall composition - before spending. What they want to add. Placeholder boxes, shapes, and labels arranged on the canvas to stand in for the final elements. The problem this feature solves. Jumping straight to creation without a layout in mind means iterating expensively, and there’s no lightweight way to block out a composition first. The plan and the production get conflated. What it enables. Regular Shapes and Text cards act as quick placeholders, letting the user block out a composition on the canvas before any asset is created - a low-cost wireframe right in the lab. The opportunity. The user works out the arrangement first and creates with a clear target, cutting wasted iterations and getting to a usable result faster. ##### Use case 6: Adding personality to a workspace for a client walkthrough

Situation. A freelancer or agency is about to share a lab with a client and wants the canvas to feel intentional and presentable rather than like a raw scratchpad. What they want to add. Decorative touches - doodle icons, sketch-style shapes, accent labels - that frame the work and make the canvas read as a considered presentation. The problem this feature solves. A bare canvas of cards looks like a work-in-progress dump, which undercuts the perceived value of the work even when the work itself is strong. First impressions get formed on presentation. What it enables. Doodle Icons and Sketch Shapes add visual warmth and framing around the substantive cards, dressing the workspace up without rebuilding it elsewhere. The opportunity. The client sees a polished, deliberate workspace and takes the work more seriously. The same lab doubles as a presentation surface, no export required. ##### Use case 7: Building a moodboard before committing to a creation

Situation. A designer or brand owner is about to create a flyer or social set and wants to gather the look-and-feel references - colours, vibes, example styles - in one place first. What they want to add. A loose wall of sticky notes, shapes, and icon accents that collect the visual direction before any asset is made. The problem this feature solves. Inspiration usually lives scattered across browser tabs and screenshots folders, disconnected from where the work actually happens, so the direction is half-remembered by the time creation starts. What it enables. Text & Media sticky notes, Regular Shapes as swatches, and Doodle Icons drag straight onto the canvas to assemble a moodboard right beside the chat that will create from it. The opportunity. The creative direction is decided and visible before a single. ##### Use case 8: Running a decision or voting board in a review

Situation. A team is reviewing several created options on the canvas and needs to mark which ones to keep, cut, or revisit. What they want to add. Quick visual markers - checkmarks, stars, arrows, short labels - placed directly on the options being judged. The problem this feature solves. Decisions made verbally or in a separate doc lose their link to the actual options, so “we liked the second one” turns into confusion later about which one that was. What it enables. Doodle Icons (star, heart, check), arrows, and text labels drop onto and around each option, recording the verdict spatially on the same surface as the work. The opportunity. The review’s outcome is unambiguous and self-documenting - anyone reopening the lab sees exactly what was chosen and why.

#### Upload Video & Analyze Recordings

##### Public definition

Upload a video file from your computer. IdeoLab watches and catalogs what’s on screen and transcribes the audio, then lands the video on the canvas as a card so chats and workflows can answer questions about it.

##### Customer problem this solves

Business work is scattered across browser tabs, documents, AI chats, design tools, folders, and notes. Every switch forces the user to re-explain context, relocate source material, and reconstruct what happened before.

##### Why this feature is valuable

This feature is valuable because it reduces manual handoffs around customer jobs such as: turn a customer testimonial into a month of marketing, make a recorded walkthrough answer questions about what was on screen, pull structured insight out of a long recorded talk. It also matters as part of a larger system: the source, conversation, or finished output can remain connected to the broader IdeoLab project instead of being treated as an isolated one-off task.

##### Why this matters in the IdeoLab experience

The marketing advantage is not simply an infinite canvas or persistent project memory; other platforms now offer those concepts. The differentiation to emphasize is the combination of business bringing business source material into the project, multiple independent AI conversations, reusable connected project context, and built-in creation options for many finished marketing outputs in the same project surface. At the feature level, the most defensible advantage is how the capability contributes to a connected project rather than existing as a separate utility.

##### Detailed use-case library

###### Use case 1: Turning a customer testimonial into a month of marketing

Situation. A marketer or small-business owner has recorded a glowing customer testimonial - a happy client on a Zoom call, a video review, a recorded interview - and wants to mine it for everything it’s worth across their channels. What they want to bring in. The exact words the customer used, the moments that hit hardest, and the specific outcomes they mentioned - captured precisely so nothing gets paraphrased or lost. The problem this feature solves. Watching a testimonial back and typing out the good parts by hand is slow, and people rarely do it. The best lines stay trapped inside a video file that gets watched once and forgotten. The proof exists but never makes it into the marketing. What it enables. With the video on the canvas in Transcript Only mode, every chat in the lab can quote the customer verbatim - pulling the strongest lines, summarizing the story, and shaping them into social posts, ad copy, landing-page quotes, or email snippets without anyone re-watching the recording. The opportunity. One testimonial becomes a stream of believable, customer-voiced content. The exact phrasing that convinced one buyer goes on to convince the next. The marketer builds a library of social proof from footage they already had, instead of letting it sit unused. Bringing the video in transcribes it automatically and turns the transcript into reusable copy in the same workspace - replacing both the transcription bill and the copy-paste-and-rewrite time.

###### Use case 2: Making a recorded walkthrough answer questions about what was on screen

Situation. An operations lead, product manager, or trainer has a screen recording - a software walkthrough, a process demo, a recorded configuration session - where the important information is shown, not narrated. Half the steps happen silently on screen. What they want to bring in. Not just the spoken words but the on-screen actions - which buttons were clicked, what settings were shown, what the interface looked like at each step. The problem this feature solves. A plain transcript captures the audio and misses everything visual. Documenting a screen recording by hand means scrubbing frame by frame, pausing constantly, and writing down what’s on screen - tedious and error-prone, and it falls apart the moment the recording is long. What it enables. Uploaded in Full Video Analysis mode, IdeoLab watches the on-screen content and transcribes the audio. Chats in the lab can then answer questions about what was visible - not just what was said - so the team can draft step-by-step documentation, SOPs, or help articles straight from the recording. The opportunity. A single screen recording becomes the source for written documentation that anyone can follow. New hires get accurate guides built from how the work is actually done. The person who recorded it stops being the only one who knows the steps, and the knowledge survives past the meeting. ##### Use case 3: Pulling structured insight out of a long recorded talk

Situation. A consultant, researcher, or content creator has a long recorded session - a webinar, a keynote, a recorded panel, an hour-long expert interview - packed with valuable points buried across its runtime. What they want to bring in. The verbatim quotes, the key arguments, and the section-by-section structure of a talk that’s far too long to re-watch every time they need something from it. The problem this feature solves. A two-hour recording is a black box. Finding the one quote you remember means scrubbing the timeline blind. Summarizing it means watching the whole thing again with a notepad. The insight is in there, but the cost of retrieving it is high enough that it mostly stays locked away. What it enables. With the talk transcribed on the canvas (a low-res export fits up to roughly two hours within the 250 MB ceiling), chats can surface exact lines, summarize any section, and reorganize the content into outlines, articles, or quote pull-lists - all without replaying the video. The opportunity. One recorded talk becomes the seed for a blog post, a thread, a set of clips, and a reference document. The expert’s best ideas get repurposed across formats. Long-form recordings stop being one-time events and become an ongoing content resource. Bringing the video in handles the transcription automatically and makes the result instantly searchable and summarizable in the lab, replacing both the service fee and the manual note-taking.

###### Use case 4: Repurposing a product demo without rewatching it

Situation. A founder or product marketer has a recorded product demo - a sales-call screen share, a feature walkthrough, a launch video - and needs to spin it into social copy, feature announcements, and website blurbs. What they want to bring in. What the product does, how it was described, which features were shown on screen, and the exact language used to explain the value. The problem this feature solves. Translating a demo into marketing copy usually means re-watching it several times, once for each piece of content, and reconstructing the details from memory or scattered notes. It’s repetitive, and small details get lost or misremembered. What it enables. In Full Video Analysis mode the demo’s on-screen features and spoken explanation both become available to every chat in the lab, so copy for different channels can be drafted from one accurate source - each one grounded in what the product actually showed and said. The opportunity. A single demo recording fuels an entire launch: the social posts, the feature page, the email, the ad. The messaging stays consistent because it all traces back to the same source. The team ships more, faster, from footage they already produced. ##### Use case 5: Building a knowledge base from training and onboarding recordings

Situation. A team lead or HR manager has a stack of recorded training sessions and onboarding calls and wants the knowledge inside them to be searchable and reusable, not locked in video files nobody opens twice. What they want to bring in. The explanations, the policies, the demonstrated procedures, and the on-screen examples from sessions that were recorded once and are too long to re-watch. The problem this feature solves. Training videos are a graveyard of useful knowledge. Nobody re-watches a 90-minute onboarding session to find one answer. Turning them into searchable written material by hand means transcribing and summarizing each one - work that almost never gets prioritized. What it enables. Each session uploaded with Full Video Analysis lets chats answer questions about both what was said and what was demonstrated on screen, so the team can build FAQs, written guides, and quick-reference docs from recordings they already have. The opportunity. Recorded training stops being a one-time event and becomes a living knowledge base. New hires get answers without booking time with a senior colleague. The same explanation, given once, serves everyone who joins afterward. ##### Use case 6: Capturing the exact words from a recorded meeting or interview

Situation. A journalist, researcher, or project manager has a recorded interview or meeting and needs precise, attributable quotes - not a rough recollection of who said what. What they want to bring in. The verbatim record of the conversation, accurate enough to quote directly, attribute correctly, and reference later without disputing what was actually said. The problem this feature solves. Working from memory or hand-scribbled notes leads to paraphrasing and mistakes. Transcribing a recording by hand to get it right is slow, and outsourcing it adds cost and turnaround time before the work can even begin. What it enables. With the recording transcribed on the canvas, chats can surface exact lines, summarize what each speaker covered, and pull attributable quotes - so the writeup, the article, or the meeting notes are grounded in what was genuinely said. The opportunity. The interview becomes a precise, quotable source the moment it’s uploaded. Quotes are accurate, attribution holds up, and the writeup gets done faster because the raw material is already in text. The recording is captured once and trusted as the record. Bringing the recording in transcribes it on upload and makes it immediately quotable and summarizable in the lab, replacing both the fee and the wait.

###### Use case 7: Mining a slide-led talk where the value is in the slides, not the narration

Situation. A course creator or analyst has a recorded presentation - a recorded slide deck talk, a financial review, a recorded pitch - where the substance lives in the slides on screen: the charts, the bullet points, the numbers. The speaker only gestures at what the slides already spell out. What they want to bring in. The on-screen content itself - the figures, the slide headings, the diagrams shown frame by frame - not just the loose spoken commentary that points at them. The problem this feature solves. A transcript of this kind of talk is nearly useless: it captures “as you can see here, that’s up significantly” while the actual number sits on a slide the transcript never sees. The real information stays locked in the visuals, and reconstructing it means pausing on every slide and copying it down by hand. What it enables. Uploaded in Full Video Analysis mode, IdeoLab watches the on-screen slides and transcribes the audio, so chats in the lab can answer questions about what was actually shown - the figures, the headings, the visuals - and reorganize the deck’s content into notes, summaries, or written recaps without anyone scrubbing slide to slide. The opportunity. A slide-led recording stops being a black box where the audio says one thing and the screen says another. The deck’s substance becomes queryable, so a single recorded talk turns into accurate written takeaways grounded in what the slides displayed. ##### Use case 8: Turning a recorded podcast or audio-led episode into show notes

Situation. A podcaster or host has a recorded episode - a long conversational interview, a solo audio show, a recorded discussion - and needs to produce show notes, timestamps, and pull-quotes from it for publishing. What they want to bring in. The full spoken record of the episode - every point raised, every notable line - captured accurately enough to summarize and quote without replaying the audio. The problem this feature solves. An audio-led episode is purely spoken, so its entire value is in the talk; but writing show notes means listening back to the whole thing with a notepad, and finding a specific quote means scrubbing the timeline blind. The episode publishes, but the written assets around it lag behind or never get made. What it enables. With the episode added in Transcript Only mode, the spoken record lands on the canvas and every chat in the lab can summarize the discussion, pull exact quotes, and shape the content into show notes, descriptions, and social snippets - all without re-listening. The opportunity. Each episode arrives with its written companion ready to draft the moment it’s uploaded. The host’s best lines get repurposed into promotion, and the back catalog becomes searchable text instead of a wall of audio files nobody revisits.

##### Marketing angle library

- Turning a customer testimonial into a month of marketing. Lead with: Watching a testimonial back and typing out the good parts by hand is slow, and people rarely do it. Payoff: One testimonial becomes a stream of believable, customer-voiced content.
- Making a recorded walkthrough answer questions about what was on screen. Lead with: A plain transcript captures the audio and misses everything visual. Payoff: A single screen recording becomes the source for written documentation that anyone can follow.
- Pulling structured insight out of a long recorded talk. Lead with: A two-hour recording is a black box. Payoff: One recorded talk becomes the seed for a blog post, a thread, a set of clips, and a reference document.
- Repurposing a product demo without rewatching it. Lead with: Translating a demo into marketing copy usually means re-watching it several times, once for each piece of content, and reconstructing the details from memory or scattered notes. Payoff: A single demo recording fuels an entire launch: the social posts, the feature page, the email, the ad.
- Building a knowledge base from training and onboarding recordings. Lead with: Training videos are a graveyard of useful knowledge. Payoff: Recorded training stops being a one-time event and becomes a living knowledge base.
- Capturing the exact words from a recorded meeting or interview. Lead with: Working from memory or hand-scribbled notes leads to paraphrasing and mistakes. Payoff: The interview becomes a precise, quotable source the moment it’s uploaded.
- Mining a slide-led talk where the value is in the slides, not the narration. Lead with: A transcript of this kind of talk is nearly useless: it captures “as you can see here, that’s up significantly” while the actual number sits on a slide the transcript never sees. Payoff: A slide-led recording stops being a black box where the audio says one thing and the screen says another.
- Turning a recorded podcast or audio-led episode into show notes. Lead with: An audio-led episode is purely spoken, so its entire value is in the talk; but writing show notes means listening back to the whole thing with a notepad, and finding a specific quote means scrubbing the timeline blind. Payoff: Each episode arrives with its written companion ready to draft the moment it’s uploaded.
- The context-switch tax. Show how much work is lost when research, briefs, AI chats, and finished assets live in separate tools.
- The project remembers the work. Position the Lab as a durable working surface rather than a one-off prompt box.
- From source to finished asset without re-briefing. Demonstrate one source feeding several creation types.

These are content/search intent phrases, not claimed keyword-volume rankings. Validate volume separately if an SEO campaign requires it.

#### URL & Web Page Sources

##### Public definition

Bring in a public web page, shared document, or spreadsheet as research material so IdeoLab can help summarize it, answer questions about it, or turn its content into new marketing work.

##### Customer problem this solves

Business work is scattered across browser tabs, documents, AI chats, design tools, folders, and notes. Every switch forces the user to re-explain context, relocate source material, and reconstruct what happened before.

##### Why this feature is valuable

This feature is valuable because it reduces manual handoffs around customer jobs such as: put a competitor’s page on the table before you respond, let the lab read your spreadsheet so you don’t have to retype it, turn an article into the source for what you create next. It also matters as part of a larger system: the source, conversation, or finished output can remain connected to the broader IdeoLab project instead of being treated as an isolated one-off task.

##### Why this matters in the IdeoLab experience

The marketing advantage is not simply an infinite canvas or persistent project memory; other platforms now offer those concepts. The differentiation to emphasize is the combination of business bringing business source material into the project, multiple independent AI conversations, reusable connected project context, and built-in creation options for many finished marketing outputs in the same project surface. At the feature level, the most defensible advantage is how the capability contributes to a connected project rather than existing as a separate utility.

##### Detailed use-case library

###### Use case 1: Putting a competitor’s page on the table before you respond

Situation. A marketer, founder, or agency strategist is about to build a counter-offer, a comparison page, or a pitch, and the competitor they’re up against has already laid out their positioning on a public landing page. What they want to bring in. The competitor’s full page - the headline, the offer, the feature list, the pricing language - captured exactly as it reads today, not as it was remembered from a glance last week. The problem this feature solves. Working from memory or a few pasted screenshots means the actual wording slips away, and re-reading the page in another tab while writing breaks concentration every few minutes. The detail that would have sharpened the response gets lost in the back-and-forth. What it enables. Once the page is a card on the canvas, every chat and workflow in the lab can quote it, contrast it, or react to it directly - drafting counter-copy that answers the competitor line by line, or feeding a comparison creation that’s grounded in their real claims. The opportunity. The response lands sharper because it’s anchored to what the competitor actually says, not a fuzzy impression of it. The team moves from reacting to anticipating, and the same source can be reused across the landing page, the sales deck, and the ad copy without re-reading anything. ##### Use case 2: Letting the lab read your spreadsheet so you don’t have to retype it

Situation. A sales lead, campaign manager, or operations owner keeps the numbers that matter - a lead list, a campaign performance tab, a budget tracker - in a Google Sheet, and now wants the lab’s chats to reason over that data. What they want to bring in. The live spreadsheet, with its rows and columns intact, so the figures inside it become something the lab can summarize, query, or turn into a narrative. The problem this feature solves. Copy-pasting cells into a chat is lossy and error-prone, and re-typing data by hand introduces mistakes the moment the sheet changes. The numbers exist, but they stay trapped in a tool the rest of the workflow can’t see. What it enables. With the sheet imported as a card, a chat can pull a summary of campaign results, surface the top leads, or draft a stakeholder update straight from the data - and any creation built afterward stays anchored to those real figures instead of invented ones. The opportunity. Reporting and analysis that used to mean exporting, reformatting, and re-explaining become a single question asked of the lab. The same spreadsheet source backs a summary email, a results graphic, and a slide in one sitting. ##### Use case 3: Turning an article into the source for what you create next

Situation. A content creator, educator, or marketer has found a long article, report, or blog post that says exactly what their audience needs to hear, and they want to reshape it into a deck, an infographic, or a tight summary. What they want to bring in. The article’s full text, captured cleanly, so it can become the raw material for a new creation rather than something to read and paraphrase by hand. The problem this feature solves. Reading a long piece and manually distilling it into talking points is slow, and switching between the article and the canvas to copy passages breaks the flow. The good material is there, but the labor of extracting it is what kills the project. What it enables. Once the article lives as a card, a chat can summarize it into bullets, a Slides creation can build a deck from its structure, or a Graphics creation can pull its key stats - all working from the same captured text, so nothing gets misremembered or misquoted. The opportunity. One strong source becomes many outputs across formats, each grounded in the same material. The creator spends their time on angle and framing instead of re-reading and retyping. ##### Use case 4: Anchoring every creation to the brief instead of reminding the AI each time

Situation. A consultant, project lead, or solo operator has a brief, a strategy doc, or a positioning statement living in a Google Doc, and wants everything they build in the lab to stay true to it. What they want to bring in. The brief itself - the goals, the tone, the constraints, the audience - as a persistent source the lab can refer back to instead of something pasted into every prompt. The problem this feature solves. Re-explaining the brief in each new chat is repetitive and inconsistent, and details drift as the work goes on. Without a single anchored source, different creations start contradicting the very document that was meant to align them. What it enables. With the doc imported as a card, every chat and workflow on the canvas can be pointed at it, so drafts, decks, and graphics all stay aligned to the same brief without the brief being re-typed into each prompt. The opportunity. The work stays coherent across a whole project because one source of truth sits on the canvas. New collaborators inherit the brief by opening the lab instead of being briefed verbally, and the output stops drifting as the session goes long. ##### Use case 5: Capturing a Drive document that lives outside the lab

Situation. A team member has reference material - a research write-up, a policy document, a past proposal - sitting in Google Drive, and needs its content available inside the lab where the new work is happening. What they want to bring in. The Drive document’s text, brought across so it stops being a file in another tab and becomes a source the lab can actually work with. The problem this feature solves. Material stranded in Drive has to be opened, scrolled, and copied by hand whenever it’s needed, and that friction means it often just gets ignored - the new work gets built without the context that already existed. What it enables. As a card on the canvas, the document’s content can be quoted, summarized, or transformed by any chat in the lab, so prior research and existing material feed directly into what’s being created now. The opportunity. Knowledge that was already written down stops being re-derived from scratch. The lab becomes the place where existing documents and new creations meet, instead of new work being done in isolation from what the team already knows. ##### Use case 6: Grabbing a page fast by dropping the link on the canvas

Situation. Someone is mid-flow building inside a lab - connecting cards, running chats - and comes across a page they want to fold in without breaking stride to open a modal and fill in a field. What they want to bring in. A web page or document they already have the link to, added with as little ceremony as possible so the import doesn’t interrupt the work in progress. The problem this feature solves. Stopping to open a modal, paste, and confirm for every source adds up across a busy session. The friction is small each time but it accumulates, and it pulls focus away from the actual building. What it enables. A useful page or document can be added to the project quickly and become reusable source material for research, writing, and creation. The opportunity. Capturing sources becomes a reflex rather than a task. The canvas fills with the references the work actually needs, because adding one costs almost nothing and never breaks concentration. ##### Use case 7: Repurposing your own published page into the next format

Situation. A marketer or small-business owner already has a live page out in the world - a product page, an about page, a published blog post - and now needs to spin its message into a deck, a set of social posts, or a one-page summary. What they want to bring in. Their own published page exactly as it currently reads, so the new creation stays true to the wording and claims already public, instead of a half-remembered version that might contradict the live site. The problem this feature solves. Rewriting from memory risks drifting from what the page actually says, and copy-pasting sections from the live site into each new prompt is slow and easy to get wrong. The canonical copy is already published, but nothing downstream can see it. What it enables. With the page imported as a card, every chat and workflow in the lab can quote it, summarize it, or transform it - building a deck, a thread, or a recap that mirrors the live page’s language without anything being retyped. The opportunity. One published page seeds many derivative formats, all consistent with what’s already live. The message stays unified across the website, the deck, and the posts because they all trace back to the same fetched source. ##### Use case 8: Keeping a reference source on hand for a whole working session

Situation. A consultant or analyst is working through a lab over an afternoon and keeps coming back to the same external reference - a guidelines page, a standards document, a source report - across several different chats. What they want to bring in. That reference page or document brought in once, so its text stays available on the canvas for the length of the session rather than being re-fetched or reopened in another tab every time it’s needed. The problem this feature solves. Reopening the same page in a separate tab and re-copying from it each time a different chat needs it is repetitive friction, and the relevant passages get pasted slightly differently each time. The source is the same, but the access keeps being re-done. What it enables. Because the imported card is scoped to the lab and reusable by every chat and workflow on the canvas, each new chat can quote, summarize, or transform the same captured text without the page being added again - one fetch serves the whole session. The opportunity. A single authoritative reference underpins everything built in the lab, so separate chats stay consistent with the same source instead of each interpreting it afresh. The canvas holds the reference once and the work reuses it indefinitely.

#### Canvas Text Nodes

##### Public definition

Adds an editable text card directly to the canvas. No modal opens - the card appears immediately, ready to type into. The card has a green header with a T icon and a three-dot menu, plus a small card toolbar (Full Screen · Zoom · Copy Text). It’s good for notes, briefs, labels, and reusable prompts you want kept on the canvas.

##### Customer problem this solves

Business work is scattered across browser tabs, documents, AI chats, design tools, folders, and notes. Every switch forces the user to re-explain context, relocate source material, and reconstruct what happened before.

##### Why this feature is valuable

This feature is valuable because it reduces manual handoffs around customer jobs such as: keep the brief in front of you instead of in another tab, sav a prompt that actually worked so chats can reuse it, label regions so a busy canvas stays readable. It also matters as part of a larger system: the source, conversation, or finished output can remain connected to the broader IdeoLab project instead of being treated as an isolated one-off task.

##### Why this matters in the IdeoLab experience

The marketing advantage is not simply an infinite canvas or persistent project memory; other platforms now offer those concepts. The differentiation to emphasize is the combination of business bringing business source material into the project, multiple independent AI conversations, reusable connected project context, and built-in creation options for many finished marketing outputs in the same project surface. At the feature level, the most defensible advantage is how the capability contributes to a connected project rather than existing as a separate utility.

##### Detailed use-case library

###### Use case 1: Keeping the brief in front of you instead of in another tab

Situation. A marketer or agency owner kicks off a lab to create assets for a client, with the brief - tone, target audience, the do’s and don’ts - sitting in a doc, an email, or a Slack thread somewhere else. What they want to do. Keep the brief visible right next to the cards they’re creating, so every chat and every variation stays anchored to what the client actually asked for. The problem this feature solves. With the brief living in a separate app, they’re constantly switching windows to re-check it, and the further the work goes the easier it is to drift off-spec. Each context switch breaks focus and reintroduces the risk of forgetting a constraint. What it enables. A Text card drops onto the canvas immediately - no modal, no upload - and they paste the brief straight in. It sits alongside the work as a permanent reference, movable to whatever corner stays out of the way. The opportunity. The whole workspace becomes self-contained: the brief, the chats, and the outputs share one surface. Anyone who opens the lab later sees the intent behind the work, not just the result. ##### Use case 2: Saving a prompt that actually worked so chats can reuse it

Situation. A creator or solopreneur lands on a prompt phrasing that produces exactly the style they want, after several rounds of trial and error inside a chat. What they want to do. Park that winning prompt somewhere durable on the canvas so they - and the chats on this lab - can reach for it again instead of rebuilding it from memory. The problem this feature solves. Good prompts get buried in chat history and are painful to dig back out. Retyping from memory rarely reproduces the exact phrasing that worked, and the quality drifts. What it enables. A Text card holds the reusable prompt out in the open, with Copy Text in the card toolbar to lift the whole thing in one click. Chats on the same canvas can reference it whenever the same kind of output is needed. The opportunity. A small library of proven prompts builds up on the canvas over time, turning one good result into a repeatable one. The lab starts to carry its own institutional memory. ##### Use case 3: Labelling regions so a busy canvas stays readable

Situation. An operations lead or project owner has a canvas that’s grown into dozens of cards across several workstreams, and it’s getting hard to tell at a glance which cards belong to which effort. What they want to do. Drop clear headings over each region - “Launch assets”, “Research”, “Drafts to review” - so the layout reads like a board instead of a pile. The problem this feature solves. An unlabelled canvas forces everyone to remember the spatial layout in their head. New collaborators land in chaos, and even the owner loses time hunting for the right cluster. What it enables. Lightweight Text cards act as section labels, dropped wherever a heading is needed and styled large enough to scan. They move freely as the canvas reorganises. The opportunity. The canvas becomes navigable to anyone who opens it, not just the person who built it. Pairs naturally with Group once a labelled cluster is ready to be bundled. ##### Use case 4: Capturing a decision before it evaporates

Situation. A founder or team lead is mid-flow in a lab when a decision gets made - “we’re going with the blue palette”, “skip the long-form version” - the kind of thing that’s obvious now and forgotten next week. What they want to do. Write the decision down on the spot, right where the work is happening, without breaking stride to open another app. The problem this feature solves. Decisions made in the flow of work rarely get recorded anywhere, so they get re-litigated later or quietly contradicted. Opening a separate doc to jot a one-liner is enough friction that it usually doesn’t happen. What it enables. A Text card appears instantly in edit mode - one click, type the note, click away to commit. The decision now lives beside the cards it applies to. The opportunity. The canvas accumulates the reasoning behind the work, not just the output. When someone asks “why did we do it this way”, the answer is right there. ##### Use case 5: Sketching an outline before sending it to a chat

Situation. A content marketer or coach knows roughly what they want a creation to say - the key points, the order, the must-include details - but wants to shape that thinking before committing it to a prompt. What they want to do. Draft the structure as plain text on the canvas first, get it right, then hand it to a chat as the basis for a creation. The problem this feature solves. Composing structure directly in a chat composer is cramped and disposable - there’s no good place to think out loud before sending. Outlining in a separate document means copy-pasting back and forth across apps. What it enables. A Text card becomes a scratch outline that stays put on the canvas. Once it reads right, its contents feed a chat via Copy Text or a connected card, so the creation starts from a considered brief instead of an off-the-cuff prompt. The opportunity. Better inputs produce better outputs on the first try, cutting down the rounds of refinement. The outline also stays on the canvas as a record of what was asked for. ##### Use case 6: A running to-do list that lives where the work does

Situation. A freelancer or small-business owner working through a lab keeps generating loose ends - “redo the headline”, “ask the client about pricing”, “still need a square crop” - the kind of follow-ups that pile up while the real work is happening. What they want to do. Track the open tasks for this lab in one spot that’s always in view, instead of in a separate to-do app that they’ll forget to check. The problem this feature solves. Loose ends jotted in another app drift away from the work they belong to, so they get missed or re-discovered the hard way. The friction of switching apps to add a single line means most of them never get written down at all. What it enables. A Text card drops on the canvas in edit mode and becomes a live checklist - click in, add a line, click away. It sits beside the cards it refers to and can be moved to a corner that stays out of the way, with Full Screen to expand it when the list grows long. The opportunity. Each lab carries its own task list right on the canvas, so the work and the things still to do never separate. Anyone who opens the lab sees what’s outstanding, not just what’s done. ##### Use case 7: Handing a written brief to a teammate inside the lab

Situation. An agency owner or team lead has set up a lab and wants a colleague to pick up the work, but the instructions for what to do next are sitting in their head or in a message thread elsewhere. What they want to do. Leave the hand-off instructions on the canvas itself so whoever takes over reads them right where the work is, with no separate doc to find. The problem this feature solves. Hand-offs that live in email or chat get detached from the workspace they describe, so the person taking over has to reconcile two places and often misses a detail. The instructions and the work end up in different apps. What it enables. A Text card holds the written hand-off out in the open on the canvas, in plain editable text. The teammate reads it in place and can lift any part of it with Copy Text - and it can be bundled with the relevant cards via Group so the instructions travel with the work they describe. The opportunity. A lab becomes self-explaining: the next person opens it and finds both the work and the directions for it on one surface, instead of chasing context across tools. ##### Use case 8: Parking text you’ll need later without leaving the canvas

Situation. A creator is mid-flow and comes across a phrase, a stat, a tagline, or a snippet of copy they know they’ll want shortly - but not this exact second. What they want to do. Stash it somewhere durable on the canvas so it’s there when the moment comes, without breaking stride to open a clipboard manager or a notes app. The problem this feature solves. Useful scraps held on the system clipboard get overwritten by the next copy, and stashing them in a separate app means a context switch every time. So the snippet is either lost or it costs focus to save. What it enables. A Text card appears instantly in edit mode - drop it, paste or type the snippet, click away. It stays put on the canvas as a holding spot, and Copy Text lifts the whole thing back out in one click when it’s needed. The opportunity. The canvas doubles as a scratchpad for the lab: anything worth keeping for later sits in plain view, surviving as long as the lab does rather than as long as the clipboard does.

#### Import Social Posts as Sources

##### Public definition

Paste a public post URL from Facebook · X · Instagram · TikTok; IdeoLab imports the caption/text/details as a card. As the modal puts it: “Paste a public post from Facebook, X, Instagram or TikTok - we’ll import the caption/text and details.”

##### Customer problem this solves

Business work is scattered across browser tabs, documents, AI chats, design tools, folders, and notes. Every switch forces the user to re-explain context, relocate source material, and reconstruct what happened before.

##### Why this feature is valuable

This feature is valuable because it reduces manual handoffs around customer jobs such as: study the post that’s already winning, lift a real customer’s words into your marketing, learn a caption style worth emulating. It also matters as part of a larger system: the source, conversation, or finished output can remain connected to the broader IdeoLab project instead of being treated as an isolated one-off task.

##### Why this matters in the IdeoLab experience

The marketing advantage is not simply an infinite canvas or persistent project memory; other platforms now offer those concepts. The differentiation to emphasize is the combination of business bringing business source material into the project, multiple independent AI conversations, reusable connected project context, and built-in creation options for many finished marketing outputs in the same project surface. At the feature level, the most defensible advantage is how the capability contributes to a connected project rather than existing as a separate utility.

##### Detailed use-case library

###### Use case 1: Studying the post that’s already winning

Situation. A social media manager or creator sees a competitor’s post taking off - high engagement, lots of shares - and wants to understand what made it land before drafting their own take on the same idea. What they want to bring in. The post’s caption and details, captured exactly as published, so the hook, the structure, and the framing become something the lab can actually examine. The problem this feature solves. Eyeballing a viral post in the feed and trying to remember what worked is unreliable, and screenshots flatten the text into something the lab can’t read or reason over. The lesson is right there but it stays locked in another app. What it enables. With the post imported as a card, a chat can break down its hook, analyze its angle, and help draft an original post that borrows the structure without copying the words - all grounded in the real caption rather than a vague recollection. The opportunity. Content decisions get made from evidence of what already performed instead of guesswork. The same winning post can inform a whole batch of new content, raising the odds that the next post lands too. ##### Use case 2: Lifting a real customer’s words into your marketing

Situation. A founder or marketer spots a public post where a real customer raves about their product, tells a transformation story, or makes a point better than any ad copy could. What they want to bring in. The customer’s own caption and details, captured faithfully, so their authentic voice can become source material for testimonials, case studies, or social proof. The problem this feature solves. Retyping someone else’s words risks errors and loses nuance, and paraphrasing a genuine testimonial drains the authenticity that made it valuable. The proof exists but moving it into the workflow by hand degrades it. What it enables. Once the post is a card, a chat can shape it into a testimonial graphic, weave it into a case study, or pull a quote for a landing page - always working from the exact words the customer wrote. The opportunity. Real customer language, which converts better than anything written in-house, flows directly into marketing assets. One genuine post becomes a quote on the site, a slide in the pitch, and a social repost, all faithful to the original. ##### Use case 3: Learning a caption style worth emulating

Situation. A creator or copywriter admires how a particular account writes - the rhythm of their hooks, the way they open, the tone they hold - and wants to absorb that style into their own captions. What they want to bring in. Example captions from that account, brought in as text, so the writing patterns become something the lab can study and adapt rather than something to imitate from memory. The problem this feature solves. Trying to reverse-engineer a writing style by scrolling and rereading is slow and imprecise, and the specifics of what makes it work slip away the moment you switch back to your own draft. What it enables. With the captions imported as cards, a chat can analyze the style - sentence length, opening moves, voice - and help draft new captions that carry the same energy applied to the user’s own topics. The opportunity. A distinctive voice that took someone else years to develop becomes a learnable pattern. The creator levels up their own writing by working from concrete examples instead of vague admiration. ##### Use case 4: Building a swipe file from posts across every platform

Situation. A content strategist is researching a campaign and wants to gather strong examples wherever they appear - a Facebook ad, an X thread, an Instagram carousel caption, a TikTok description - into one place to work from. What they want to bring in. A collection of standout posts from across Facebook, X, Instagram, and TikTok, each captured as a card, so a cross-platform research set lives together in one lab. The problem this feature solves. Inspiration scattered across four apps is impossible to work with as a set - bouncing between platforms and trying to hold them all in mind defeats the point, and screenshots in a folder can’t be queried or compared. What it enables. Each post imported as a card sits alongside the others on the canvas, so a chat can compare approaches across platforms, spot common patterns, or synthesize the best of all of them into a content direction. The opportunity. A genuine cross-platform view of what works replaces the platform-by-platform guessing most teams settle for. The whole swipe file becomes a single source the lab can reason over at once. ##### Use case 5: Turning a thread or caption into a longer piece of content

Situation. A creator wrote a post that resonated - a punchy X thread, a thoughtful Instagram caption - and wants to expand it into a newsletter, a blog post, or a script while it’s still fresh. What they want to bring in. Their own published post, captured as text, so the original idea becomes the seed for a longer creation instead of something to rewrite from scratch. The problem this feature solves. Working from a post in one tab while drafting in another means copying fragments back and forth and losing the thread of the original. The idea already proved itself, but re-capturing it by hand is friction that often stalls the expansion. What it enables. With the post imported as a card, a chat can expand it into long-form, restructure it for a different format, or use it as the backbone for a script - all anchored to the exact post that already connected with an audience. The opportunity. Proven short-form ideas get a second life as long-form content without starting over. One resonant caption becomes a newsletter, a blog post, and a video outline, each true to what already worked. ##### Use case 6: Capturing a post fast by dropping the link on the canvas

Situation. Someone is in the middle of working inside a lab and scrolls past a post worth keeping, but doesn’t want to break their flow to open a modal and fill in a field just to save it. What they want to bring in. A public post they already have the link to, added with as little ceremony as possible so the capture doesn’t interrupt the work in progress. The problem this feature solves. Opening the Add Social URL modal, pasting, and confirming for every post adds friction that accumulates across a research-heavy session and pulls focus from the actual building. What it enables. A public social post can be brought into the project quickly so its wording and context can be studied and reused as research. The opportunity. Collecting social sources becomes a reflex rather than a chore. The canvas fills with the references the work actually needs, because adding one costs almost nothing and never breaks concentration. ##### Use case 7: Repurposing your own old post for a new platform

Situation. A creator or small-business owner has a post that did well on one platform months ago and wants to reshape it for a different audience - turning a punchy X post into an Instagram caption, or a Facebook update into a TikTok description. What they want to bring in. Their own published post, captured faithfully as a card, so the original wording and framing become the starting point for the new version instead of a half-remembered draft. The problem this feature solves. Digging an old post back out of a feed and retyping it loses the exact phrasing that worked, and rewriting from memory drifts from what actually resonated the first time. What it enables. With the post available as source material, IdeoLab can help adapt the original message for another platform, audience, or format instead of starting from a blank page. The opportunity. A back catalogue of posts that already performed becomes reusable raw material, so one good idea works across every platform instead of being spent once. ##### Use case 8: Settling an internal debate with the original post on the table

Situation. A marketing team is arguing about whether a particular post is worth modeling their next campaign on, and everyone is working from a different screenshot or a different memory of what it actually said. What they want to bring in. The exact post - its caption and details, captured as published - as a single shared card in the lab, so the whole team is reasoning over the same source rather than competing recollections. The problem this feature solves. Debating a post from memory or scattered screenshots means the conversation keeps slipping back to “what did it actually say,” and no one can quote it precisely or interrogate it together. What it enables. Once the post is available in the project, the team can study the real wording, compare approaches, and make decisions from the same source. The opportunity. A judgment call gets made from the actual artifact instead of from whoever remembers it best, so the decision is grounded and the team aligns faster.
