#### Document & Spreadsheet Sources

##### Public definition

Upload a document; IdeoLab pulls out text, headings, and tables so chats and workflows can quote it. Supported formats: TXT · PDF (text) · DOCX · CSV · XLSX · XLS. Max 100 MB / ~500 pages.

##### Customer problem this solves

Business work is scattered across browser tabs, documents, AI chats, design tools, folders, and notes. Every switch forces the user to re-explain context, relocate source material, and reconstruct what happened before.

##### Why this feature is valuable

This feature is valuable because it reduces manual handoffs around customer jobs such as: anchor every creation to the client’s actual brief, put a contract or proposal to work without re-reading it, pull exact figures out of a pdf report. It also matters as part of a larger system: the source, conversation, or finished output can remain connected to the broader IdeoLab project instead of being treated as an isolated one-off task.

##### Why this matters in the IdeoLab experience

The marketing advantage is not simply an infinite canvas or persistent project memory; other platforms now offer those concepts. The differentiation to emphasize is the combination of business bringing business source material into the project, multiple independent AI conversations, reusable connected project context, and built-in creation options for many finished marketing outputs in the same project surface. At the feature level, the most defensible advantage is how the capability contributes to a connected project rather than existing as a separate utility.

##### Detailed use-case library

###### Use case 1: Anchoring every creation to the client’s actual brief

Situation. An agency strategist or freelancer is starting work for a client and has the brief in hand - a DOCX brief, a PDF scope document, a filled-out questionnaire - full of the details that everything they produce needs to respect. What they want to bring in. The client’s goals, target audience, tone rules, do’s and don’ts, and key messaging - captured exactly as written so nothing gets misremembered halfway through the project. The problem this feature solves. A brief that lives in a downloads folder gets read once and drifts out of mind. Re-reading it before every task, or copy-pasting the relevant bits into each prompt, is tedious - so the work slowly wanders away from what the client actually asked for. What it enables. With the brief uploaded, IdeoLab extracts its text and headings so every chat in the lab can quote it directly. Creations stay grounded in the client’s own words, and the AI can check drafts against the brief without anyone re-typing it. The opportunity. The whole lab works from one source of truth. Revisions go down because the work matches the brief the first time. The client feels heard because their requirements show up faithfully in the output. The strategist spends their energy on ideas instead of policing consistency. ##### Use case 2: Putting a contract or proposal to work without re-reading it

Situation. A consultant, account manager, or operations lead needs to act on a long contract or proposal - answer questions about it, summarize it for a colleague, draft something based on its terms - without rereading thirty pages each time. What they want to bring in. The specific clauses, deliverables, dates, and terms buried inside a long document, retrievable on demand instead of by manual page-flipping. The problem this feature solves. Finding one clause in a long PDF means scrolling and skimming until you hit it. Summarizing a proposal for someone means reading the whole thing and condensing it by hand. The answers are all in the document, but pulling them out is slow every single time. What it enables. Uploaded as a canvas card, the document’s text, headings, and tables become quotable across the lab. Chats can surface exact clauses, summarize sections, compare terms, and draft follow-ups grounded in the document - without anyone scrolling through it. The opportunity. A dense contract becomes something the team can interrogate in plain language. Questions get answered in seconds, summaries are accurate because they trace to the source, and follow-up documents inherit the right terms automatically. The document stops being a wall of text and becomes a tool. ##### Use case 3: Pulling exact figures out of a PDF report

Situation. An analyst, marketer, or founder has a text-based PDF report - a market study, a financial summary, an industry whitepaper - and needs the specific numbers and findings inside it for a deck, a post, or a decision. What they want to bring in. The precise figures, the table values, and the section headings from a report where getting a number wrong would undermine the whole point. The problem this feature solves. Re-keying figures out of a PDF by hand invites transcription errors, and copying tables out of a PDF usually mangles the formatting. The data is right there, but moving it anywhere reliably is fiddly and slow. What it enables. IdeoLab extracts the report’s text, headings, and tables (for text-based PDFs), so chats can quote exact figures and pull table values verbatim. The numbers that go into a summary or a graphic come straight from the source, not from error-prone manual entry. The opportunity. Reports become a dependable data source instead of a copy-paste hazard. Figures land accurately the first time, tables stay intact, and the analyst can trust that what the chat quotes matches the document. Decisions and content rest on numbers that are actually correct. ##### Use case 4: Repurposing a product spreadsheet row by row

Situation. An ecommerce manager or marketer has a product catalog or inventory spreadsheet - a CSV or XLSX with rows of products, specs, and prices - and needs to turn those rows into descriptions, comparisons, or social posts. What they want to bring in. The structured rows and columns - product names, attributes, prices, categories - preserved as data the lab can read and work through systematically. The problem this feature solves. Writing copy for dozens of products means tabbing back and forth between the spreadsheet and the writing surface, transcribing details one product at a time. It’s mind-numbing, slow, and easy to mix up which spec belongs to which item. What it enables. Uploaded as a document card, the spreadsheet’s tables are extracted so chats can reference any row’s values and work through the catalog systematically - drafting per-product descriptions, comparison tables, or batch social copy grounded in the actual data. The opportunity. A catalog that used to mean days of manual copywriting becomes a structured source the lab can process at speed. Every product gets accurate, consistent copy drawn from its real specs. The manager scales content across the whole inventory instead of rationing it to the top sellers. ##### Use case 5: Driving a chart or comparison from structured data

Situation. A marketer, ops lead, or small-business owner has results or metrics in a CSV or XLSX - survey responses, sales figures, a feature matrix - and wants to turn that data into a summary, a comparison, or a visual without rebuilding it by hand. What they want to bring in. The clean rows and columns of a dataset, intact and accurate, ready to be summarized or shaped into something a non-technical audience can grasp. The problem this feature solves. Reformatting raw data into a presentable summary usually means manually re-entering values, which is slow and error-prone. The insight is in the numbers, but the work of making them readable stands in the way of ever sharing them. What it enables. With the spreadsheet’s tables extracted on the canvas, chats can read the structured values directly and use them to drive a summary, a comparison, or a data-led graphic in the lab - working from the real figures rather than a re-typed copy. The opportunity. Raw data becomes something the team can communicate. The numbers reach stakeholders in a form they understand, the values stay accurate because they come from the source file, and the person sharing them looks organized and clear. Insight that used to stay in a spreadsheet finally gets seen. ##### Use case 6: Turning a knowledge base of documents into instant answers

Situation. A support lead, educator, or team manager has a collection of reference documents - policy manuals, product specs, FAQs, training material in TXT, PDF, or DOCX - and wants quick, quotable answers from them instead of hunting through files. What they want to bring in. The full text and structure of reference documents that hold the answers people keep asking for but rarely find quickly on their own. The problem this feature solves. When the answer lives somewhere in a 200-page manual, finding it means knowing where to look and scrolling until you get there. People ask a colleague instead, the same questions get answered over and over, and the documentation goes underused despite being complete. What it enables. Each document uploaded (up to roughly 500 pages each) has its text, headings, and tables extracted, so chats can locate and quote the exact passage that answers a question - turning a pile of reference material into something you can simply ask. The opportunity. The documentation finally earns its keep. Answers come back in seconds with the source text quoted, repeat questions stop landing on the same person, and the team trusts the material because the AI points straight to it. Knowledge that was technically available becomes genuinely accessible. ##### Use case 7: Repurposing one long document into many shorter pieces

Situation. A marketer, educator, or founder has a single substantial document - a whitepaper, a webinar transcript saved as TXT, a long DOCX guide - and needs to spin it out into smaller assets: a summary, a set of key takeaways, an outline for a deck. What they want to bring in. The full text and section headings of one source document, captured intact so every spin-off traces back to the same original wording. The problem this feature solves. Reworking a long piece into shorter formats means rereading it, deciding what matters, and retyping the relevant parts into each new draft - repeated effort that drifts further from the source with every pass. What it enables. With the document uploaded as a canvas card, IdeoLab extracts its text and headings so chats can quote any section on demand and shape it into a summary, a takeaway list, or an outline - all grounded in the one source rather than re-typed from memory. The opportunity. A single anchor document becomes a content well the lab draws from repeatedly. Every derived piece stays faithful to the original, and the work shifts from rereading and retyping to simply asking for the next format. ##### Use case 8: Cross-checking two documents against each other

Situation. A consultant, operations lead, or account manager has two related documents - an old contract and its renewal, a draft proposal and the client’s brief, last quarter’s report and this quarter’s - and needs to see where they agree, differ, or fall short. What they want to bring in. The text, headings, and tables of both documents, side by side in the same lab, so their contents can be lined up instead of held in memory. The problem this feature solves. Comparing two long files by hand means flipping between them, holding one’s details in your head while you scan the other, and hoping you don’t miss the clause or figure that changed. What it enables. Each document uploaded as its own card has its text, headings, and tables extracted and made searchable across the lab, so a chat can quote and contrast passages or table values from both - surfacing the differences without anyone reading them in parallel. The opportunity. Two dense documents become a single answerable question. Discrepancies, gaps, and changes surface in plain language, traced to the exact source text, instead of being caught (or missed) by manual side-by-side reading.

#### YouTube Video & Channel Research

##### Public definition

Bring YouTube content in as research material. The YouTube Import Studio imports a single video - or scans a creator’s channel and bulk-imports selected uploads - and fetches each video’s full title, transcript, and stats automatically so chats can reference what was said.

##### Customer problem this solves

Business work is scattered across browser tabs, documents, AI chats, design tools, folders, and notes. Every switch forces the user to re-explain context, relocate source material, and reconstruct what happened before.

##### Why this feature is valuable

This feature is valuable because it reduces manual handoffs around customer jobs such as: mine a long podcast episode without watching it twice, turn a tutorial into a deck you can teach from, compress a long talk into notes people will actually read. It also matters as part of a larger system: the source, conversation, or finished output can remain connected to the broader IdeoLab project instead of being treated as an isolated one-off task.

##### Why this matters in the IdeoLab experience

The marketing advantage is not simply an infinite canvas or persistent project memory; other platforms now offer those concepts. The differentiation to emphasize is the combination of business bringing business source material into the project, multiple independent AI conversations, reusable connected project context, and built-in creation options for many finished marketing outputs in the same project surface. At the feature level, the most defensible advantage is how the capability contributes to a connected project rather than existing as a separate utility.

##### Detailed use-case library

###### Use case 1: Mining a long podcast episode without watching it twice

Situation. A content marketer or researcher knows a two-hour podcast episode holds a few brilliant quotes and insights, but doesn’t have the time to rewatch it scrubbing for the exact moments. What they want to bring in. The episode’s full transcript and title, fetched automatically, so everything that was said becomes searchable text instead of audio they’d have to sit through again. The problem this feature solves. Pulling quotes from a long video by hand means scrubbing the timeline, pausing, and transcribing by ear - slow, error-prone, and miserable for anything over an hour. The gold is in there but digging it out manually rarely happens. What it enables. With the transcript on the canvas, a chat can surface the strongest quotes, pull out the key arguments, or find every moment a topic came up - working from the complete text, attributed accurately to what was actually said. The opportunity. Long-form conversations become a reusable quote bank and insight source. One episode feeds a newsletter section, a set of social posts, and a blog reference, all grounded in the speaker’s real words. ##### Use case 2: Turning a tutorial into a deck you can teach from

Situation. A trainer, course creator, or team lead found a YouTube tutorial that explains a process well and wants to reshape it into a slide deck for a class, an onboarding session, or an internal workshop. What they want to bring in. The tutorial’s transcript and title, captured cleanly, so its structure and steps become the raw material for a deck rather than something to rewatch and outline by hand. The problem this feature solves. Converting a video into slides normally means watching it with the player on one side and a deck on the other, pausing constantly to note each step - tedious work that turns a thirty-minute video into an afternoon. What it enables. With the transcript imported as a card, a Slides creation can build a deck from the tutorial’s flow, or a chat can outline it step by step - all anchored to what the video actually taught, in the order it taught it. The opportunity. Knowledge that lived only as a video becomes teachable, editable material. The same tutorial can seed a deck, a handout, and a quick-reference summary without watching it more than once. ##### Use case 3: Compressing a long talk into notes people will actually read

Situation. A knowledge worker or student watched (or wants to skip) a long conference talk, keynote, or lecture and needs the substance distilled into bullet-point notes they can scan in two minutes. What they want to bring in. The talk’s complete transcript, fetched automatically, so the full content is available to summarize rather than something to rewatch and condense by memory. The problem this feature solves. Note-taking from a long video means watching the whole thing while typing, and the notes are only as good as the attention paid in the moment. Rewatching to fill gaps doubles the time. The insight is there but capturing it cleanly is the bottleneck. What it enables. With the transcript as a card, a chat can summarize the talk into key points, extract the main takeaways, or pull the action items - producing clean notes from the complete text instead of partial real-time scribbles. The opportunity. Hours of talks become minutes of readable notes that can be shared with a team or saved for reference. Knowledge gets captured reliably instead of half-remembered. ##### Use case 4: Building a research set from a channel in one pass

Situation. A strategist or analyst is studying a creator, a competitor, or a subject-matter expert and wants several of their recent videos as research - not just one, but a representative set to reason across. What they want to bring in. A selection of the channel’s recent uploads, each with its own title, transcript, and stats, brought in together so a body of that creator’s work lives in the lab at once. The problem this feature solves. Importing videos one at a time, or worse, watching each to take notes, makes assembling any real research set prohibitively slow. The breadth needed to spot patterns across a creator’s work never gets gathered. What it enables. Channel-scan mode lets the user filter recent uploads and bulk-import the ones they select, dropping a card per video on the canvas - so a chat can compare themes across the set, track how the creator’s messaging evolved, or synthesize the whole body of work. The opportunity. Genuine cross-video analysis replaces conclusions drawn from a single example. The full set becomes one source the lab can reason over, surfacing patterns no single video would reveal. ##### Use case 5: Working a video with a ready-made framework instead of a blank prompt

Situation. A creator imports a video and wants to do something specific with it - sharpen its hook, audit its opening, restructure its teaching - but isn’t sure how to prompt the lab to get there. What they want to bring in. The video’s transcript as a working source, paired with a proven prompt approach so the analysis starts from a structured framework rather than a blank box. The problem this feature solves. Knowing a video holds potential is easy; knowing how to interrogate it well is hard. A blank prompt leaves most of the value on the table because the right questions never get asked. What it enables. The modal’s YouTube Prompt Hacks panel offers a Long-form Frameworks library - Title Hook Framework Builder, Big Promise Blueprint Builder, Anti-Bounce Opening Upgrade, and more - so the imported transcript can be run through a battle-tested framework that knows what to look for. The opportunity. The user gets expert-level analysis of long-form content without being an expert at prompting for it. The frameworks turn a raw transcript into a structured breakdown of what works and what to fix. ##### Use case 6: Grabbing a single video the moment it’s relevant

Situation. Someone working inside a lab comes across a single YouTube video - a video link or a Short - that’s directly relevant to what they’re building, and wants it in fast without channel scanning or extra steps. What they want to bring in. That one video, with its full title, transcript, and stats fetched automatically, added straight to the canvas so the lab can reference what was said in it. The problem this feature solves. When only one video matters, anything heavier than a quick paste-and-add is overkill. Watching it to take notes, or transcribing it by hand, is wildly out of proportion to grabbing a single source. What it enables. Pasting a video or Shorts URL into the single-video mode shows a preview with a Transcript Available indicator, and one click on ADD VIDEO TO CANVAS drops the card in - title, transcript, and stats ready for any chat or workflow to quote, summarize, or transform. The opportunity. Adding a relevant video becomes a quick reflex, so the right source is on the canvas the moment it’s needed. The work stays grounded in what the video actually said, without ever leaving the lab to watch it. ##### Use case 7: Sizing up a competitor’s catalog by what their numbers say

Situation. A marketer or strategist is studying a competitor’s YouTube presence and wants to understand which of their videos actually landed - not by impression, but by evidence - across a swath of their recent output. What they want to bring in. A selection of the competitor’s recent uploads brought in together, each with its own stats alongside its title and transcript, so the body of work arrives with the numbers attached rather than as titles alone. The problem this feature solves. Judging a competitor’s catalog by eyeballing the channel page mixes guesswork with whichever video happens to be pinned; the per-video signal that separates a hit from a dud never gets gathered in one place. What it enables. Channel-scan mode lets the user filter recent uploads and bulk-import the ones they select, dropping a card per video - each carrying its stats - so a chat can reason across the set: which themes recur in the strongest performers, how the framing differs from the weaker ones. The opportunity. Competitive read-throughs move from impression to evidence. The selected set becomes one source the lab can interrogate, with the stats on each card grounding the comparison instead of relying on memory of the channel page. ##### Use case 8: Spinning one Short into a batch of posts

Situation. A creator or social manager has a YouTube Short that performed well and wants to wring more out of it - turn what was said in those few seconds into a run of social copy - without rewatching and transcribing it by ear. What they want to bring in. That single Short, with its full title and transcript fetched automatically, dropped straight onto the canvas as a source the lab can quote and reshape. The problem this feature solves. A Short is short, but capturing its words precisely enough to repurpose still means scrubbing and re-typing; the line that made it work is easy to paraphrase wrong. What it enables. Pasting the Shorts URL into single-video mode and clicking ADD VIDEO TO CANVAS lands the card with its transcript, so a chat or workflow can transform what was said into a set of posts - anchored to the Short’s actual wording rather than a rough recollection. The opportunity. One strong Short stretches into a week of grounded social copy, every piece traceable to what the video really said, without leaving the lab to rewatch it.

#### Image Sources & Visual Understanding

##### Public definition

Upload one or more image files from your computer. They land as image cards on the canvas, where IdeoLab reads any on-image text and tags key visuals. Images can be used as a reference for Edit Image or Blend Images, or as a source for analysis (e.g. caption creation, brand audit).

##### Customer problem this solves

Business work is scattered across browser tabs, documents, AI chats, design tools, folders, and notes. Every switch forces the user to re-explain context, relocate source material, and reconstruct what happened before.

##### Why this feature is valuable

This feature is valuable because it reduces manual handoffs around customer jobs such as: give a brand image a reference to match, read the text off a screenshot so a chat can use it, size up a competitor’s visual. It also matters as part of a larger system: the source, conversation, or finished output can remain connected to the broader IdeoLab project instead of being treated as an isolated one-off task.

##### Why this matters in the IdeoLab experience

The marketing advantage is not simply an infinite canvas or persistent project memory; other platforms now offer those concepts. The differentiation to emphasize is the combination of business bringing business source material into the project, multiple independent AI conversations, reusable connected project context, and built-in creation options for many finished marketing outputs in the same project surface. At the feature level, the most defensible advantage is how the capability contributes to a connected project rather than existing as a separate utility.

##### Detailed use-case library

###### Use case 1: Giving a brand image a reference to match

Situation. A brand owner or marketer wants a new image to feel like it belongs to their brand - matching an existing look, a colour palette, a style of photography - and has a reference image that captures exactly the feel they’re after. What they want to bring in. A visual example of the style they want - a past creation, a mood image, a brand photo - so the AI has something concrete to anchor to instead of a vague description. The problem this feature solves. Describing a visual style in words is hard and unreliable; “clean and modern with warm tones” means something different to everyone. Without a reference, creations drift off-brand and the back-and-forth to correct them eats time. What it enables. Uploaded to the canvas, the image becomes a card that can be dragged or connected into a chat as a reference for Edit Image or Blend Images, or as a style cue for new image creations. The AI works from the actual look instead of an interpretation of words. The opportunity. New images are easier to keep on-brand because IdeoLab can use the visual reference directly instead of relying only on a written description. The brand stays visually consistent across creations, and the owner stops re-explaining their aesthetic on every request. Their visual identity holds together without a style guide. ##### Use case 2: Reading the text off a screenshot so a chat can use it

Situation. Someone has information trapped in an image - a screenshot of a message, a photo of a slide, a graphic with text baked in, a picture of a printed page - and needs those words as usable text. What they want to bring in. The text that’s locked inside an image, extracted accurately enough to quote, summarize, or build on, rather than retyped by hand. The problem this feature solves. Text inside an image can’t be copied. Getting it out means squinting at the screen and retyping it character by character - slow, and easy to introduce typos in exactly the details that matter. What it enables. IdeoLab reads on-image text (OCR) when the image lands on the canvas, so chats can quote the extracted words directly - turning a screenshot or photo into source text for summaries, replies, or new creations without any manual transcription. The opportunity. Information stops being stranded in image form. A screenshot becomes quotable text in seconds, the retyping disappears, and the details stay accurate because they’re read, not re-keyed. Anything captured as a picture becomes something the lab can actually work with. ##### Use case 3: Sizing up a competitor’s visual

Situation. A marketer or founder has seen a competitor’s ad, post, or product image and wants to understand what makes it work - and how their own could stand out - using the actual visual, not a description of it. What they want to bring in. The competitor’s image itself, including any text on it, so the analysis is grounded in what the competitor really did rather than a secondhand account. The problem this feature solves. Critiquing a visual from memory or a verbal summary loses the details that matter - the exact claim, the layout, the emphasis. Transcribing the on-image copy and describing the composition by hand is tedious and lossy. What it enables. Uploaded to the canvas, the image gets its on-image text read (OCR) and its key visuals tagged, so chats can analyze the composition, quote the competitor’s copy, and reason about what’s shown - a grounded basis for a brand audit or a positioning critique. The opportunity. Competitive analysis gets sharper because it’s based on the real artifact. The marketer can pull the competitor’s exact messaging, spot what’s emphasized, and craft a response that genuinely differentiates. Guesswork gives way to evidence. ##### Use case 4: Putting a product photo to work

Situation. An ecommerce seller or maker has a product photo and wants to do something with it - edit it, blend it into a new scene, write a caption from it, or audit how it presents the product. What they want to bring in. The product image itself, ready to serve as raw material for editing, blending, or analysis rather than just sitting in a folder. The problem this feature solves. A product photo on its own is inert. Turning it into edited variants, composite scenes, or written copy normally means moving it between separate tools - an editor, a writing app - each a context switch that slows the work. What it enables. On the canvas, the photo becomes a card IdeoLab tags for key visuals and can use as a reference for Edit Image or Blend Images, or as a source for caption creation and brand audit - all in the same lab, dragged or connected straight into a chat. The opportunity. One product photo turns into edited variations, lifestyle composites, and ready-to-post captions without leaving the workspace. The seller gets more mileage from a single shot, and the whole flow - from photo to finished asset to copy - stays in one place. ##### Use case 5: Staging several references for a single blended creation

Situation. A creator or designer wants to combine elements from multiple images - a subject from one, a background from another, a style from a third - into one cohesive creation. What they want to bring in. A set of images, all staged together, ready to act as the combined source material for a blended creation. The problem this feature solves. Feeding visual sources in one at a time is clumsy, and assembling a multi-image composite by hand in an editor takes real skill and time. Without an easy way to stage several references at once, ambitious combinations rarely get attempted. What it enables. Multiple images can be selected and added in a single pass (each up to 10 MB), landing as separate cards on the canvas. From there they can be used together as sources for Blend Images or a batch analysis, all available to the same chat. The opportunity. Combining visuals stops being a chore and becomes a quick move. The creator can try ambitious mixes - subject here, setting there, style from a third - and iterate freely. More references staged easily means more creative range from material they already have. ##### Use case 6: Capturing something on screen with a paste instead of a file

Situation. Someone is mid-flow and spots something worth keeping - a chart in a report, a frame from a video, a snippet of a design - and wants it into the lab immediately without saving a file first. What they want to bring in. A quick screen capture of whatever’s in front of them, dropped straight into the workspace without the detour of saving, naming, and uploading a file. The problem this feature solves. The usual capture-save-locate-upload loop breaks concentration and adds friction to a small action. Often the thought of all those steps is enough to skip capturing the thing at all, and a useful reference is lost. What it enables. IdeoLab lets you screenshot and paste an image directly onto the canvas, bypassing the modal entirely. The pasted image becomes a card like any other - its on-image text read (OCR) and key visuals tagged - instantly available to chats for quoting or as a reference. The opportunity. Capturing a reference becomes as fast as the thought to keep it. The workflow stays unbroken, useful visuals get grabbed instead of forgotten, and the canvas fills with material gathered in the moment. Friction stops standing between an idea and acting on it. ##### Use case 7: Turning an existing creation into a caption-ready source

Situation. A social manager or small-business owner has an image they want to post - a finished graphic, a photo, a past creation - but still needs the words to go with it, and wants the caption to actually describe what’s in the picture. What they want to bring in. The image itself as the basis for its own copy, so the caption is written from what the visual shows rather than from a separate description typed out by hand. The problem this feature solves. Writing a caption blind - describing an image in a prompt instead of letting the AI see it - produces generic copy that misses the details that matter, and re-describing the picture in words every time is repetitive work. What it enables. Uploaded to the canvas, the image becomes a card whose key visuals are tagged and whose on-image text is read (OCR), so it can be used as a source for caption creation - the AI writes from the actual visual and any text on it. The opportunity. Captions come out specific to the image because the AI worked from the picture, not a paraphrase. The post-to-caption gap closes, and the copy reflects what viewers will actually see. ##### Use case 8: Auditing an old asset against the brand before it goes back out

Situation. A marketer or agency owner is about to reuse an existing asset - a flyer, a banner, a product shot - and wants to check whether its look and its on-image wording still hold up to the brand before it ships again. What they want to bring in. The asset itself, including the text baked into it, so the audit is based on the real artifact rather than a memory of what it said. The problem this feature solves. Eyeballing an old asset for off-brand drift is unreliable, and the copy printed on it is locked in the image where it can’t be quickly checked or quoted against current messaging. What it enables. On the canvas, the asset becomes a card with its on-image text read (OCR) and its key visuals tagged, so it can serve as a source for a brand audit - a chat can quote the wording and reason about the visual against brand expectations. The opportunity. The review is grounded in the actual asset, so off-brand visuals and stale on-image copy get caught before reuse rather than after the asset is back in circulation.

Payoff: Captions come out specific to the image because the AI worked from the picture, not a paraphrase.

#### Audio Recording & Transcription

##### Public definition

Two ways to add audio to the lab as a transcribable card. Opens a dropdown with Record Audio (in-browser recording) and Upload Audio File (existing file).

##### Customer problem this solves

Business work is scattered across browser tabs, documents, AI chats, design tools, folders, and notes. Every switch forces the user to re-explain context, relocate source material, and reconstruct what happened before.

##### Why this feature is valuable

This feature is valuable because it reduces manual handoffs around customer jobs such as: capture a brief out loud while the thinking is fresh, turn a sales call into content and follow-ups, mine a podcast or interview clip for content. It also matters as part of a larger system: the source, conversation, or finished output can remain connected to the broader IdeoLab project instead of being treated as an isolated one-off task.

##### Why this matters in the IdeoLab experience

The marketing advantage is not simply an infinite canvas or persistent project memory; other platforms now offer those concepts. The differentiation to emphasize is the combination of business bringing business source material into the project, multiple independent AI conversations, reusable connected project context, and built-in creation options for many finished marketing outputs in the same project surface. At the feature level, the most defensible advantage is how the capability contributes to a connected project rather than existing as a separate utility.

##### Detailed use-case library

###### Use case 1: Capturing a brief out loud while the thinking is fresh

Situation. A founder or marketer has a clear idea of what they want - the angle, the audience, the must-haves - and would rather just say it than stop and type it all out. What they want to bring in. Their spoken brief, captured on the spot and turned into text the lab’s chats can work from. The problem this feature solves. Typing a full brief is slow and tends to flatten the nuance; the energy of the idea drains out somewhere between the thought and the keyboard. Many people just don’t write the brief at all and the detail gets lost. What it enables. Record Audio captures the brief directly in the browser, it’s transcribed automatically, and it lands as a card whose transcript the lab’s chats can quote and build on. The opportunity. The full, nuanced brief - said the way the person actually thinks about it - becomes the foundation every creation in the lab draws from, instead of a thin typed summary. ##### Use case 2: Turning a sales call into content and follow-ups

Situation. A sales rep or consultant has just finished a recorded discovery call full of the prospect’s exact words, objections, and priorities. What they want to bring in. The call recording, transcribed, so the prospect’s own language is available to draft a follow-up, a proposal, or tailored content. The problem this feature solves. The most valuable material - how the prospect described their problem - sits trapped in an audio file nobody will replay. Transcribing it by hand or paying a service is enough friction that it usually doesn’t happen, and the follow-up gets written from vague memory. What it enables. Upload Audio File brings the recording in, it’s transcribed, and the transcript card feeds the lab’s chats - so a follow-up email or proposal can quote the prospect directly. The opportunity. The follow-up speaks to exactly what the prospect said they cared about, which lands far harder than a generic recap. The whole call becomes reusable material instead of a one-time conversation. ##### Use case 3: Mining a podcast or interview clip for content

Situation. A content creator or marketer has an interview, a podcast segment, or a recorded talk packed with quotable insight they want to repurpose. What they want to bring in. The audio clip, transcribed, so its best lines can be pulled into posts, articles, or graphics. The problem this feature solves. Finding the good quotes means scrubbing back and forth through audio with a notepad - tedious enough that most clips never get repurposed and the insight stays locked in a format nobody can skim. What it enables. The clip is uploaded and transcribed into a card; the lab’s chats can then quote and reshape the transcript into whatever format the content needs. The opportunity. One recording becomes a week of posts, pull-quotes, and snippets. Spoken material that would have been heard once gets a second life as text-based content. ##### Use case 4: Capturing a meeting’s decisions before they evaporate

Situation. A team lead or project manager has just wrapped a working session where decisions, owners, and next steps were agreed out loud. What they want to bring in. A recording of the discussion, transcribed, so the decisions and action items can be written up without relying on anyone’s memory. The problem this feature solves. Verbal decisions don’t survive the day - by the next morning people remember them differently, and the one person taking notes always misses something. The meeting’s value leaks away. What it enables. Record Audio captures the session, or an existing recording is uploaded; either way it’s transcribed into a card the lab’s chats can turn into a clean summary or action list. The opportunity. Everyone works from the same accurate record of what was actually said and decided. The “wait, what did we agree on” conversation disappears. ##### Use case 5: Bringing customer feedback in their own voice into the work

Situation. A product or support lead has recorded customer calls or voicemails full of real frustration, requests, and praise that should be shaping decisions. What they want to bring in. The customer audio, transcribed, so their literal words can inform messaging, roadmap, or support content. The problem this feature solves. Customer recordings pile up unlistened because reviewing and transcribing them is a chore. The voice of the customer - the single most useful input - goes unused, and decisions get made on assumption instead. What it enables. The recordings are uploaded and transcribed into cards; the lab’s chats can analyse the transcripts for recurring themes, exact phrasing, and unmet needs. The opportunity. Messaging and decisions get grounded in what customers actually said, in their own words, rather than in internal guesswork. The feedback finally gets acted on. ##### Use case 6: Dictating notes in the field without a desk

Situation. An agent, inspector, or consultant is out on a site visit and needs to capture observations the moment they happen, hands often full, no good way to type. What they want to bring in. A spoken record of what they’re seeing, captured in the browser and ready to be written up later. The problem this feature solves. Notes scribbled on the go are messy and incomplete, and details get forgotten by the time the person is back at a keyboard. The richest observations happen exactly when typing is least possible. What it enables. Record Audio captures the observations on the spot in the browser; the transcript lands as a card the lab’s chats can later expand into a report or summary. The opportunity. Nothing observed in the field is lost. The write-up back at the office starts from a complete spoken record instead of fragmentary memory. ##### Use case 7: Turning a recorded talk or training into reusable reference material

Situation. A team lead or course creator has a recorded webinar, workshop, or training session and wants its content available as text the lab can draw on later. What they want to bring in. The session recording, transcribed, so its explanations and examples become a reference the lab’s chats can summarise, restructure, or pull from. The problem this feature solves. A long recording is impossible to skim - finding the part that matters means scrubbing through the whole thing, so the knowledge stays locked in a format nobody revisits and the effort of replaying it is rarely worth it. What it enables. Upload Audio File brings the recording in, it’s transcribed into a card, and the lab’s chats can quote and reshape the transcript into notes, summaries, or follow-up material. The opportunity. A one-time session becomes a lasting, searchable reference instead of an archived file - the content keeps working long after the talk ended. ##### Use case 8: Catching a fleeting idea before it’s gone

Situation. A founder or creator gets a sudden idea - on a walk, between meetings, mid-commute - and knows it’ll be gone within the hour if it isn’t captured now. What they want to bring in. The idea spoken out loud the moment it strikes, captured and turned into text the lab can work from later. The problem this feature solves. Good ideas arrive at inconvenient moments and fade fast; opening a doc to type them out is friction enough that the thought is often lost before it’s recorded. What it enables. Record Audio captures the idea on the spot in the browser, it’s transcribed automatically, and it lands as a card whose transcript the lab’s chats can later expand into a plan or draft. The opportunity. Fleeting ideas get caught at full fidelity instead of half-remembered later - the spark becomes raw material the lab can build on.

#### Canvas Grouping

##### Public definition

An action (not a modal) - select multiple cards first, then click Group (layers/stack icon, 11th in the resource bar) to combine them into a single grouped card that moves and organizes together. Clicking Group with nothing selected does nothing.

##### Customer problem this solves

Business work is scattered across browser tabs, documents, AI chats, design tools, folders, and notes. Every switch forces the user to re-explain context, relocate source material, and reconstruct what happened before.

##### Why this feature is valuable

This feature is valuable because it reduces manual handoffs around customer jobs such as: keep a campaign’s assets from drifting apart, reclaim a canvas that got noisy, treat a research bundle as one input to a chat. It also matters as part of a larger system: the source, conversation, or finished output can remain connected to the broader IdeoLab project instead of being treated as an isolated one-off task.

##### Why this matters in the IdeoLab experience

The marketing advantage is not simply an infinite canvas or persistent project memory; other platforms now offer those concepts. The differentiation to emphasize is the combination of business bringing business source material into the project, multiple independent AI conversations, reusable connected project context, and built-in creation options for many finished marketing outputs in the same project surface. At the feature level, the most defensible advantage is how the capability contributes to a connected project rather than existing as a separate utility.

##### Detailed use-case library

###### Use case 1: Keeping a campaign’s assets from drifting apart

Situation. An agency owner or marketer has built a cluster of cards for one campaign - a few graphics, a couple of chats, a brief - sitting together in a corner of a lab that also holds work for other efforts. What they want to do. Lock that cluster together so it moves as one and stays intact when the canvas gets rearranged around it. The problem this feature solves. Loose cards drift. Every pan, every new card dropped nearby, every cleanup risks scattering a set that only makes sense together. Re-collecting the strays later is tedious and error-prone. What it enables. Selecting the cards and clicking Group combines them into a single grouped card that moves and organises as a unit. The campaign becomes one thing on the canvas instead of a fragile pile. The opportunity. The lab can hold several campaigns side by side without them bleeding into each other, because each one is a self-contained block. ##### Use case 2: Reclaiming a canvas that got noisy

Situation. A solopreneur or project lead has been iterating in a lab for a while, and the canvas has filled with dozens of cards - finished, abandoned, and in-progress - until navigating it has become a chore. What they want to do. Collapse the finished or low-priority clusters down so the active work has room to breathe. The problem this feature solves. A sprawling canvas slows everything down - finding the card that matters means panning past everything that doesn’t. The clutter creates a quiet drag on every interaction. What it enables. Drag-selecting a noisy region and grouping it turns a dozen cards into one tidy card that can be moved aside. The mess becomes a single, ignorable block. The opportunity. Attention goes back to the current task. The history is still there, bundled and out of the way, not deleted. ##### Use case 3: Treating a research bundle as one input to a chat

Situation. A strategist or content creator has gathered several reference cards - sources, notes, an image or two - that together form the context they want a chat to draw on. What they want to do. Hand that whole set to a chat as a single connected input, rather than wiring each card in one by one. The problem this feature solves. Connecting many individual cards into a chat is fiddly and easy to get wrong - miss one and the context is incomplete; the relationship between the pieces is also lost when they’re attached separately. What it enables. Grouping the research first turns the bundle into one card that can be organised and moved as a unit alongside the chat, keeping the related material together as a coherent set. The opportunity. The reference set stays intact and reusable - the same bundle can sit beside multiple chats instead of being reassembled each time. ##### Use case 4: Moving a finished deliverable around as a single piece

Situation. A freelancer or team lead has assembled everything a client deliverable needs - the asset, its variations, the notes that explain it - spread across several cards in one area. What they want to do. Shift that entire deliverable to a new spot on the canvas in one motion, without leaving any part of it behind. The problem this feature solves. Repositioning a multi-card deliverable one card at a time is slow and risks splitting it up - a stray card left in the old position breaks the set. What it enables. Grouping the cards means the whole deliverable moves and organises together. Drag the grouped card and everything inside follows. The opportunity. Deliverables become handle-able objects on the canvas, easy to line up, stage for review, or arrange into a clean reading order. ##### Use case 5: Handing off a clean canvas to a collaborator

Situation. An operations lead is about to bring a teammate into a lab that they’ve been working in solo, and the canvas reflects their own working order more than anything a newcomer would understand. What they want to do. Bundle the canvas into clear, labelled blocks so the collaborator lands in something structured instead of a wall of loose cards. The problem this feature solves. A flat field of cards is opaque to anyone who didn’t build it. Without visible structure, the newcomer wastes time figuring out what belongs with what before they can contribute. What it enables. Grouping related cards into discrete cards - often paired with a Text label per block - gives the canvas a legible shape. Each grouped card reads as one unit of work. The opportunity. The collaborator orients in seconds and starts contributing instead of decoding. The lab scales from one person to a team without a reorganisation effort. ##### Use case 6: Separating two efforts that grew into the same corner

Situation. A marketer started two related pieces of work in the same area of a lab, and over time their cards have intermingled until it’s no longer obvious which card belongs to which effort. What they want to do. Pull each effort into its own self-contained block so the two stop overlapping and can be told apart at a glance. The problem this feature solves. When unrelated cards share one patch of canvas, every move risks grabbing the wrong one, and the boundary between the two efforts exists only in the user’s head. What it enables. Shift+clicking the cards of one effort and clicking Group binds them into a single card; repeating it for the second effort produces a second card. The two efforts become two distinct units that move independently. The opportunity. One crowded corner resolves into clear, separable blocks, each one safe to reposition without disturbing the other. ##### Use case 7: Lining the canvas up into a clean reading order

Situation. A freelancer wants the cards in a lab arranged in a deliberate left-to-right sequence - intro, then options, then the final pick - but the cards exist as scattered loose pieces rather than orderly stages. What they want to do. Turn each stage into one movable object so the whole canvas can be laid out as a tidy sequence. The problem this feature solves. Arranging a sequence out of dozens of individual cards means nudging each one into place and re-nudging the strays - and a single missed card breaks the order. What it enables. Grouping the cards of each stage gives every stage a single grouped card that moves and organises as a unit. Dragging those few cards into line is far quicker than herding every card. The opportunity. The canvas reads as an intentional progression rather than a random field, making it easy to walk someone through stage by stage. ##### Use case 8: Labelling a cluster so its purpose is unmistakable

Situation. A solopreneur has several clusters of cards in a lab and, returning after a break, can’t immediately remember what each pile was for. What they want to do. Give each cluster a visible name so its purpose is obvious without opening any card. The problem this feature solves. A bare pile of cards carries no label - figuring out what it is means inspecting its contents, which defeats the point of grouping at all. What it enables. Grouping the cards into one card and pairing it with a Text label turns the cluster into a single, named block that announces what it holds. The opportunity. The lab becomes self-describing: every grouped card states its own purpose, so picking up where you left off is instant.

#### Multiple Chat Cards & Creation Hubs

##### Public definition

The Chat item spawns a new chat card on the canvas - and that panel is the single most important object in a lab. Everything else in the resource bar brings material onto the canvas; the chat card is where that material gets turned into something.

##### Customer problem this solves

Business work is scattered across browser tabs, documents, AI chats, design tools, folders, and notes. Every switch forces the user to re-explain context, relocate source material, and reconstruct what happened before.

##### Why this feature is valuable

This feature is valuable because it reduces manual handoffs around customer jobs such as: start a clean thread without disturbing the one that’s working, run parallel threads on the same source material, isolate an experiment so the main thread stays safe. It also matters as part of a larger system: the source, conversation, or finished output can remain connected to the broader IdeoLab project instead of being treated as an isolated one-off task.

##### Why this matters in the IdeoLab experience

The marketing advantage is not simply an infinite canvas or persistent project memory; other platforms now offer those concepts. The differentiation to emphasize is the combination of business bringing business source material into the project, multiple independent AI conversations, reusable connected project context, and built-in creation options for many finished marketing outputs in the same project surface. At the feature level, the most defensible advantage is how the capability contributes to a connected project rather than existing as a separate utility.

##### Detailed use-case library

###### Use case 1: Starting a clean thread without disturbing the one that’s working

Situation. A marketer or creator has a chat that’s deep into one direction - a long back-and-forth that produced good results - and now wants to explore something different on the same lab. What they want to do. Begin a fresh conversation from a clean slate while leaving the productive thread exactly as it is. The problem this feature solves. Continuing in the same chat muddies a focused thread with an unrelated tangent, and the accumulated context can pull new prompts off course. But abandoning the good thread isn’t an option either. What it enables. Clicking Chat spawns a brand-new, independent panel as its own card on the canvas. The original keeps its history intact; the new one starts empty. The opportunity. Multiple lines of work coexist on one lab, each with its own clean context, instead of being forced through a single conversation. ##### Use case 2: Running parallel threads on the same source material

Situation. A strategist or agency owner has reference material on the canvas and wants to pursue several angles from it at once - different audiences, different formats, different tones. What they want to do. Work multiple directions in parallel rather than waiting for one thread to finish before trying the next. The problem this feature solves. A single chat is sequential - every idea waits in line behind the last. Comparing approaches means scrolling back and forth through one tangled history. What it enables. Several chat cards spawned onto the same canvas can each pursue their own angle independently, all drawing on the shared material around them. (How each panel is configured is covered in 4.2 Chat interface.) The opportunity. Approaches can be developed and compared side by side, laid out spatially on the canvas instead of buried in one linear transcript. ##### Use case 3: Isolating an experiment so the main thread stays safe

Situation. A solopreneur or designer is happy with where their primary chat is heading but wants to try a riskier idea that might lead nowhere. What they want to do. Run the experiment somewhere it can’t derail the work they’ve already invested in. The problem this feature solves. Testing a wild idea in the main thread risks steering a good conversation into a dead end, and there’s no clean way to back out once the context is polluted. What it enables. A separate spawned chat acts as a sandbox - fully independent, discardable if it flops, promotable if it works. The main thread never feels the experiment. The opportunity. Risk-taking becomes cheap. Bold ideas get tried without jeopardising the safe, productive line of work running alongside. ##### Use case 4: Giving each collaborator their own space on a shared lab

Situation. A small team is working in the same lab, and more than one person wants to drive a conversation toward their own piece of the project. What they want to do. Let each person own a chat card without stepping on someone else’s thread. The problem this feature solves. One shared chat turns into a crossed-wires mess when several people prompt into it - context collides and nobody’s thread stays coherent. What it enables. Each collaborator spawns their own chat card on the canvas, laid out in their own area, all within the same lab. The threads run independently while the work stays in one place. The opportunity. A lab scales from solo to team use without fragmenting across separate workspaces - everyone’s contributions land on a shared surface. ##### Use case 5: Keeping conversations organised by topic instead of one endless scroll

Situation. A content creator or project lead has a lab covering several distinct topics, and the single chat they started has become a sprawling transcript that mixes all of them together. What they want to do. Split the work into a chat per topic so each conversation stays focused and easy to return to. The problem this feature solves. One ever-growing thread buries the topic you need under everything else. Finding where a particular discussion happened means scrolling through unrelated history. What it enables. Spawning a dedicated chat card per topic gives each its own card on the canvas, positioned wherever it belongs in the layout. The topics become spatially separate instead of chronologically tangled. The opportunity. Returning to a thread later is a matter of going to its place on the canvas, not scrolling a wall of text - the canvas itself becomes the index. ##### Use case 6: The one place every creation actually gets made

Situation. A small-business owner needs a flyer today, a slide deck tomorrow, and a batch of social posts next week, and doesn’t want to learn a different tool for each. What they want to do. Produce all of it from one familiar place. The problem this feature solves. Most stacks scatter each output across a separate app - a design tool, a deck tool, a social scheduler - each with its own learning curve and its own export-import dance. What it enables. A single chat card switches between Images, Social, Graphics, Sites, Slides, Mindmaps modes; the owner prompts in plain language and each Assistant builds the creation as a studio card right on the canvas. The chat is the one entry point to all six creation modes. The opportunity. The whole content operation runs from one conversation surface - learn it once, make anything. ##### Use case 7: Grounding the AI in your own research and references

Situation. An agency strategist has pulled a client’s website, a Google Business listing, and a written brief onto the canvas, and wants every creation and answer to reflect that specific context - not generic output. What they want to do. Make the AI reason from their material rather than starting cold on each prompt. The problem this feature solves. Re-pasting the brief, the research, and the source links into every prompt is tedious and error-prone, and the AI still drifts off-context between messages. What it enables. They connect the Research Leads card, the URL card, and the Text brief into the chat (4.2.2.8 Connected cards). From then on, every Send - whether a question or a creation - is grounded in that connected context automatically. The opportunity. The chat becomes a reasoning surface over the whole lab: ask it anything about the connected material, or create from it, and the answers stay on-brand and on-fact. ##### Use case 8: Driving the entire workflow from one conversation

Situation. A founder wants to go from a raw idea to a finished asset in a single sitting - research it, think it through, then produce the deliverable - without breaking flow. What they want to do. Reason and create in the same thread instead of switching between a “thinking” tool and a “making” tool. The problem this feature solves. Strategy and production usually live in different places, so the insight from one rarely carries cleanly into the other. What it enables. In one chat the founder talks through the idea (plain chat), connects any research they pull in, then switches the same panel to Slides or Graphics mode and creates the deliverable - the conversation’s context carrying straight into the creation. The opportunity. Thinking and making collapse into one continuous flow, with nothing lost in the handoff.

#### Prompt Templates Library

##### Public definition

The Templates pill in the chat composer opens the Prompt Templates library - a full-screen view of ready-made prompt starters. The page has a Search… field, a + New Template button (to save your own), and a grid of template cards. Each card shows an icon, a title, a short description, and a category tag (e.g. Sales & Marketing) - for example “Sentiment Analysis and Marketing Strategies - turn your latest customer reviews into clear, growth-ready insights…”.

##### Customer problem this solves

Business work is scattered across browser tabs, documents, AI chats, design tools, folders, and notes. Every switch forces the user to re-explain context, relocate source material, and reconstruct what happened before.

##### Why this feature is valuable

This feature is valuable because it reduces manual handoffs around customer jobs such as: reuse proven prompts, standardize prompts across a team, start common marketing tasks without writing prompts from scratch. It also matters as part of a larger system: the source, conversation, or finished output can remain connected to the broader IdeoLab project instead of being treated as an isolated one-off task.

##### Why this matters in the IdeoLab experience

The marketing advantage is not simply an infinite canvas or persistent project memory; other platforms now offer those concepts. The differentiation to emphasize is the combination of business bringing business source material into the project, multiple independent AI conversations, reusable connected project context, and built-in creation options for many finished marketing outputs in the same project surface. At the feature level, the most defensible advantage is how the capability contributes to a connected project rather than existing as a separate utility.

##### Detailed use-case library

###### Derived marketing scenario 1: Reuse proven prompts

Situation. A user needs to reuse proven prompts but the work is slowed by context switching, repeated setup, or manual handoffs.

What the feature enables. Prompt Templates Library gives the user a direct way to perform that job inside the IdeoLab workspace using the customer-facing capability described above.

The marketing opportunity. Show the feature in the context of a complete project, not as an isolated button. Connect the job to the broader workflow where the source or work can continue into the next marketing activity.

###### Derived marketing scenario 2: Standardize prompts across a team

Situation. A user needs to standardize prompts across a team but the work is slowed by context switching, repeated setup, or manual handoffs.

What the feature enables. Prompt Templates Library gives the user a direct way to perform that job inside the IdeoLab workspace using the customer-facing capability described above.

The marketing opportunity. Show the feature in the context of a complete project, not as an isolated button. Connect the job to the broader workflow where the source or work can continue into the next marketing activity.

###### Derived marketing scenario 3: Start common marketing tasks without writing prompts from scratch

Situation. A user needs to start common marketing tasks without writing prompts from scratch but the work is slowed by context switching, repeated setup, or manual handoffs.

What the feature enables. Prompt Templates Library gives the user a direct way to perform that job inside the IdeoLab workspace using the customer-facing capability described above.

The marketing opportunity. Show the feature in the context of a complete project, not as an isolated button. Connect the job to the broader workflow where the source or work can continue into the next marketing activity.

#### Persistent Connected Context

##### Public definition

You can wire any canvas card (an image studio card, a text card, a URL card, a research card, etc.) into a chat card by dragging from its connection handle to the chat. From that point on, the connected card feeds into the AI as context for every Send in that chat - without you having to @-mention or re-attach it each time.

##### Customer problem this solves

Business work is scattered across browser tabs, documents, AI chats, design tools, folders, and notes. Every switch forces the user to re-explain context, relocate source material, and reconstruct what happened before.

##### Why this feature is valuable

This feature is valuable because it reduces manual handoffs around customer jobs such as: keep a brief or research source connected to every message in a chat, ground repeated AI work in persistent project context, create multiple assets from the same connected source material. It also matters as part of a larger system: the source, conversation, or finished output can remain connected to the broader IdeoLab project instead of being treated as an isolated one-off task.

##### Why this matters in the IdeoLab experience

The marketing advantage is not simply an infinite canvas or persistent project memory; other platforms now offer those concepts. The differentiation to emphasize is the combination of business bringing business source material into the project, multiple independent AI conversations, reusable connected project context, and built-in creation options for many finished marketing outputs in the same project surface. At the feature level, the most defensible advantage is how the capability contributes to a connected project rather than existing as a separate utility.

## 5. Research & Business Intelligence

### System-level marketing definition

IdeoLab turns websites, local business listings, reviews, and lead sets into reusable intelligence that can stay attached to the work and immediately feed strategy, outreach, presentations, graphics, social content, and other creations.

### System-level customer problem

Research is usually collected in one tool and then manually summarized, copied, and re-explained in another tool before anything can be created from it.

### System-level differentiation

Specialized SEO and local-marketing platforms can go deeper on dedicated ranking, listing, and reputation workflows. IdeoLab’s strategic angle is research-to-creation continuity: the intelligence becomes working context for the same environment that creates the marketing response.

### Feature index

- Website Deep Research
- Google Business / Local Business Research
- Find Local Businesses & Lead Research

#### Website Deep Research

##### Public definition

Analyses one website end-to-end. You provide a Website URL and choose how many Pages to Research (10, 20, 30, 40, 50, 75, 100, or 125), then click Start Website Research.

##### Customer problem this solves

Research is usually collected in one tool and then manually summarized, copied, and re-explained in another tool before anything can be created from it.

##### Why this feature is valuable

This feature is valuable because it reduces manual handoffs around customer jobs such as: walk into a redesign pitch already knowing the site’s weak spots, prepare for a sales call by understanding what a company actually sells, find the conversion leaks on your own site before spending on traffic. It also matters as part of a larger system: the source, conversation, or finished output can remain connected to the broader IdeoLab project instead of being treated as an isolated one-off task.

##### Why this matters in the IdeoLab experience

Specialized SEO and local-marketing platforms can go deeper on dedicated ranking, listing, and reputation workflows. IdeoLab’s strategic angle is research-to-creation continuity: the intelligence becomes working context for the same environment that creates the marketing response. Do not claim IdeoLab replaces specialist SEO suites for every technical use case; emphasize how quickly intelligence becomes usable context for marketing and sales work.

##### Detailed use-case library

###### Use case 1: Walking into a redesign pitch already knowing the site’s weak spots

Situation. A web designer or agency is pitching a website rebuild for a prospect and needs to show, specifically, what’s wrong with the current site rather than offering vague “it looks dated” opinions. What they want to bring in. The prospect’s live website - its pages, messaging, and conversion flows - crawled and structured so it can be analysed before the meeting. The problem this feature solves. Auditing a site by hand means clicking through every page, noting the navigation, judging the copy, and hunting for missing CTAs - slow, subjective work that produces nothing reusable. What it enables. One URL plus a page count crawls the site into a card, and the Conversion Map → Conversion & CTA Opportunity Map and Messaging Audit hacks surface concrete gaps - weak headlines, missing calls-to-action, broken conversion paths. The opportunity. The designer pitches with a specific punch-list the prospect can see is true, shifting the conversation from “should we redesign” to “here’s what we’ll fix first.” ##### Use case 2: Prepping for a sales call by understanding what a company actually sells

Situation. A salesperson has a call with a company whose offering is spread across a sprawling website, and needs to understand their products, services, and positioning fast. What they want to bring in. The company’s site, crawled deep enough to capture the full product/service range and how they talk about it. The problem this feature solves. Skimming a site before a call gives a shallow read; the real structure - what they lead with, what they bury, who they target - takes far longer to map by hand. What it enables. A deep crawl plus the Site Structure and Messaging Audit hacks lay out the offering and the messaging so the rep walks in fluent in the prospect’s own language. The opportunity. The call opens with relevance instead of discovery questions, and the rep can tailor the pitch to gaps the prospect’s own site reveals. ##### Use case 3: Finding the conversion leaks on your own site before spending on traffic

Situation. A business owner is about to drive paid traffic to their site and wants to be sure the pages those clicks land on actually convert. What they want to bring in. Their own website, crawled and analysed the way a critical visitor - or a CRO specialist - would. The problem this feature solves. Owners see their own site every day and stop noticing the missing CTA, the buried pricing, the confusing path to purchase. Paid traffic to a leaky site quietly wastes budget. What it enables. The Conversion & CTA Opportunity Map maps the booking and form flows and flags friction points; the SEO & Keyword Optimization Scan catches discoverability gaps. The opportunity. The owner fixes the leaks before the ad spend starts, so every click lands on a page built to convert.

##### Marketing angle library

- Walking into a redesign pitch already knowing the site’s weak spots. Lead with: Auditing a site by hand means clicking through every page, noting the navigation, judging the copy, and hunting for missing CTAs - slow, subjective work that produces nothing reusable. Payoff: The designer pitches with a specific punch-list the prospect can see is true, shifting the conversation from “should we redesign” to “here’s what we’ll fix first.”
- Prepping for a sales call by understanding what a company actually sells. Lead with: Skimming a site before a call gives a shallow read; the real structure - what they lead with, what they bury, who they target - takes far longer to map by hand. Payoff: The call opens with relevance instead of discovery questions, and the rep can tailor the pitch to gaps the prospect’s own site reveals.
- Finding the conversion leaks on your own site before spending on traffic. Lead with: Owners see their own site every day and stop noticing the missing CTA, the buried pricing, the confusing path to purchase. Payoff: The owner fixes the leaks before the ad spend starts, so every click lands on a page built to convert.
- Research that does not die in a report. The point is what the business can create and decide from the research next.
- Evidence before opinion. Use real website, listing, review, or lead data to sharpen strategy and outreach.
- Research-to-creation continuity. Show the same intelligence feeding slides, graphics, social, sites, or outreach.
