#### Google Business / Local Business Research

##### Public definition

Pulls a live Google Business (Google Business Profile / Maps) listing into the lab. Add a listing via either tab - Search Listing (type a business name, pick from results) or Paste Link (paste a Google Business URL) - and optionally toggle which data to pull in: GBL Details · SEO Optimization · Last 20 Reviews · Website Analysis. Click Research Businesses (the modal’s button; the card on the Choose-type screen reads Add Business Listing) and the listing appears as a canvas card.

##### Customer problem this solves

Research is usually collected in one tool and then manually summarized, copied, and re-explained in another tool before anything can be created from it.

##### Why this feature is valuable

This feature is valuable because it reduces manual handoffs around customer jobs such as: win a local client by knowing their listing better than they do, turn a competitor’s reviews into your ad copy, find the service you should be selling but aren’t. It also matters as part of a larger system: the source, conversation, or finished output can remain connected to the broader IdeoLab project instead of being treated as an isolated one-off task.

##### Why this matters in the IdeoLab experience

Specialized SEO and local-marketing platforms can go deeper on dedicated ranking, listing, and reputation workflows. IdeoLab’s strategic angle is research-to-creation continuity: the intelligence becomes working context for the same environment that creates the marketing response. Do not claim IdeoLab replaces specialist SEO suites for every technical use case; emphasize how quickly intelligence becomes usable context for marketing and sales work.

##### Detailed use-case library

###### Use case 1: Winning a local client by knowing their listing better than they do

Situation. A marketing freelancer or agency owner is preparing a pitch for a local business - a dentist, a restaurant, an HVAC company - and needs to walk in already knowing exactly what’s wrong with how that business shows up on Google. What they want to bring in. The prospect’s real Google Business listing - its category, services, SEO signals, and recent reviews - pulled into the lab as a card they can analyse before the meeting. The problem this feature solves. Doing this by hand means opening Google Maps, reading the listing, scrolling reviews, cross-checking categories against competitors, and taking notes in a separate doc - an hour of tab-juggling that produces nothing reusable. Most freelancers skip it and walk in with a generic pitch that sounds like everyone else’s. What it enables. One search adds the listing as a canvas card, and the Listing Optimization → Category & Service Relevance Audit Prompt Hack runs a structured audit against it inside a chat. The output is a ready-made list of gaps the freelancer can quote directly in the pitch. The opportunity. The freelancer arrives with specifics - wrong primary category, missing services, weak SEO fields - instead of platitudes. The prospect feels understood before any money changes hands, and the conversation shifts from “what do you do” to “when can you fix this.” ##### Use case 2: Turning a competitor’s reviews into your ad copy

Situation. A small-business owner or copywriter is writing ads and sales pages and is staring at a blank page, trying to guess what actually makes customers in this market buy. What they want to bring in. A real listing’s last 20 reviews - their own or a thriving competitor’s - as raw, honest language straight from paying customers. The problem this feature solves. Reading dozens of reviews by hand to find the recurring praise, the emotional triggers, and the exact phrases customers use is slow, and the patterns blur together. Most people write ads from imagination instead of evidence, and the copy falls flat. What it enables. With the listing card on the canvas and reviews toggled in, the Review Insights → Review Sales Angle Intelligence Prompt Hack mines the reviews for the selling angles customers themselves keep raising - ready to drop into ad copy, headlines, or a sales script. The opportunity. The ads speak in the customer’s own words about the things they already care about, so they convert better. The owner stops guessing and starts writing from what the market has literally told them matters. ##### Use case 3: Finding the service you should be selling but aren’t

Situation. A service-business owner - a salon, a clinic, a repair shop - suspects there’s demand they’re not capturing but has no structured way to find it. What they want to bring in. The listing’s reviews, where customers casually mention things they wished were offered, asked about, or had to go elsewhere for. The problem this feature solves. These signals are buried across dozens of individual reviews, one offhand comment at a time. Skimming them by hand, nobody connects “three people mentioned this” into “this is a service worth launching.” The insight is sitting in plain sight and goes unread. What it enables. The Review Insights → Review-Driven Service Insights Prompt Hack reads the reviews on the listing card and surfaces the unmet needs and requested services customers keep raising - a concrete list, not a hunch. The opportunity. The owner discovers a new offering the market is already asking for, with built-in proof of demand. Decisions about what to add next stop being guesswork and start being evidence-backed. ##### Use case 4: Sizing up the competition before entering a market

Situation. A franchise scout, a relocating business owner, or a consultant is assessing how hard it’ll be to rank and win in a local market dominated by a few entrenched players. What they want to bring in. A leading competitor’s Google Business listing - its authority signals, SEO fields, and review volume - as a benchmark card in the lab. The problem this feature solves. Judging a competitor’s strength by eyeballing their Maps profile is impressionistic, and comparing several by hand is tedious and inconsistent. The decision to enter a market often gets made on gut feel instead of a real read of who you’re up against. What it enables. The Competitive Analysis → Map Pack Authority Signal Analysis Prompt Hack runs against the competitor card and breaks down the authority signals that are keeping them in the local map pack - the specific levers a newcomer would have to match or beat. The opportunity. The scout walks into the market knowing exactly how strong the incumbents are and where their soft spots are, turning a risky bet into an informed one. ##### Use case 5: Briefing the whole team off one shared research card

Situation. An agency account lead has just onboarded a local client and needs the strategist, the copywriter, and the ads buyer all working from the same accurate picture of where the client stands today. What they want to bring in. The client’s full listing - GBL details, SEO optimization data, last 20 reviews, and website analysis all toggled in - as a single canvas card the team can analyse together. The problem this feature solves. When everyone does their own research, they arrive with different, partial, and sometimes contradictory pictures of the client. Reconciling that wastes a kickoff meeting, and important details slip through the gap between roles. What it enables. One person adds the listing with all four data toggles on, and every Prompt Hack - listing audit, review angles, service gaps, competitive read - runs against that same card, with each output feeding the relevant teammate’s chat or workflow. The opportunity. The whole team starts aligned on a single source of truth about the client. The strategist’s plan, the copywriter’s angles, and the ads buyer’s targeting all trace back to the same research instead of three separate guesses. ##### Use case 6: Auditing your own listing before you spend on ads

Situation. A local-business owner is about to pour budget into Google Ads or a marketing push and wants to make sure the listing those clicks land on is actually in good shape. What they want to bring in. Their own Google Business listing, pulled into the lab so they can scrutinise it the way a prospective customer - or a marketing pro - would. The problem this feature solves. Most owners never audit their own listing critically; they see it every day and stop noticing the wrong category, the thin service list, the unanswered reviews. Spending on ads to send traffic to a weak profile quietly wastes the budget. What it enables. The listing becomes a card, and the Category & Service Relevance Audit plus the review-based Prompt Hacks expose exactly what a new visitor would notice - gaps to fix before the ad money starts flowing. The opportunity. The owner fixes the foundation first, so every ad dollar lands on a listing that converts instead of leaking. The same research doubles as a punch-list for ongoing improvement.

##### Marketing angle library

- Winning a local client by knowing their listing better than they do. Lead with: Doing this by hand means opening Google Maps, reading the listing, scrolling reviews, cross-checking categories against competitors, and taking notes in a separate doc - an hour of tab-juggling that produces nothing reusable. Payoff: The freelancer arrives with specifics - wrong primary category, missing services, weak SEO fields - instead of platitudes.
- Turning a competitor’s reviews into your ad copy. - Sizing up the competition before entering a market. - Briefing the whole team off one shared research card. Lead with: When everyone does their own research, they arrive with different, partial, and sometimes contradictory pictures of the client. Payoff: The whole team starts aligned on a single source of truth about the client.
- Auditing your own listing before you spend on ads. Lead with: Most owners never audit their own listing critically; they see it every day and stop noticing the wrong category, the thin service list, the unanswered reviews. Payoff: The owner fixes the foundation first, so every ad dollar lands on a listing that converts instead of leaking.

#### Find Local Businesses & Lead Research

##### Public definition

Finds local lead opportunities by Industry / Keyword, City, and State / Region, with a Number Of Leads selector (e.g. 25). Clicking Find Local Businesses opens a fullscreen lead workspace where you review, filter, and download the leads - or add selected leads to the canvas as source cards for prospecting, lead qualification, and outreach planning.

##### Customer problem this solves

Research is usually collected in one tool and then manually summarized, copied, and re-explained in another tool before anything can be created from it.

##### Why this feature is valuable

This feature is valuable because it reduces manual handoffs around customer jobs such as: build a local prospecting list from scratch, decide which local leads to chase first, find underserved local markets to enter. It also matters as part of a larger system: the source, conversation, or finished output can remain connected to the broader IdeoLab project instead of being treated as an isolated one-off task.

##### Why this matters in the IdeoLab experience

Specialized SEO and local-marketing platforms can go deeper on dedicated ranking, listing, and reputation workflows. IdeoLab’s strategic angle is research-to-creation continuity: the intelligence becomes working context for the same environment that creates the marketing response. Do not claim IdeoLab replaces specialist SEO suites for every technical use case; emphasize how quickly intelligence becomes usable context for marketing and sales work.

##### Detailed use-case library

###### Use case 1: Building a local prospecting list from scratch

Situation. A sales rep or agency is opening a new market and has no list - just a service to sell and a region to sell it in. What they want to bring in. A set of real local businesses matching an industry and location, as leads they can work. The problem this feature solves. Building a prospect list by hand means searching Maps, copying names and details one by one into a spreadsheet - hours of tedious data entry before any selling starts. What it enables. A keyword, city, region, and lead count produce a fullscreen lead workspace; the rep reviews and filters the results and adds the selected leads to the canvas, then runs the Lead Discovery → Local Lead Opportunity Scan hack to prioritise them. The opportunity. The rep starts the week with a ranked, ready-to-work list instead of a blank spreadsheet, and spends their time selling rather than compiling. ##### Use case 2: Deciding which local leads to chase first

Situation. A small sales team has more leads than time and needs to focus on the ones most likely to convert. What they want to bring in. Their pulled local leads, scored by how ready each is to buy the services on offer. The problem this feature solves. Without a system, reps work leads in arbitrary order and burn time on businesses that were never going to buy, while hot prospects go cold. What it enables. The Lead Qualification → Lead Readiness Qualification and Service Fit Mapping hacks estimate each lead’s readiness (SEO, reputation, website, analytics) and map each to the most relevant service - turning a flat list into a prioritised pipeline. The opportunity. The team works the best-fit, highest-readiness leads first, lifting conversion rates without adding headcount. ##### Use case 3: Finding underserved local markets to enter

Situation. A multi-location operator or franchisor is looking for a town or niche where demand outstrips the quality of the local competition. What they want to bring in. A region’s local businesses in a category, analysed for where the existing players are weak. The problem this feature solves. Spotting a coverage gap by hand means profiling every competitor in a market and comparing them - impractical at any real scale. What it enables. The Lead Discovery → Market Coverage Gap Finder and Local SEO Gaps → Local SEO Gap Audit / Website Technical Gap Audit hacks surface markets where businesses have weak digital presence relative to demand - and the Outreach Angles → Outreach Angle Builder turns the finding into a way in. The opportunity. The operator enters where the competition is weakest, with an outreach angle already in hand.

##### Marketing angle library

- Building a local prospecting list from scratch. Lead with: Building a prospect list by hand means searching Maps, copying names and details one by one into a spreadsheet - hours of tedious data entry before any selling starts. Payoff: The rep starts the week with a ranked, ready-to-work list instead of a blank spreadsheet, and spends their time selling rather than compiling.
- Deciding which local leads to chase first. Lead with: Without a system, reps work leads in arbitrary order and burn time on businesses that were never going to buy, while hot prospects go cold. Payoff: The team works the best-fit, highest-readiness leads first, lifting conversion rates without adding headcount.
- Finding underserved local markets to enter. Lead with: Spotting a coverage gap by hand means profiling every competitor in a market and comparing them - impractical at any real scale. Payoff: The operator enters where the competition is weakest, with an outreach angle already in hand.
- Research that does not die in a report. The point is what the business can create and decide from the research next.

## 6. AI Image Creation

### System-level marketing definition

IdeoLab’s image system covers original generation, targeted editing, multi-image blending, coordinated photo-shoot workflows, and post-generation refinement inside Image Studio.

### System-level customer problem

Businesses need more visual content than they can realistically photograph, commission, retouch, and repurpose by hand.

### System-level differentiation

General image tools can generate and edit excellent images. IdeoLab’s stronger marketing story is not simply image quality; it is that visual creation sits inside a broader business-content system where research, references, source images, social workflows, graphics, sites, and automation can contribute to the same project.

### Feature index

- Text to Image
- Edit Image
- Blend Images
- AI Photo Shoot
- Image Studio

#### Text to Image

##### Public definition

Text to Image creates a brand-new image from a written prompt - no source file needed. Describe what you want to see, choose creation options such as aspect ratio, and IdeoLab produces it in roughly 15-30 seconds. Every Send produces a new Image Studio holding one image, or several if you request multiple variations.

##### Customer problem this solves

Businesses need more visual content than they can realistically photograph, commission, retouch, and repurpose by hand.

##### Why this feature is valuable

This feature is valuable because it reduces manual handoffs around customer jobs such as: a product that needs a hero before the photoshoot budget is approved, social content that stops the scroll, a website that needs atmosphere, not just information. It also matters as part of a larger system: the source, conversation, or finished output can remain connected to the broader IdeoLab project instead of being treated as an isolated one-off task.

##### Why this matters in the IdeoLab experience

General image tools can generate and edit excellent images. IdeoLab’s stronger marketing story is not simply image quality; it is that visual creation sits inside a broader business-content system where research, references, source images, social workflows, graphics, sites, and automation can contribute to the same project. At the feature level, emphasize the workflow around the asset - source context, review, refinement, reuse, publishing, or analytics - not unsupported claims that the raw AI output is universally better.

##### Detailed use-case library

###### Use case 1: A product that needs a hero before the photoshoot budget is approved

Situation. A founder or marketing lead is building a landing page for a new product - an item not yet photographed, a concept still in prototyping, or an upcoming launch where waiting for a real shoot would mean missing the launch window. What they want to bring to life. A single polished product image that conveys quality, context, and brand mood - something that makes the product feel real and desirable on a page that doesn’t yet have real photography to draw from. The problem this feature solves. Real product photography costs money and takes lead time. Waiting for it delays the page, delays the campaign, delays the ability to test. A placeholder from stock looks generic and doesn’t represent the actual product at all. What it creates. A crisp, photorealistic product shot - the item positioned in a styled environment (marble counter, minimal studio, natural setting) with lighting and depth of field that match the brand’s tone, using a clean standard ratio suited to above-the-fold hero placement. The opportunity. The page goes live on schedule. Visitors respond to something that looks real and intentional. The team gets data on messaging and layout while the final photography is still in production. The image can be swapped the moment the real shoot is done - with no lost launch momentum. This produces a usable stand-in in under a minute.

###### Use case 2: Social content that stops the scroll

Situation. A content creator, social media manager, or solopreneur publishes daily or near-daily across Instagram and LinkedIn. They have a message they want to send - a quote, a tip, a strong opinion - but they don’t have a visual that matches the mood and they can’t use the same stock photo again. What they want to bring to life. A striking, mood-matched visual that feels native to the platform and gives the text post something to anchor to - not a generic backdrop, but an image that makes people stop and pay attention to the words. The problem this feature solves. Stock photo libraries feel recycled and impersonal. Custom photography requires planning. The result is that content either goes out without a strong visual, or a familiar stock image gets reused until followers tune it out entirely. What it creates. A custom social image - a square or portrait format composition built around the specific mood and subject of the post, using a visual style that fits the creator’s voice (bold and graphic, or warm and human, or clean and minimal). The opportunity. The post goes out with a visual that matches the caption instead of undercutting it. Engagement - saves, shares, comments - tends to correlate with visual quality and originality. Over weeks, a consistent visual style builds a feed that looks like a brand, not a content stream.

###### Use case 3: A website that needs atmosphere, not just information

Situation. A local business owner - a restaurant, a spa, a boutique fitness studio - is rebuilding their website or landing page. The current images are either stock photographs that look like everyone else’s site, or old phone photos that undersell the space. What they want to bring to life. The feeling of the place - its warmth, its energy, its character - in a format that works across the hero section, about page, and any promotional banners they run. The problem this feature solves. Real photography of the space requires scheduling, a photographer, post-processing, and money. In the meantime the website stays on hold or uses whatever exists, which undercuts the premium positioning the business is trying to build. What it creates. A mood-matched atmospheric image of the kind of environment the business wants to convey - a candlelit table scene, a sun-drenched treatment room, a post-workout energy shot - in landscape format optimised for hero placement, with a visual style that communicates the brand without describing a real space. The opportunity. The website goes up or gets updated without waiting on a shoot. Visitors arrive with the right emotional expectation, which makes the real experience feel consistent with what the page promised. The image is good enough to use across ads and social as well. This produces a usable scene-setter in under a minute for a fraction of the cost.

###### Use case 4: Training and internal materials that don’t look like slide clip-art

Situation. A learning and development manager, team lead, or corporate trainer is putting together an employee training module, onboarding deck, or internal handbook. Every slide needs a visual, and the options are either generic clip-art or the same rotating stock images the company has used for years. What they want to bring to life. Illustrations or scene-setting images that match the specific topic - customer service scenarios, workplace safety situations, a product walkthrough moment - in a consistent style that makes the materials feel professionally produced. The problem this feature solves. Generic visuals make training feel low-effort and low-priority. When the image doesn’t match the message, learners tune out faster. The alternative - commissioning custom illustrations - requires a designer, a brief, revision cycles, and budget. What it creates. Scenario-specific custom illustrations - a team at a whiteboard, a customer interaction moment, a safety procedure in action - in a flat vector or clean photorealistic style, sized to fit slide presentations in standard 16:9 landscape format. The opportunity. Training materials feel intentional and polished instead of assembled from a shared drive of reused images. Learners engage longer. The team producing the materials can move faster because they’re not blocked waiting on a design resource to come free. This produces the same visual coherence without the cost or lead time.

###### Use case 5: A consulting proposal that looks like it came from a larger firm

Situation. An independent consultant, boutique agency, or freelance strategist is putting together a proposal or client presentation. The work is strong, but the document looks like any other Word doc - no visuals that convey scale, intention, or brand. What they want to bring to life. A professional visual - a hero image for the cover page, a section divider that sets the tone, an abstract image that represents transformation or strategic thinking - that makes the proposal feel like it came from a firm twice the size. The problem this feature solves. Consultants tend to win or lose on first impression. A proposal without strong visual design reads as less premium than a competitor’s even when the content is better. Finding the right stock image is time-consuming, and it often ends up looking generic. What it creates. A custom abstract or conceptual image - a team ascending, a system in motion, a convergence of elements - in portrait or standard ratio, styled to feel authoritative and premium without being flashy. The opportunity. The proposal lands differently. Decision-makers notice the presentation before they read a word, and that impression colours how they read the content. A proposal that looks like the output of a serious professional firm is more likely to be treated as one. Adding custom photography or illustration to that process adds cost and time. This creates the key visual in under two minutes.

###### Use case 6: An event that needs a visual identity before the details are final

Situation. A community organiser, marketing coordinator, or business owner is running an event - a launch, a workshop, a fundraiser - and needs to start promoting it before all the logistical details are nailed down. They need a banner image to get posts and emails going. What they want to bring to life. An event-appropriate visual - energetic, welcoming, or premium depending on the tone - that can carry the promotion across social announcements, email headers, and a simple landing page. The problem this feature solves. Waiting until the event details are confirmed to start promotion means compressed promotion windows. Stock event photography looks recycled. Hiring a designer to produce a custom event visual before the logistics are settled feels premature. What it creates. A styled event atmosphere image - a crowd of people with energy, a beautifully lit empty venue, an abstract representation of the theme - in landscape format sized for email headers and social sharing, with a visual mood that matches the event’s positioning. The opportunity. Promotion starts earlier. The visual builds anticipation and signals that this is an event worth attending. When the practical details are finalised, the same image family holds the campaign together across all touchpoints. This produces a usable campaign anchor image in under a minute.

###### Use case 7: An ad creative tested without spending on a shoot

Situation. A performance marketer or growth-focused founder is running paid social ads and wants to test multiple creative concepts before committing to a full production shoot. They have three different angle hypotheses but no visuals to go with them yet. What they want to bring to life. Three distinct visual directions - different moods, different subjects, different compositions - so they can run them against each other and find out which angle resonates before investing in production. The problem this feature solves. Paid creative testing requires multiple visual variants. Producing those variants through traditional means - photography, design, retouching - is slow and expensive, which means most teams either test with low-quality visuals or skip the test phase and guess. What it creates. Three separate images - each a distinct creative direction, using a square or portrait ratio built for feed ads - with different compositions, subjects, and moods that correspond to each angle hypothesis. Created in minutes, not days. The opportunity. The team runs three ad sets simultaneously, each with a real creative concept behind it. Within 48 hours, they have data on which direction performs. The winner gets invested in properly - with a real shoot, if the results justify it. This produces three testable concepts in under five minutes.

###### Use case 8: A book, guide, or digital product that needs cover art

Situation. A coach, author, course creator, or content marketer has finished (or is finishing) a digital product - an ebook, a PDF guide, an online course - and needs a cover image that conveys the value and professionalism of the content inside. What they want to bring to life. A cover visual that matches the product’s topic and intended audience, communicates the transformation or value, and looks like it belongs on a shelf next to professionally published material. The problem this feature solves. Digital products sink or swim on presentation. A low-effort cover signals a low-effort product. Hiring a cover designer takes time, requires a creative brief, goes through revision cycles, and costs money that an early-stage creator may not have yet. What it creates. A purpose-built cover illustration or stylised photograph - the right subject, mood, and composition for the product’s topic - sized in portrait format for an ebook cover or course thumbnail, in a style that communicates the product’s positioning (professional, warm and approachable, bold and tactical). The opportunity. The product launches looking polished. Buyers judge the cover before they read the description, and a strong visual builds the confidence they need to purchase. The same image can be used as a social announcement, a lead magnet preview, and an email header. This produces the core visual in under a minute.

##### Marketing angle library

- A product that needs a hero before the photoshoot budget is approved. Lead with: Real product photography costs money and takes lead time. Payoff: The page goes live on schedule.
- Social content that stops the scroll. Lead with: Stock photo libraries feel recycled and impersonal. Payoff: The post goes out with a visual that matches the caption instead of undercutting it.
- A website that needs atmosphere, not just information. Lead with: Real photography of the space requires scheduling, a photographer, post-processing, and money. Payoff: The website goes up or gets updated without waiting on a shoot.
- Training and internal materials that don’t look like slide clip-art. Lead with: Generic visuals make training feel low-effort and low-priority. Payoff: Training materials feel intentional and polished instead of assembled from a shared drive of reused images.
- A consulting proposal that looks like it came from a larger firm. Lead with: Consultants tend to win or lose on first impression. Payoff: The proposal lands differently.
- An event that needs a visual identity before the details are final. Lead with: Waiting until the event details are confirmed to start promotion means compressed promotion windows. Payoff: Promotion starts earlier.
- An ad creative tested without spending on a shoot. Lead with: Paid creative testing requires multiple visual variants. Payoff: The team runs three ad sets simultaneously, each with a real creative concept behind it.
- A book, guide, or digital product that needs cover art. Lead with: Digital products sink or swim on presentation. Payoff: The product launches looking polished.
- More mileage from assets you already own. Use editing, blending, and reference-led workflows instead of always starting from scratch.

#### Edit Image

##### Public definition

Edit Image takes a source image you already have and transforms it based on a written instruction - removing elements, swapping backgrounds, changing the style, adjusting lighting, or adding new details - while keeping the rest of the image recognisable. Every Send produces a new Image Studio holding one edited result, or several if you request multiple variations.

##### Customer problem this solves

Businesses need more visual content than they can realistically photograph, commission, retouch, and repurpose by hand.

##### Why this feature is valuable

This feature is valuable because it reduces manual handoffs around customer jobs such as: remove a background from a product image, swap a background to match the season or campaign, brand-align an image that almost works. It also matters as part of a larger system: the source, conversation, or finished output can remain connected to the broader IdeoLab project instead of being treated as an isolated one-off task.

##### Why this matters in the IdeoLab experience

General image tools can generate and edit excellent images. IdeoLab’s stronger marketing story is not simply image quality; it is that visual creation sits inside a broader business-content system where research, references, source images, social workflows, graphics, sites, and automation can contribute to the same project. At the feature level, emphasize the workflow around the asset - source context, review, refinement, reuse, publishing, or analytics - not unsupported claims that the raw AI output is universally better.

##### Detailed use-case library

###### Use case 1: Removing a background from a product image

Situation. A product owner, ecommerce seller, or marketing team has good product photos but needs clean, background-free versions for their website’s product grid, digital ads, or marketplace listings - and the photos were shot against a non-white or textured background. What they want to bring to life. The product, clean and isolated, without the cluttered shelf, coloured wall, or hand holding it that appeared in the original shot. The problem this feature solves. Background removal tools work inconsistently on complex edges (hair, fur, transparent packaging) and still require manual cleanup. Sending product images to a retoucher takes days and costs money per image, which adds up fast across a catalogue. What it creates. The same product, isolated on a transparent-equivalent white or neutral background, edges clean enough for marketplace and ad use, ready for placement on any coloured background in the brand’s design system. The opportunity. The product page goes live with consistent, professional imagery across every SKU. The isolated image gets reused across ads, email, social, and print without needing a new retouching round. The catalogue can be updated quickly as inventory changes. This removes the background in under a minute per image.

###### Use case 2: Swapping a background to match the season or campaign

Situation. A retailer, restaurateur, or hospitality business has strong hero images from a shoot that happened in one season, but needs the same content to feel right in a different season - a summer beach shoot repurposed for a winter promotion, or a studio shot that needs an outdoor lifestyle context. What they want to bring to life. The same subject or product, placed in a new environment that fits the current campaign - a snowy outdoor setting, a festive interior, a spring marketplace scene - with lighting that reads as natural. The problem this feature solves. Booking a new shoot for each season or campaign is expensive and logistically complicated. Keeping the same image in every campaign makes the brand look static. The gap between the shoot and the need means imagery is always slightly behind. What it creates. The original subject re-placed in a new background that matches the campaign’s seasonal or thematic context - lighting and shadows updated to match the new setting, overall image feeling like it was shot there rather than composited. The opportunity. The same core imagery asset serves multiple campaigns across the year. Campaign imagery stays fresh and context-appropriate without the budget for multiple shoots. The brand looks responsive and current rather than relying on a single evergreen asset. This achieves the same output in minutes.

###### Use case 3: Brand-aligning an image that almost works

Situation. A marketing manager, brand director, or agency account team has sourced an image - from stock, from a client, or from a past shoot - that’s close to what they need but doesn’t quite match the brand guidelines. The colours are off, the mood is too bright or too dark, or there’s a stylistic element that conflicts. What they want to bring to life. The same composition and subject, but with brand colours reflected in the palette, a tonal grade that fits the brand’s emotional register, and any conflicting elements adjusted or removed. The problem this feature solves. Stock images are by definition not brand-specific. Getting a designer to rework the colour grading and styling of a photo takes time and requires creative direction back-and-forth. The result is often a compromise rather than a strong brand-matched asset. What it creates. The original image with a new tonal treatment - colours pulled toward the brand palette, mood adjusted to fit the brand’s voice, any elements that conflict with the visual identity softened or removed - in the original format and ratio. The opportunity. The image passes brand review without another round of design work. The campaign goes out with consistent visual identity across all assets. The team builds a library of brand-matched imagery faster than they could through traditional production. This does the same work in minutes.

###### Use case 4: Cleaning up a photo that almost made the cut

Situation. A photographer, content creator, or small business owner has a photo that’s almost perfect - good light, good composition, good subject - but there’s a distracting element in it: a bin in the background, a cable on the floor, someone walking through the frame, a logo that shouldn’t be visible. What they want to bring to life. The same image with the distraction removed, leaving the rest of the image looking natural and untouched. The problem this feature solves. Photoshop retouching requires either skill or a specialist. Sending it to someone else takes time. The photo ends up sitting unused or the imperfect version goes out because it’s the best available option. What it creates. The same photo with the distracting element cleanly removed, the background filled in naturally, and the rest of the image looking as if nothing was ever there - using the same tone, texture, and light as the surrounding area. The opportunity. The photo moves from “almost” to “ready to use.” The business or creator gets more usable assets from each shoot without needing to reshoot or rely on expensive post-production services. This does it in under a minute.

###### Use case 5: Adding a new element to an existing scene

Situation. A marketing or creative team has a clean background image - a location, an interior, a lifestyle scene - and wants to place a new object, person, or design element into it without shooting the composite from scratch. What they want to bring to life. The same scene, now with an additional element that makes sense in the context - a logo on a wall, a product on a table, a person in a doorway, a seasonal decoration in a storefront - rendered with matching light and perspective. The problem this feature solves. Compositing is a specialist skill that requires hours of work even for an experienced designer. The creative teams that most need this capability - small marketing teams, ecommerce operators, solopreneurs - are the least likely to have that skill in-house. What it creates. The original scene with the new element placed naturally - consistent lighting direction, correct perspective and scale, no visible edge where the addition was made - looking like it was shot that way. The opportunity. The team creates scene variations without reshooting. A product gets placed in multiple lifestyle contexts without a separate shoot for each. A campaign image gets updated for a different market by swapping the element, not the whole photo. This produces the composite in under a minute.

###### Use case 6: Restyling an image to match a new visual direction

Situation. A brand, content studio, or creative team is evolving their visual identity - moving from a dark moody aesthetic to clean minimalist, or from photorealistic to illustrated - and has a library of existing images that don’t match the new direction. What they want to bring to life. The same subjects and compositions from the existing library, but with the new visual style applied across them - consistent look and feel without a full reshoot of the archive. The problem this feature solves. Visual rebrands are expensive because they seem to require reshooting everything. Most brands end up with a patchwork of old and new imagery living side by side, which makes the rebrand look half-finished for months. What it creates. The same image with a new rendering - flat vector style applied to a photograph, illustration aesthetic layered over a product shot, dark cinematic grade applied to a bright lifestyle image - maintaining the composition and subject while transforming the visual treatment. The opportunity. The library shifts toward the new visual direction without a full production investment. Key hero images get updated first. The brand looks consistent sooner, and the rebrand can be rolled out more gradually without a visible discontinuity. This handles each image in under a minute.

###### Use case 7: Updating a hero image for a new market or region

Situation. A business expanding into a new market - a different country, demographic, or cultural context - has brand imagery that was produced for their primary market and doesn’t translate. The people, the settings, or the visual references feel specific to one context. What they want to bring to life. Localised versions of existing hero and campaign images - same products, same compositions, but settings and contexts that feel culturally appropriate and representative for the target market. The problem this feature solves. Shooting localised imagery for each new market requires a separate production in each location, a local creative team, and significant budget. Most brands end up using the same imagery globally by default, which creates a disconnect with local audiences. What it creates. The existing hero image with the background, context, or secondary elements updated to reflect the new market’s cultural and visual context - same product, different world around it, with consistent lighting and perspective. The opportunity. The brand enters the new market with imagery that speaks directly to that audience. Campaign performance improves because the creative resonates rather than feeling imported. The localisation cost is a fraction of a full local shoot. This localises existing assets in minutes per image.

###### Use case 8: Enhancing a real photo taken on a phone

Situation. A local business owner, restaurateur, or small retailer took their own product or space photos on a smartphone. The photos are good enough to use but look like phone photos - slightly flat, slightly noisy, slightly off in the lighting - compared to professional imagery. What they want to bring to life. The same composition and subject, but with the quality lifted to feel more professional: sharper, better-lit, with colours that match how things actually look in good light. The problem this feature solves. Professional editing of phone photos by a specialist still takes time and money, even though the skill gap between “phone photo” and “usable marketing asset” is narrower than ever. Most business owners either use the photo as-is or never use it at all. What it creates. The same image with quality enhanced - sharpened details, improved lighting balance, reduced noise, natural colour correction - in a way that makes it usable for websites, social, and print without looking artificially over-processed. The opportunity. The business’s online presence looks more polished without the cost and overhead of a professional shoot. Every decent phone photo becomes a potential marketing asset. The visual standard of the whole brand improves over time as each improved image replaces a weaker one. This does the same enhancement in under a minute.

#### Blend Images

##### Public definition

Blend Images combines two or more of your own images into a new visual. You can describe which subjects, backgrounds, styles, or visual elements should carry forward, then review and refine the resulting image in Image Studio.

##### Customer problem this solves

Businesses need more visual content than they can realistically photograph, commission, retouch, and repurpose by hand.

##### Why this feature is valuable

This feature is valuable because it reduces manual handoffs around customer jobs such as: apply one brand’s style to another image, place a product in a lifestyle context, create a consistent visual across two team headshots. It also matters as part of a larger system: the source, conversation, or finished output can remain connected to the broader IdeoLab project instead of being treated as an isolated one-off task.

##### Why this matters in the IdeoLab experience

General image tools can generate and edit excellent images. IdeoLab’s stronger marketing story is not simply image quality; it is that visual creation sits inside a broader business-content system where research, references, source images, social workflows, graphics, sites, and automation can contribute to the same project. At the feature level, emphasize the workflow around the asset - source context, review, refinement, reuse, publishing, or analytics - not unsupported claims that the raw AI output is universally better.

##### Detailed use-case library

###### Use case 1: Applying one brand’s style to another image

Situation. A creative director, brand manager, or designer has two images: one that has the right subject or composition and one that has the right visual style. They want the final result to combine both - the subject from one, the style from the other - so the output looks like it was shot or created in that style from the start. What they want to bring to life. A new image where the subject from the first image is rendered in the visual style, colour palette, and aesthetic of the second - as if the subject had always existed in that world. The problem this feature solves. Style transfer requires either specialist software, a design team with retouching skills, or an AI tool that accepts two separate inputs. Manually combining a subject, style, background, and visual treatment can require significant design work. Describing the intended blend in plain language creates a faster starting point for exploration. What it creates. A new image combining the subject from source one and the visual treatment from source two - rendered in the target style with consistent colour, texture, and mood applied to the entire composition. Produced in the ratio best suited to the intended placement. The opportunity. The creative team can explore style directions quickly by testing multiple reference style images against the same subject. What normally requires a specialist retoucher and multiple rounds of feedback gets explored in minutes, with each variant ready to show the client immediately. This produces the same exploration in under five minutes.

###### Use case 2: Placing a product in a lifestyle context

Situation. An ecommerce owner or brand marketer has a clean studio product shot - product on white - and a lifestyle or environmental image that represents where the product is used. They want to see the product in that environment for use in ads, the homepage, and social content. What they want to bring to life. The product from the studio shot, placed naturally in the lifestyle scene from the second image - same product, in context, looking like it belongs there with matching perspective, scale, and lighting. The problem this feature solves. Lifestyle product photography requires either shooting the product on location or expensive compositing by a retoucher. Studio-only imagery performs poorly in feed ads because it lacks the context that makes products feel desirable and real in people’s lives. What it creates. A composite where the product from image one is placed in the setting from image two - lighting adjusted to match the scene, perspective and scale consistent, the overall image reading as natural rather than digitally assembled. The opportunity. The product gets placed in multiple lifestyle contexts - kitchen, office, outdoor, social setting - without shooting in each one. Each variation becomes a different ad creative or homepage hero. The same core product shot generates an entire campaign’s worth of contextual imagery. This produces each variation in under a minute.

###### Use case 3: Creating a consistent visual across two team headshots

Situation. A business, agency, or professional services firm has headshots from two different photographers or two different sessions - different lighting, different backgrounds, different tones - and needs them to look like they belong to the same team on the website. What they want to bring to life. A unified look across both headshots - consistent background treatment, consistent tonal grade, consistent lighting feel - so the About page or team section reads as one visual family rather than a mismatched archive. The problem this feature solves. Headshot editing to create visual consistency requires a retoucher to colour-match across multiple images, which is time-consuming and has to be redone every time a new team member is added or a photo is updated. What it creates. A blend that takes the background or lighting treatment from image one and applies it to produce a consistent visual output where both subjects look as if they were shot in the same session, same environment, same lighting rig. The opportunity. The website’s team page looks professional and cohesive from day one rather than growing in an ad-hoc way as photos accumulate. Future headshots can be matched to the same style without a fresh round of retouching on the existing ones. This handles the consistency in minutes.

###### Use case 4: Exploring a product design concept before production

Situation. A product designer, entrepreneur, or creative team has two concepts for a product or packaging design - two different colour directions, two different material finishes, two different structural forms - and wants to see what a hybrid of the two might look like before committing either to production. What they want to bring to life. A conceptual rendering that merges the strongest elements of both designs - the colour system from one, the form or material from the other - into a unified visual concept that doesn’t yet exist physically. The problem this feature solves. Design exploration before production requires either a 3D modeller, a product visualiser, or a hand-crafted composite by a designer. Each round of exploration costs time and money, which means teams tend to commit to a direction earlier than they should. What it creates. A new product image that blends the visual elements from both source concepts - colours, forms, textures, materials - into a coherent single rendering, in the ratio needed for stakeholder presentations. The opportunity. The team explores hybrid directions quickly and cheaply before committing to tooling, materials, or production costs. Concepts that wouldn’t have been explored due to time constraints get explored. Better decisions get made earlier. This produces each exploration in minutes.

###### Use case 5: Building a campaign image from multiple reference images

Situation. A marketing team or creative director has a campaign concept that pulls from multiple visual references - a specific lighting reference from one mood-board image, a colour palette from a brand shoot, a compositional reference from an industry photo - and wants to create a campaign image that synthesises all three. What they want to bring to life. A single campaign image that captures the lighting, colour, and composition ideas from multiple references in one coherent output - not copying any of them but combining their strongest elements into something new. The problem this feature solves. Mood board references are typically used as a creative brief for a photographer or illustrator, which requires interpretation, a production session, and multiple rounds of feedback before the output looks like what the brief described. The gap between reference and result is often frustrating and expensive. What it creates. A campaign image blended from all three references - the lighting from one, the palette from another, the compositional structure from the third - rendered as a single coherent image in the format needed for the campaign. The opportunity. The creative team gets from mood board to usable campaign asset in minutes rather than weeks. The result can be used as a direction-setting reference for a real production, as a client presentation image, or as the campaign asset itself if the output is strong enough. This produces a synthesised directional image in under a minute.

###### Use case 6: Merging two location aesthetics for a brand campaign

Situation. A travel brand, hospitality company, or lifestyle business wants to create campaign imagery that suggests a blended experience - the interior warmth of one venue combined with the outdoor scenery of another, or the energy of one city merged with the skyline of another. What they want to bring to life. A single atmospheric image that feels like it captures both locations - creating a sense of place that draws from multiple real references into an idealised version. The problem this feature solves. Location compositing at a professional level requires either shooting on location or sourcing rights to multiple location images and hiring a compositor to blend them believably. Most businesses settle for one location or one generic stock image. What it creates. A merged environmental image that draws from both source locations - their light quality, architectural details, colour palette, and atmosphere - blended into a single scene that reads as natural and evocative rather than digitally assembled. The opportunity. Campaign imagery communicates a richer sense of place and experience than a single location photo could. The brand can create imagery that represents an idealised version of what they offer without a multi-location production shoot. This produces each image in under a minute.

###### Use case 7: Producing a consistent visual identity across a content series

Situation. A content creator, podcaster, or brand running an ongoing series - weekly posts, a content programme, a course curriculum - needs a consistent visual style across episode or post covers that feel like a unified series without being identical. What they want to bring to life. A family of images that all share the same visual DNA - same colour treatment, same general composition style, same mood - but with different subjects or content per episode, so the series looks designed rather than assembled. The problem this feature solves. Creating a visual template and applying it to new images each week requires design software skills, a template, and time per episode. Without a systematic approach, the content looks inconsistent over time, which undermines the sense of a professional series. What it creates. A new episode or post image that blends the series’ established visual style (from an existing cover) with the specific content or subject of the new episode - same look and feel, different subject - in square or portrait format for social and feed use. The opportunity. The series builds a recognisable visual identity over time without requiring design work each episode. Audiences start to recognise the style before they read the title, which builds trust and increases recognition in busy feeds. This produces each new cover in minutes by blending from the established style.

###### Use case 8: Combining a hand-drawn illustration with a photograph

Situation. An artist, creative agency, or brand wants to create images in a mixed-media style - hand-drawn elements or illustrations merged with photography - for use in editorial, advertising, or social content. This aesthetic is popular but requires significant artistic skill to execute convincingly. What they want to bring to life. Images that feel genuinely hybrid - photographic realism in some areas, illustrative line work or watercolour treatment in others - merged seamlessly rather than looking like a Photoshop filter. The problem this feature solves. Mixed-media imagery typically requires a skilled illustrator working on top of a photograph, which is time-consuming and expensive. Tools that apply illustration effects to photos produce generic outputs that don’t look hand-crafted. What it creates. A new image that combines the realism of the photographic source with the expressive qualities of the illustration reference, creating a cohesive mixed-media result rather than a simple overlay. The opportunity. The brand or creator develops a distinctive visual language that’s harder to copy than standard photography or standard illustration. The style becomes a signature that makes their content immediately recognisable. This explores and produces the same aesthetic in minutes.

##### Marketing angle library

- Applying one brand’s style to another image. Lead with: Style transfer requires either specialist software, a design team with retouching skills, or an AI tool that accepts two separate inputs. Payoff: The creative team can explore style directions quickly by testing multiple reference style images against the same subject.
- Placing a product in a lifestyle context. Lead with: Lifestyle product photography requires either shooting the product on location or expensive compositing by a retoucher. Payoff: The product gets placed in multiple lifestyle contexts - kitchen, office, outdoor, social setting - without shooting in each one.
- Creating a consistent visual across two team headshots. Lead with: Headshot editing to create visual consistency requires a retoucher to colour-match across multiple images, which is time-consuming and has to be redone every time a new team member is added or a photo is updated. Payoff: The website’s team page looks professional and cohesive from day one rather than growing in an ad-hoc way as photos accumulate.
- Exploring a product design concept before production. Lead with: Design exploration before production requires either a 3D modeller, a product visualiser, or a hand-crafted composite by a designer. Payoff: The team explores hybrid directions quickly and cheaply before committing to tooling, materials, or production costs.
- Building a campaign image from multiple reference images. Lead with: Mood board references are typically used as a creative brief for a photographer or illustrator, which requires interpretation, a production session, and multiple rounds of feedback before the output looks like what the brief described. Payoff: The creative team gets from mood board to usable campaign asset in minutes rather than weeks.
- Merging two location aesthetics for a brand campaign. Lead with: Location compositing at a professional level requires either shooting on location or sourcing rights to multiple location images and hiring a compositor to blend them believably. Payoff: Campaign imagery communicates a richer sense of place and experience than a single location photo could.
- Producing a consistent visual identity across a content series. Lead with: Creating a visual template and applying it to new images each week requires design software skills, a template, and time per episode. Payoff: The series builds a recognisable visual identity over time without requiring design work each episode.
- Combining a hand-drawn illustration with a photograph. Lead with: Mixed-media imagery typically requires a skilled illustrator working on top of a photograph, which is time-consuming and expensive. Payoff: The brand or creator develops a distinctive visual language that’s harder to copy than standard photography or standard illustration.
- More mileage from assets you already own. Use editing, blending, and reference-led workflows instead of always starting from scratch.
- From one image to a campaign system. Show how visuals can flow into graphics, social, sites, and presentations.
- First draft is not the finish line. Studio refinement makes AI imagery an iterative production workflow.

#### AI Photo Shoot

##### Public definition

Photo Shoot produces a coordinated gallery of images centred on a single subject - people, a product, a service, or a location. Rather than creating one image from a prompt and being done, it runs a two-step creative process: first it builds a creative brief and 6 sample shots for you to review, then it produces the full gallery after you approve the direction or ask for a reshoot. The result is a set of images that share consistent style, lighting, and mood while varying in framing, angle, and moment - the output of a professional photo shoot, created in minutes.

##### Customer problem this solves

Businesses need more visual content than they can realistically photograph, commission, retouch, and repurpose by hand.

##### Why this feature is valuable

This feature is valuable because it reduces manual handoffs around customer jobs such as: people: Founder portraits for a new business website, people: Team imagery for a company rebrand, product: Ecommerce hero photography for a new product launch. It also matters as part of a larger system: the source, conversation, or finished output can remain connected to the broader IdeoLab project instead of being treated as an isolated one-off task.

##### Why this matters in the IdeoLab experience

General image tools can generate and edit excellent images. IdeoLab’s stronger marketing story is not simply image quality; it is that visual creation sits inside a broader business-content system where research, references, source images, social workflows, graphics, sites, and automation can contribute to the same project. At the feature level, emphasize the workflow around the asset - source context, review, refinement, reuse, publishing, or analytics - not unsupported claims that the raw AI output is universally better.

##### Detailed use-case library

###### Use case 1: People: Founder portraits for a new business website

Situation. A business owner, consultant, or solo founder is launching or relaunching their website and needs professional-looking portraits for the about page, bio section, and speaker profile - images that convey credibility and approachability without being stiff or generic. What they want to bring to life. A set of consistent portraits that feel natural and professional - different framings, different moments in the same space - so the about page has variety without looking like it came from a photo booth. The problem this feature solves. Booking a brand photographer for a portrait session costs money and requires scheduling. The resulting gallery is fixed - if the business direction changes or a new use case comes up, there’s no easy way to get new shots. And most people don’t photograph naturally under pressure. What it creates. A coordinated set of portrait images in a consistent environment - soft natural light, modern office or clean studio - showing the founder from different angles and distances with expressions that read as confident and human, in the ratio suited to the website layout. The opportunity. The about page goes live with imagery that makes the business look established and intentional from day one. The gallery provides enough variety for LinkedIn, email signatures, and PR outreach without looking like the same headshot everywhere. This produces a cohesive gallery in under five minutes.

###### Use case 2: People: Team imagery for a company rebrand

Situation. A growing company is rebranding and needs updated team imagery for the website, social profiles, and recruitment materials. The current team page is a mix of old headshots from different eras, different photographers, and different visual styles that make the company look fragmented. What they want to bring to life. A unified team visual direction - people shown in the context of the work environment, with consistent lighting and mood - that makes the team page feel cohesive and contemporary. The problem this feature solves. Getting an entire team photographed in a consistent way requires coordinating everyone’s schedules, booking a photographer, and either doing it all in one day or accepting inconsistency across different sessions. Brands often end up with some people well-photographed and others not. What it creates. A team imagery concept set - people around a table, people in conversation, people working - with consistent warm natural lighting, similar colour treatment, and a candid energy that feels more honest than formal corporate headshots. The opportunity. The team page becomes a recruitment asset. Prospective employees see an environment they want to work in before they read the job description. The brand feels human and contemporary rather than corporate and static. This produces a directional set and usable gallery in minutes.

###### Use case 3: Product: Ecommerce hero photography for a new product launch

Situation. An ecommerce brand or product-focused business is launching a new product. They need a gallery of product images for the listing page, the homepage feature section, and the launch campaign - multiple angles, different contexts, a hero shot and detail shots - before the product has been photographed. What they want to bring to life. A complete set of product images that makes the new product look intentional and premium - clean studio shots, lifestyle context images, close-up detail shots - all in a consistent visual style. The problem this feature solves. Product photography requires either the physical product, a photographer, a studio, and styling - or expensive 3D product rendering. Neither is available on the timeline of a fast-moving launch. The listing page often goes up with placeholder images or generic visuals. What it creates. A product shoot gallery - hero shots, context shots, and detail shots - with the product described in the brief rendered consistently across all images, in the square or portrait ratio needed for listing pages and social sharing. The opportunity. The product listing launches looking finished and credible from day one. The gallery generates confidence in buyers who might otherwise hesitate because the images look provisional. Each image in the gallery can be used across different marketing channels without the visual family falling apart. This produces a full gallery in minutes.

###### Use case 4: Product: Seasonal campaign imagery for an existing product

Situation. A retailer, brand, or ecommerce seller wants to promote an existing product in a seasonal context - the same product styled for a holiday, a season change, or a campaign theme - without reshooting it each time. What they want to bring to life. The product, familiar from existing imagery, now placed in a seasonal context - festive environment, seasonal colour palette, appropriate props and styling - that makes the campaign feel fresh and timely. The problem this feature solves. Seasonal campaigns require seasonal imagery. Reshooting the same product in a different context every few months is expensive and logistically difficult. Most brands either reuse the same evergreen images across seasons or invest in full reshoots, with nothing in between. What it creates. A gallery of the product in a seasonal or campaign-specific setting - consistent style, different environment - with multiple framings that suit different placements (email header, social square, story). The opportunity. The campaign feels current and intentional. The product gets new life in each seasonal context without a full production investment. The images are varied enough across the gallery to fuel a full campaign across email, social, and ads. This produces a campaign-ready gallery per season in minutes.

###### Use case 5: Service: Marketing imagery for a professional service

Situation. A consultant, lawyer, accountant, coach, or professional service provider needs marketing imagery that shows what they actually do - not just a headshot and a stock photo of a handshake, but images that communicate the experience of working with them. What they want to bring to life. A set of images that show the service in action - a conversation, a review of documents, a coaching moment, a presentation - captured in a way that feels genuine rather than staged. The problem this feature solves. Professional services are hard to photograph because the work is often invisible or highly contextual. Generic stock photography of business people is interchangeable and builds no trust. Real photography of the service requires access, client consent, and scheduling. What it creates. A gallery of service-in-action images - specific to the type of service, the setting described, the people involved - with consistent professional lighting and a mood that fits the service’s positioning (approachable, authoritative, collaborative). The opportunity. The website and marketing materials show what it actually feels like to engage the service. Prospective clients can imagine the experience before they reach out. The images work across the site, LinkedIn, and proposals. This produces a service imagery gallery in minutes.

###### Use case 6: Service: Imagery for a trade or skilled craft business

Situation. A plumber, electrician, chef, hairdresser, tattoo artist, or other skilled tradesperson wants imagery that shows the craft in action - hands at work, tools in use, the moment of skill - for their website, local listings, and social media. What they want to bring to life. A gallery of images centred on the craft itself - specific, evocative, and professional enough to make the work look intentional and worth paying for. The problem this feature solves. Most tradespeople have no professional imagery at all, or rely on after-photos of finished work. The craft itself - the skill, the process, the moment - is rarely photographed because it requires a second person with a camera and good timing. What it creates. A gallery of process-centred images showing the service being performed - the specific tools, the action, the environment - with a visual style that conveys craft and professionalism in the ratio needed for social and web use. The opportunity. The business stands out from competitors who have no imagery or generic stock. Local discovery (Google, Yelp, social) improves when listings have strong visual content. The business looks as professional as the work it produces. This produces a gallery in minutes.

###### Use case 7: Location: Hospitality venue imagery for a new listing

Situation. A restaurant, hotel, Airbnb host, event venue, or café is setting up a new listing or relaunching an existing one and needs imagery that makes the space look inviting - not just what it looks like, but how it feels to be there. What they want to bring to life. A gallery of atmospheric venue images - the space in its best light, different areas featured, different times of day implied - that communicates the experience before someone arrives. The problem this feature solves. Professional interior photography requires a photographer, advance planning, and usually staging the space - managing lighting, arranging furniture, controlling for guests or staff in frame. Most venue owners either use phone photos that undersell the space or invest in a shoot they can only afford once. What it creates. A gallery of the venue’s key areas - entrance, seating, ambiance details - in the specific aesthetic described: warm and intimate, bright and airy, moody and cinematic - with consistent light treatment and a sense of atmosphere across every image. The opportunity. The listing converts better. Travellers and diners book based on how a space makes them feel, and strong imagery creates the emotional response before they experience it in person. The gallery is varied enough to use across TripAdvisor, Airbnb, Google Business, the website, and Instagram. This produces a gallery in minutes.

###### Use case 8: Location: Office or studio environment imagery for a company about page

Situation. A company, creative studio, or co-working space wants imagery of their workspace for the about page, employer branding, or recruitment materials - images that convey the culture, the environment, and what it feels like to work there. What they want to bring to life. A gallery of workspace images that are intentional and varied - not just a photo of desks, but the details, the spaces between spaces, the light coming in - that communicate “this is a good place to work.” The problem this feature solves. Office photography is either treated as an afterthought (phone photos of empty desks) or treated as a major production (a corporate photographer who needs access, scheduling, and release forms for anyone in frame). The result is either imagery that’s too casual or imagery that’s too staged. What it creates. A gallery of workspace images - specific areas described in the brief, the aesthetic and culture communicated through environment and light - that feel genuinely representative of the space without requiring access to the actual room. The opportunity. Employer branding improves. Candidates look at the workspace imagery before they read the job description, and what they see forms an expectation of the culture. A gallery that communicates thoughtful design and a good working environment is a recruitment asset. For a growing company that needs updated imagery regularly, annual photography costs accumulate quickly. This produces a current-looking gallery in minutes.

###### Use case 9: People: Customer story imagery for marketing campaigns

Situation. A brand, SaaS company, or service business wants to illustrate customer success stories - case studies, testimonials, campaign imagery - with imagery of customers in the context of the problem being solved or the outcome being achieved. What they want to bring to life. Images of real-world customers using the product or service, in the environments and situations the brand’s customers actually inhabit - not posed testimonial photos, but contextual, human images that make the story believable. The problem this feature solves. Securing access to real customers for a photo shoot is logistically difficult and requires releases, coordination, and often budget to compensate participants. Stock photos of happy customers with laptops are interchangeable and lack credibility. What it creates. A gallery of contextual customer images - specific to the industry, the use case, and the moment of use described in the brief - with consistent lighting and a tone that fits the campaign’s emotional register. The opportunity. The case study or campaign lands with more credibility because the imagery is specific rather than generic. Prospective customers see people like themselves in situations they recognise, which makes the testimonial or campaign message resonate. For a series of case study stories, that cost multiplies per story. This produces a contextual gallery per story in minutes.

###### Use case 10: Location: Retail environment imagery for a marketing refresh

Situation. A retail brand, franchise, or multi-location business wants fresh imagery of their retail environment - stores, display areas, branded spaces - for a website refresh, seasonal campaign, or franchisee recruitment materials. What they want to bring to life. A gallery of retail environment images that show the brand at its best - the visual merchandising, the in-store atmosphere, the moment a customer enters and feels the brand - in a way that communicates the brand promise visually. The problem this feature solves. Getting imagery from every store in a multi-location business requires either centralised photography visits (expensive, logistically complex) or relying on each location to supply their own photos (inconsistent quality, inconsistent presentation). Most retail brands have a perpetual gap between how they want to look and what photography they actually have. What it creates. A gallery of retail environment images specific to the brand’s aesthetic - the colour scheme, the display style, the lighting feel - with enough variation across the gallery to suit a full campaign rollout across digital and print. The opportunity. Marketing and recruitment materials look consistent and on-brand regardless of which store they represent. Franchisee recruitment is supported by imagery that makes the concept look compelling. The website stays visually current without a fresh photography production per season. This produces campaign-ready imagery per location type in minutes.
