## 8. AI Graphics

### System-level marketing definition

IdeoLab turns prompts and connected source material into finished infographics, flyers, and video thumbnails, then keeps those assets editable inside Graphics Studio.

### System-level customer problem

Most non-designers know the message they need to communicate but struggle to turn it into a clear, professional visual without templates, design software, or a specialist.

### System-level differentiation

Canva and Adobe Express are extremely strong design platforms with templates and expanding generative AI. IdeoLab’s angle is not that templates are obsolete everywhere; it is that a business can move directly from its own research, copy, images, or project context into a created graphic without rebuilding the message in a separate design workflow.

### Feature index

- AI Infographics
- AI Flyers
- AI Video Thumbnails
- Graphics Studio

#### AI Infographics

##### Public definition

Infographics creates a structured, multi-section visual - data, comparisons, processes, timelines, anatomy diagrams. Every Send produces a new Studio with one infographic in it - or several, if you ask for multiple variations. You control the layout (Type), the look (Style), the orientation (Size), and how many you want at once (Variations).

##### Customer problem this solves

Most non-designers know the message they need to communicate but struggle to turn it into a clear, professional visual without templates, design software, or a specialist.

##### Why this feature is valuable

This feature is valuable because it reduces manual handoffs around customer jobs such as: stop the scroll with a stat that matters, turn a complicated offer into an obvious yes, make a product impossible to misunderstand. It also matters as part of a larger system: the source, conversation, or finished output can remain connected to the broader IdeoLab project instead of being treated as an isolated one-off task.

##### Why this matters in the IdeoLab experience

Canva and Adobe Express are extremely strong design platforms with templates and expanding generative AI. IdeoLab’s angle is not that templates are obsolete everywhere; it is that a business can move directly from its own research, copy, images, or project context into a created graphic without rebuilding the message in a separate design workflow. At the feature level, emphasize the workflow around the asset - source context, review, refinement, reuse, publishing, or analytics - not unsupported claims that the raw AI output is universally better.

##### Detailed use-case library

###### Use case 1: Stopping the scroll with a stat that matters

Situation. A marketer or brand owner has a compelling piece of data - a study result, an industry trend, a before-and-after number - and needs it to cut through on social media where plain text gets ignored and attention lasts seconds. What they want to bring to life. A single powerful statistic or short set of numbers that tells a story at a glance and makes people stop, read, and share. The problem this feature solves. A text post with numbers blends into the feed. The data exists and the insight is real, but nothing makes it visual, ownable, or worth sharing. The moment passes and the opportunity to build authority is lost. What it creates. A bold, single-focus statistical layout with the numbers as the hero - large, clear, surrounded by just enough context to make them land. Created using a data-led layout and a clean modern visual style, it looks like something a brand would pay a designer to produce. Ready to post in under two minutes. The opportunity. Followers stop scrolling because something caught their eye. They save it, share it to their story, send it to someone who needs to see it. The brand gets associated with knowledge and authority in their space. Over time that consistency builds a following that trusts them before they ever try to sell anything. One compelling graphic can reach thousands of people who never would have read a caption. This produces the same output in under two minutes with no design skills required.

###### Use case 2: Turning a complicated offer into an obvious yes

Situation. A service business, consultant, or agency offers something valuable but hard to explain - a multi-stage process, a layered package, a methodology - and needs potential clients to understand it instantly on a website, in a proposal, or before a sales call. What they want to bring to life. The scope, the stages, the deliverables, and the outcome - laid out so clearly that a prospect gets it without needing anyone to walk them through it. The problem this feature solves. Complex offers lose people before they reach the good part. A wall of text in a proposal or on a service page creates hesitation instead of confidence. Most prospects won’t read far enough to understand why it’s worth it. What it creates. A clean, structured process layout that walks the reader through each stage in sequence - using a step-by-step flow with icons and a professional flat visual style that feels polished and intentional. Something a prospect can read in thirty seconds and immediately understand. The opportunity. A prospect arrives at the sales call already understanding what they are buying instead of spending the first twenty minutes asking basic questions. They forward it to the decision maker who wasn’t on the call. The proposal feels more credible than anything a competitor sent. The business closes faster and with less back and forth. This produces one on demand in minutes that can be reused and refined across every pitch at no additional cost.

###### Use case 3: Making a product impossible to misunderstand

Situation. An ecommerce brand or local business needs to explain what makes their product different - the features, the materials, the benefits - to someone who is browsing and deciding whether it is worth their money. What they want to bring to life. The details that matter most to a buyer, laid out so that the value of the product is obvious without needing to read a paragraph of description. The problem this feature solves. Written product descriptions require effort, and most people won’t make that effort. The features that justify the price get buried in text nobody finishes. Sales are lost not because the product isn’t good, but because the value never landed. What it creates. A side-by-side comparison or feature breakdown layout that puts the most important details front and centre - using a bold, icon-led visual style that makes each benefit scannable in seconds. Designed to live on a product page, in an ad, or as an organic social post. The opportunity. A potential customer sees exactly what they are getting, understands why it is worth the price, and moves toward a purchase without needing to search for more information or ask a question. Return rates drop because buyers knew what they were getting. The product page converts better without touching a single word of copy. This produces it in minutes and can be iterated instantly when the product or offer changes.

###### Use case 4: Building a content series without a design team

Situation. A solopreneur, coach, or creator needs to show up consistently on social media with educational content that builds trust and grows an audience - but has no design resources, no budget to outsource, and no time to learn a design tool. What they want to bring to life. A repeatable format - a weekly tip, a framework, a breakdown, a how-to - that feels on brand and looks intentional every single time. The problem this feature solves. Without a designer, content either looks unprofessional or never gets made at all. The ideas exist but the execution bottleneck kills consistency. Inconsistency kills growth. What it creates. A series of clean, structured educational graphics - each one built around a clear idea with a visual hierarchy that guides the reader through the content naturally. Using a consistent visual style across creations, each post looks like it belongs to the same brand without manually matching colours or layouts. The opportunity. They show up every week with content that positions them as an expert. Their audience grows because the content is easy to consume and easy to share. They spend their time on ideas and strategy instead of fighting with design tools. Over months that consistency compounds into a following, a reputation, and an inbound pipeline that didn’t exist before. This removes that cost entirely while maintaining quality and allowing unlimited iteration.

###### Use case 5: Giving a new process somewhere to live after the meeting

Situation. An operations manager or business owner has rolled out a new workflow, policy, or procedure and needs the whole team to follow it consistently - not just the people who were in the room when it was explained. What they want to bring to life. The steps, the sequence, who owns what, and where things can go wrong - clear enough that nobody needs to ask for clarification and nobody can misinterpret it. The problem this feature solves. Written SOPs and email instructions get read once and forgotten. Verbal walkthroughs don’t survive the day. People revert to old habits because there is nothing simple enough to refer back to when they need it. What it creates. A connected step-by-step flow diagram with each stage clearly labelled, sequenced with directional arrows, and supported by short descriptions - built using a process layout and a clean professional visual style that works pinned to a wall, saved in a shared drive, or embedded in an onboarding document. The opportunity. The team has one thing to look at when they are unsure. New hires get up to speed faster because the process is visible from day one. The manager stops answering the same questions on repeat. Mistakes that came from ambiguity disappear. The business runs more consistently without the owner being the single point of failure. An instructional designer charges more. This produces a clear, visual version in minutes that can be updated instantly whenever the process changes.

###### Use case 6: Reporting results in a way that actually gets remembered

Situation. A marketer, account manager, or analyst needs to share performance data or results with a client, executive, or stakeholder who doesn’t have the time or context to interpret raw numbers and needs to feel confident in what they are seeing. What they want to bring to life. The key metrics, the trend, and the so-what - the story behind the numbers communicated in a way that shows progress and builds trust without requiring the audience to do any work. The problem this feature solves. Spreadsheets and exported data communicate nothing to someone who isn’t already close to the numbers. The work gets done but the value of it never lands. Clients feel uncertain. Executives ask for the same clarification every month. The relationship suffers not because of poor results but because of poor communication. What it creates. A data-led visual report that turns the most important numbers into a clear narrative - using a statistical layout with growth indicators, clean typography, and a professional visual style that makes the results feel authoritative and easy to present in any setting. The opportunity. The client or executive reads it in thirty seconds and understands exactly where things stand. They feel confident in the relationship and the investment. The person presenting it is seen as someone who communicates with clarity and professionalism - which builds trust far beyond the numbers themselves. Renewals and approvals become easier because the value is always visible. This produces a polished report visual on demand for any reporting cycle at no additional cost.

###### Use case 7: Showing the journey that makes someone believe in what you’ve built

Situation. A founder, brand owner, or community leader is marking a milestone - an anniversary, a chapter close, a roadmap reveal - and wants the people around them (customers, supporters, team) to feel the weight of what’s been built and where it’s going next. What they want to bring to life. The key moments - when it started, what changed along the way, what mattered, what’s next - laid out so the story lands instead of getting lost in a long caption nobody reads. The problem this feature solves. A list of milestones told in plain text feels like a CV. A few photos in a carousel don’t carry the throughline. The emotional weight of the journey - the proof that this thing was built carefully over time - never lands. Trust that could have deepened slips away. What it creates. A chronological visual that walks the eye from first moment to most recent, with each milestone labelled and paced so the journey feels real. Built using a timeline layout and a warm, brand-aligned visual style, it works as a social post, a website section, an email banner, or a print piece for an anniversary event. The opportunity. Customers see how long the brand has been earning trust. Supporters share it because it captures something they’ve been part of. The team feels recognised. New audiences arriving for the first time get the full story in fifteen seconds and decide whether this is a brand they care about. Anniversaries become marketing moments. Brand history stops being trapped in the founder’s head. This produces a polished version in minutes that can be updated the moment a new milestone happens.

###### Use case 8: Helping someone understand what’s actually inside what they’re paying for

Situation. A creator, educator, service provider, or product owner is trying to explain how something is built, what’s inside it, or what makes it work - to an audience that can’t take it apart for themselves. What they want to bring to life. The components, the layers, the structure, or the system - laid out so the audience sees what’s actually in there and grasps in seconds why it works the way it does. The problem this feature solves. Describing internals in words asks the audience to do all the imagining. A single product photo doesn’t show what’s inside. Most people give up before they understand, which means the value, the craft, or the lesson never lands. Premium products get judged like commodities. Lessons that need a clear mental model become hard to teach. What it creates. An anatomy-style breakdown that pulls the subject apart visually, with each component labelled and connected back to the whole. Built using an exploded-view layout and a clean technical visual style that suits learning or product-education contexts. The result works on a sales page, in a customer FAQ, inside a training module, or as a YouTube video thumbnail. The opportunity. A potential buyer understands why the product is priced the way it is, because they see the work inside. A new hire or student gets a system explained in a fraction of the usual time. A customer support reply that used to take five paragraphs becomes one shareable image. Education that used to require a video becomes a single graphic anyone can save and reference later. Specialist medical or engineering visuals run higher. This produces one in minutes and can be regenerated as the product or curriculum evolves.

##### Marketing angle library

- Stopping the scroll with a stat that matters. Lead with: A text post with numbers blends into the feed. Payoff: Followers stop scrolling because something caught their eye.
- Turning a complicated offer into an obvious yes. Lead with: Complex offers lose people before they reach the good part. Payoff: A prospect arrives at the sales call already understanding what they are buying instead of spending the first twenty minutes asking basic questions.
- Making a product impossible to misunderstand. Lead with: Written product descriptions require effort, and most people won’t make that effort. Payoff: A potential customer sees exactly what they are getting, understands why it is worth the price, and moves toward a purchase without needing to search for more information or ask a question.
- Building a content series without a design team. Lead with: Without a designer, content either looks unprofessional or never gets made at all. Payoff: They show up every week with content that positions them as an expert.
- Giving a new process somewhere to live after the meeting. Lead with: Written SOPs and email instructions get read once and forgotten. Payoff: The team has one thing to look at when they are unsure.
- Reporting results in a way that actually gets remembered. Lead with: Spreadsheets and exported data communicate nothing to someone who isn’t already close to the numbers. Payoff: The client or executive reads it in thirty seconds and understands exactly where things stand.
- Showing the journey that makes someone believe in what you’ve built. Lead with: A list of milestones told in plain text feels like a CV. Payoff: Customers see how long the brand has been earning trust.
- Helping someone understand what’s actually inside what they’re paying for. Lead with: Describing internals in words asks the audience to do all the imagining. Payoff: A potential buyer understands why the product is priced the way it is, because they see the work inside.
- Skip the blank template. Start from the message, research, or source material rather than hunting for a layout first.
- Communication before decoration. The graphic should clarify the offer or idea, not merely look attractive.
- One message, multiple visual forms. Research and copy can become an infographic, flyer, or thumbnail in the same project.

##### Claims to emphasize

- Show how the feature contributes useful context or finished work that can support another IdeoLab marketing activity.

- When comparing alternatives, acknowledge their real strengths and make a narrower workflow-based differentiation claim.

#### AI Flyers

##### Public definition

Flyers creates a promotional 1-page graphic for events, sales, announcements, launches, parties, and the like. Each Send produces a new Studio with one flyer in it - or several, if you ask for multiple variations - assembled from a structured Creative Brief you review and approve first. Each flyer has a layered layout: headline, support copy, date and location, badge, CTA, hero / supporting / background imagery.

##### Customer problem this solves

Most non-designers know the message they need to communicate but struggle to turn it into a clear, professional visual without templates, design software, or a specialist.

##### Why this feature is valuable

This feature is valuable because it reduces manual handoffs around customer jobs such as: fill a room when the date is locked in, drive urgency around a limited-time offer, gett people to register before a deadline. It also matters as part of a larger system: the source, conversation, or finished output can remain connected to the broader IdeoLab project instead of being treated as an isolated one-off task.

##### Why this matters in the IdeoLab experience

Canva and Adobe Express are extremely strong design platforms with templates and expanding generative AI. IdeoLab’s angle is not that templates are obsolete everywhere; it is that a business can move directly from its own research, copy, images, or project context into a created graphic without rebuilding the message in a separate design workflow. At the feature level, emphasize the workflow around the asset - source context, review, refinement, reuse, publishing, or analytics - not unsupported claims that the raw AI output is universally better.

##### Detailed use-case library

###### Use case 1: Filling a room when the date is locked in

Situation. A small business owner, community organiser, or event host has scheduled a one-time gathering - a class, a tasting, a launch night, a community meetup - and needs people to actually show up. What they want to bring to life. The reason to come, the time, the place, and the feeling that this is going to be worth carving out an evening for. The problem this feature solves. A text post or email about an event is easy to skim past. People say “sounds nice” and forget. Without a single shareable image, there’s nothing to pin to a community board, send to a friend, or post on a story. The room stays half full and the host wonders why turnout was low. What it creates. A 1-page promotional image with the event name big at the top, a short line of support copy that captures the vibe, the date and location front and centre, a clear call to action like “RSVP” or “See you there”, and a hero image that sets the mood - warm and casual for a community event, bold and modern for a launch. The visual style and layout are reviewed before rendering so colours, hero image, and tone match the gathering’s feel. The opportunity. People share the flyer because it looks intentional, not improvised. Friends invite friends. The event lands in group chats and on community boards. Turnout is higher because the message felt real, not last-minute. The host is seen as someone who runs things properly, which makes the next event easier to fill too. This produces a ready-to-share version in minutes, plus variations to test on different channels.

###### Use case 2: Driving urgency around a limited-time offer

Situation. An ecommerce store, retailer, salon, restaurant, or course creator is running a short-window promotion - a seasonal sale, a flash discount, a launch bonus, a deadline-driven offer - and needs the urgency to land on every channel they post on. What they want to bring to life. The discount or offer, the deadline, and the reason to act now - communicated loud enough to cut through and clean enough to feel intentional, not desperate. The problem this feature solves. A plain post saying “20% off this weekend” doesn’t move people. Without a high-contrast visual with the offer as the hero, the promotion drowns in the feed. Customers who would have bought never see it clearly enough to act. The window closes and revenue gets left on the table. What it creates. A bold 1-page graphic with a prominent badge - “40% OFF”, “FREE SHIPPING”, “ENDS SUNDAY” - sitting on top of a hero image of the product or service. The headline, support copy, deadline, and call to action like “Shop now” are reviewed and refined before the final render, with a high-contrast design style chosen to cut through a busy feed. Multiple variations are created at once so the team can A/B test on ads, stories, and email. The opportunity. The urgency lands. Customers tap, click, convert. The same asset works as a paid ad, an organic post, a story, and an email header without rework. The promotion drives more revenue per send because the message is unmissable. The team builds a library of promo-ready templates they can reuse the next time a sale runs. This produces a full set of promo variations in minutes, with no production lag between deciding to run a sale and being ready to push it live.

###### Use case 3: Getting people to register before a deadline

Situation. A coach, educator, fitness trainer, online course creator, or workshop host is opening enrolment for a session with limited spots and needs to convert interest into signups before the date arrives. What they want to bring to life. What the workshop is, who it’s for, when it happens, what someone walks away with, and a clear next step to register. The problem this feature solves. A landing page link with no visual context gets ignored. People are interested but don’t click. Without a single shareable image that captures the offer and the deadline, signups trickle in instead of filling the room. The workshop runs under capacity and revenue is left behind. What it creates. A clean 1-page graphic with the workshop title as the headline, a line or two on the outcome, the date and location (or “live online”) shown prominently, and a call to action like “Save your spot” or “Register now”. The visual style is soft and inviting - palette and typography reviewed before rendering - and a hero image sets the tone for the kind of session this is. Created in a portrait layout for easy social and email use. The opportunity. Interest converts into registrations because the next step is obvious. The flyer gets shared by the people most likely to attend with the people they want to attend with. The host fills the room and shows up to teach a session that’s actually full. The same flyer template runs every time a new session is opened, with only the date and details swapped. This produces a polished registration-ready flyer in minutes and can be re-rendered every cohort with no additional design cost.

###### Use case 4: Showing what working with you actually looks like

Situation. A consultant, agency, freelancer, or service provider is sending a proposal, replying to an inbound enquiry, or following up after a discovery call, and needs the prospect to walk away with a clear picture of what they’re being offered. What they want to bring to life. What the service is, what’s included, what it produces, and what makes choosing this provider different - packaged so a prospect can read it without a meeting and forward it to a decision maker who wasn’t on the call. The problem this feature solves. Long PDFs and dense email replies don’t land. The decision maker who needs to sign off never gets a clean version. The provider comes across as someone who sends text-heavy documents, not someone who treats their craft with intention. Deals die slowly in inboxes. What it creates. A 1-page service overview with a clear headline that names the offer, supporting copy that frames the outcome, a clean list or visual breakdown of what’s included, and a call to action like “Let’s talk” or “Reserve your slot”. Designed in portrait orientation for clean PDF and email delivery, with a professional visual style - palette, typography, hero image - reviewed before rendering so it feels on-brand instead of generic. The opportunity. The prospect forwards it without needing to summarise it. The decision maker reads it in thirty seconds. The provider looks more polished than every competitor who sent a wall of text. The same 1-pager travels across every channel - proposals, emails, websites, link-in-bios - doing the work of an in-person pitch when nobody is in the room. This produces a polished version in minutes and can be re-rendered the moment the offer, pricing, or positioning changes.

###### Use case 5: Recruiting the right people for the right role

Situation. A founder, hiring manager, or agency recruiting on behalf of a client is opening a role and needs to attract the kind of candidates who care, not just anyone who saw the LinkedIn post. What they want to bring to life. The role, what makes the team or company worth joining, what the work is actually like, and how to apply - packaged with enough character that the right people lean in instead of scrolling past. The problem this feature solves. A plain job post is one of thousands. The companies that get the best candidates are the ones whose openings feel like an invitation, not a job description. Without a visual asset that’s easy to share on a story, a Slack, or a personal feed, the role stays trapped on a job board and the team waits weeks for applications that never come. What it creates. A modern recruiting flyer with a headline like “We’re hiring” over a hero image that hints at the team or the work, a short line of support copy describing who this role is for, a call to action like “Apply now”, and a footer with the URL or contact. The design style is reviewed before rendering - colours, typography, and accents pulled to match the company brand so it feels distinct from generic job-board listings. The opportunity. People share it because it looks like a brand they’d want to work for, not a job ad. The right candidates apply because the role feels real. The team gets to choose from people who care, not from whoever was desperate enough to apply to anything. Time-to-hire shortens and quality of pipeline goes up. This produces one in minutes, with variations for LinkedIn, Instagram, and printed posters at no additional cost.

###### Use case 6: Making sure the whole team gets the same announcement

Situation. A founder, ops manager, or team lead has something the whole company needs to know - a new policy, a leadership change, a team milestone, a holiday schedule, a tool rollout - and needs every person to actually take it in, not just the ones who happened to read the email. What they want to bring to life. What is happening, what it changes, when it kicks in, and what people need to do or not do - communicated with enough intention that nobody can claim they missed it. The problem this feature solves. Long internal emails get scanned. Slack posts scroll off. Verbal announcements in a meeting don’t survive the day. The information that matters gets lost, the people who need it most miss it, and the team operates on stale or wrong assumptions. The leader ends up answering the same questions for weeks. What it creates. A clean 1-page announcement with a clear headline, a short paragraph of support copy framing the change, the effective date front and centre, and a footer with who to contact if there are questions. The visual style is professional and on-brand - palette and typography reviewed before rendering - so it feels intentional, like something leadership cared enough to design properly. Suitable to pin in Slack, post on a team board, or print and pin in the office. The opportunity. Everyone sees the same message. The team trusts that announcements from leadership are deliberate and considered. Confusion drops. Repetitive questions disappear. Culture strengthens because the company is communicating like a company that respects its people’s time, not like one firing off emails in a hurry. This produces a polished announcement in minutes that any manager can spin up themselves.

###### Use case 7: Telling customers something they need to know without it feeling like an email

Situation. A local business, restaurant, salon, clinic, or service provider has something to communicate to customers - new opening hours, a temporary closure, a menu update, a location move, a service change - and needs the information to land warmly, not bureaucratically. What they want to bring to life. The change, when it takes effect, and how it affects the customer - communicated with warmth and clarity, so people don’t show up at the wrong time or feel confused about what’s happening. The problem this feature solves. A wall of text in a post or a sterile email update feels cold and easy to ignore. Customers miss the change, show up at a closed door, complain online, or just stop coming. The business loses trust over something that was meant to be a simple heads-up. Word of mouth turns negative for the wrong reason. What it creates. A clean 1-page graphic with a friendly headline, a short paragraph explaining what’s changing, the effective date prominently placed, a footer thanking the customer or offering an alternative, and a hero image that keeps the tone warm. The visual style is reviewed before rendering - palette and feel tuned to match how the business already talks to its customers - and rendered in portrait for easy posting on a window, on a story, and in an email. The opportunity. Customers see the change in time. They feel respected because the business communicated it properly, not like a corporate update. Goodwill is preserved through what could have been a frustrating moment. The flyer doubles as an in-store sign and a digital post - same effort, two channels covered. The relationship stays warm even when the news isn’t. Multi-location businesses lean on creative ops teams costing thousands monthly. This produces it in minutes, with variations for storefront print, Instagram, and email at no extra cost.

###### Use case 8: Marking a moment that deserves more than a caption

Situation. A founder, creator, or business owner has reached a milestone worth marking - a book launch, a podcast launch, a one-year anniversary, a major win, a re-opening, a re-brand reveal - and wants the announcement to land like a real moment, not a hurried social post. What they want to bring to life. The milestone, what it means, why it matters, and what people can do next - packaged as a single image that feels like an invitation to celebrate, not just a brag. The problem this feature solves. Big moments lose their weight when they’re announced with a stock-feeling graphic or a plain photo and caption. The audience scrolls past. The people closest to the journey don’t feel invited to celebrate. A moment that took years to earn gets twenty seconds of attention. Brand equity that should have grown stays flat. What it creates. A polished 1-page announcement with a celebratory headline - “One year in”, “Now available”, “We’re back” - a hero image at the centre, a short line of support copy capturing the meaning, a call to action like “Get the book”, “Subscribe”, or “Join us Friday”, and a warm footer thanking supporters. The design style is reviewed before rendering so colours and typography feel intentional and brand-aligned. Variations are created so it works on a feed, a story, an email header, and a printed invite. The opportunity. Supporters feel the milestone and share it like a real moment. New audiences arriving for the first time get a snapshot of a brand that pays attention to detail. Press, partners, and customers see something forward-able. The milestone earns the visibility it deserves and compounds into trust the brand can draw on for the next launch, ask, or pivot. This produces one in minutes, in any orientation, with full creative control over palette, tone, and call to action.

##### Marketing angle library

- Filling a room when the date is locked in. Lead with: A text post or email about an event is easy to skim past. Payoff: People share the flyer because it looks intentional, not improvised.
- Driving urgency around a limited-time offer. Lead with: A plain post saying “20% off this weekend” doesn’t move people. Payoff: The urgency lands.
- Getting people to register before a deadline. Lead with: A landing page link with no visual context gets ignored. Payoff: Interest converts into registrations because the next step is obvious.
- Showing what working with you actually looks like. Lead with: Long PDFs and dense email replies don’t land. Payoff: The prospect forwards it without needing to summarise it.
- Recruiting the right people for the right role. Lead with: A plain job post is one of thousands. Payoff: People share it because it looks like a brand they’d want to work for, not a job ad.
- Making sure the whole team gets the same announcement. Lead with: Long internal emails get scanned. Payoff: Everyone sees the same message.
- Telling customers something they need to know without it feeling like an email. Lead with: A wall of text in a post or a sterile email update feels cold and easy to ignore. Payoff: Customers see the change in time.
- Marking a moment that deserves more than a caption. Lead with: Big moments lose their weight when they’re announced with a stock-feeling graphic or a plain photo and caption. Payoff: Supporters feel the milestone and share it like a real moment.
- Skip the blank template. Start from the message, research, or source material rather than hunting for a layout first.
- Communication before decoration. The graphic should clarify the offer or idea, not merely look attractive.
- One message, multiple visual forms. Research and copy can become an infographic, flyer, or thumbnail in the same project.

#### AI Video Thumbnails

##### Public definition

Thumbnails creates a click-driving image - sized and styled for YouTube, blog heroes, podcast artwork, course covers, and social cards. Every Send produces a Graphics Studio with the created thumbnail(s) in it.

##### Customer problem this solves

Most non-designers know the message they need to communicate but struggle to turn it into a clear, professional visual without templates, design software, or a specialist.

##### Why this feature is valuable

This feature is valuable because it reduces manual handoffs around customer jobs such as: design a youtube video thumbnail to drive clicks, create a blog post hero image, produce podcast episode artwork. It also matters as part of a larger system: the source, conversation, or finished output can remain connected to the broader IdeoLab project instead of being treated as an isolated one-off task.

##### Why this matters in the IdeoLab experience

Canva and Adobe Express are extremely strong design platforms with templates and expanding generative AI. IdeoLab’s angle is not that templates are obsolete everywhere; it is that a business can move directly from its own research, copy, images, or project context into a created graphic without rebuilding the message in a separate design workflow. At the feature level, emphasize the workflow around the asset - source context, review, refinement, reuse, publishing, or analytics - not unsupported claims that the raw AI output is universally better.

##### Detailed use-case library

###### Use case 1: Designing a YouTube video thumbnail to drive clicks

Situation. A YouTuber, video marketer, or content team has uploaded a new video and needs a thumbnail that drives a high click-through rate. The thumbnail needs to communicate the video’s value at a glance - bold headline, strong visual hook, face or subject clearly visible. What they want to bring to life. A thumbnail that stops scrollers on the YouTube home screen - high contrast, readable title at small sizes, a face or focal object that triggers curiosity, and a design that matches the channel’s visual identity. The problem this feature solves. Good YouTube thumbnails require graphic design skill - composition, contrast, font pairing, image treatment. Most creators either use auto-generated frames or basic text-over-image thumbnails that underperform against deliberately designed competitors. What it creates. A polished thumbnail created from the video title and described visual elements, rendered in one of the 12 designs suited to the content type. The Creative Brief step lets the creator review and refine the headline and visual elements before rendering. The opportunity. Every video gets a properly designed thumbnail in under two minutes, consistently. Thumbnail quality directly correlates with YouTube click-through rate - better thumbnails mean more views from the same subscriber base. This creates each thumbnail in under two minutes.

###### Use case 2: Creating a blog post hero image

Situation. A content marketer, blogger, or editorial team publishes articles regularly and needs a compelling hero image for each post - for both the blog header and social sharing previews. They don’t have a designer on demand. What they want to bring to life. A 16:9 hero image that visually represents the article’s topic - strong enough to hold attention in a social feed, readable as a thumbnail in a content widget, and consistent with the brand’s editorial aesthetic. The problem this feature solves. Most content teams either use generic stock photos that don’t differentiate the article, or skip the hero image entirely because commissioning custom graphics is slow and expensive. Either outcome reduces click-through rates on social and email. What it creates. A custom blog hero created from the article title and topic - visually themed to the content with a headline element and supporting visual - in the European Minimalist, Bold Creator, or other design suited to the brand’s tone. The opportunity. Every article ships with a properly designed hero image on the day it’s published. Social preview cards, newsletter images, and blog headers all use the same high-quality asset. This creates a custom, branded hero in under two minutes.

###### Use case 3: Producing podcast episode artwork

Situation. A podcast producer, creator, or brand publishes episodes regularly and needs per-episode artwork for Spotify, Apple Podcasts, and promotional social posts. Episode-specific artwork drives discoverability and positions individual episodes as standalone content. What they want to bring to life. A square (1:1) thumbnail for each episode - featuring the episode title, episode number, a portrait of the host or guest, and the podcast’s colour palette - ready to upload to the podcast host and schedule on social. The problem this feature solves. Most podcast teams default to a single static cover artwork for every episode because producing individual episode thumbnails requires design time they don’t have. Platforms that surface episode-level artwork (Spotify, YouTube Music) favour channels with varied episode-specific visuals. What it creates. A square episode thumbnail created from the episode title and guest/topic description - in the Bold Creator or Dubai Luxury design for interview-style shows, or the Danger Alert or Lost Temple design for narrative/investigative shows. The opportunity. Every episode ships with its own visual identity. The artwork is used for platform uploads, Instagram carousels, LinkedIn posts, and email promotion without additional graphic production. This creates each episode’s artwork in under two minutes.

###### Use case 4: Creating a LinkedIn article cover image

Situation. A professional, executive, or B2B content team publishes LinkedIn articles and wants a cover image that positions the article as premium content and drives reads from the LinkedIn feed. What they want to bring to life. A 16:9 LinkedIn article cover that communicates the article’s professional relevance at a glance - clean design, readable title, visual authority that matches the author’s positioning in their industry. The problem this feature solves. LinkedIn articles with no cover image or generic stock photos blend into the feed. A well-designed cover image signals the article is worth reading and increases the click-through rate from profile visitors and followers. What it creates. A professional-quality cover image created from the article title and described topic - in the European Minimalist or World Map design for data-driven content, or the Dubai Luxury or Bold Creator design for leadership and personal brand content. The opportunity. Every LinkedIn article gets a polished cover that positions the author as a serious thought leader. The cover is also used as the preview image when the article is shared on other platforms. This creates each cover in under two minutes.

###### Use case 5: Creating consistent course lesson thumbnails

Situation. An online educator, course creator, or training team has a course with 10-30 lessons and needs a consistent, branded thumbnail for each lesson - for the course platform’s lesson list, YouTube, and social promotion. What they want to bring to life. A consistent thumbnail template applied to each lesson - same design, same colour palette, same layout - but with each lesson’s specific title and number. Students scanning the course outline should see a coherent, professional visual identity. The problem this feature solves. Producing 20-30 individually branded lesson thumbnails requires either design time (slow and expensive) or a Canva template workflow that still requires manual editing per lesson. Most courses default to plain text lists with no thumbnail, which looks unprofessional on modern platforms. What it creates. A batch of lesson thumbnails - each created from the lesson title and number, all using the same Design to ensure consistency - produced in a single session, one per Send. The opportunity. The course launches with a professional visual identity across all lessons. Students perceive it as higher quality than courses with no thumbnails. The design consistency reinforces brand recognition for the creator. This creates each thumbnail in under two minutes, with the same design applied consistently.

###### Use case 6: Creating thumbnail variations for A/B click-through testing

Situation. A data-driven content team or marketer wants to test multiple thumbnail concepts for the same video or article to find which drives the highest click-through rate. They need 3-4 distinct visual treatments of the same title to test simultaneously. What they want to bring to life. A set of 4 thumbnail variations - each using a different design, different visual element placement, or different headline framing - that can be A/B tested on YouTube Studio, Facebook, or an email A/B test. The problem this feature solves. Running proper A/B thumbnail tests requires producing multiple quality thumbnail variants - usually one per design cycle with a designer, which slows down testing velocity. Most teams test once or twice a year at most. What it creates. 4 thumbnail variations created in a single Send (Variations = 4) - all based on the same prompt but rendered in the selected design with natural variation in composition and element placement - ready for A/B testing. The opportunity. The team can run A/B thumbnail tests weekly rather than monthly. Learning which visual treatments drive higher CTR accelerates content performance without increasing production costs. This creates all 4 in under two minutes in a single Send.

###### Use case 7: Producing an email newsletter hero image

Situation. An email marketer, newsletter publisher, or brand wants to include a custom header image in each newsletter issue - a visual banner that communicates the issue’s theme and makes the newsletter feel like a designed publication rather than a wall of text. What they want to bring to life. A 16:9 newsletter header created from the issue’s headline theme - clean enough to render well in email clients, visually engaging enough to reward opening the email, in a design that’s consistent with the newsletter’s brand. The problem this feature solves. Most newsletters default to a static logo header because producing a custom hero for each issue takes design time. Issues with custom headers consistently show higher read-through rates and subscriber retention. What it creates. A newsletter header image created from the issue title and topic - in the European Minimalist, Pastel Packing, or Bold Creator design depending on the newsletter’s tone - sized at 16:9 for email rendering. The opportunity. Every newsletter issue has a custom header that makes the issue feel designed and worth reading. The header is also used as the social preview image when the newsletter is shared. This creates each header in under two minutes.

###### Use case 8: Designing a course platform or membership site card image

Situation. An online educator, membership site owner, or digital product creator has a collection of products - courses, guides, templates, workshops - and needs a card image for each that appears in their course platform library, Gumroad, or landing page grid. What they want to bring to life. A square (1:1) or landscape (16:9) card image for each product - consistent in style across the library but individually branded with each product’s title, so the library looks like a coherent, professional storefront. The problem this feature solves. Platform libraries and grid layouts look unprofessional when products have mismatched or absent cover images. A coherent visual library signals an established brand and increases conversion from visitors browsing the catalogue. What it creates. Card images created from each product’s title and topic - all using the same Design for library-wide consistency - produced individually in a single session, one Send per product. The opportunity. The entire product library has a consistent visual identity in under an hour. New products added to the library match the existing style without commissioning a designer. This creates each card image in under two minutes.

#### Graphics Studio

##### Public definition

Graphics Studio is the full-screen editor that opens a Studio - the organizational group that holds the items produced by a Graphics creation (infographics, flyers, or thumbnails). Inside it you can refine any item, edit multiple items together via batch select, upscale, download, copy the original prompt, and (when launched from a lab) push edited variants back to the canvas.

## 9. AI Presentations

### System-level marketing definition

IdeoLab supports two distinct presentation jobs: Concept Slides can synthesize an idea or body of source material into a deck, while Exact Slides is designed for cases where the user wants the supplied wording and structure preserved more closely. Presentation Studio supports refinement after creation.

### System-level customer problem

Presentation work mixes strategy, writing, structure, design, and formatting. The user may either need AI to help invent the story or need AI to faithfully turn an already-approved story into slides.

### System-level differentiation

Gamma, Beautiful.ai, Pitch, Canva, and Adobe Express all offer substantial AI presentation workflows. IdeoLab’s strongest differentiators are the Concept-versus-Exact distinction and the ability to build from research and source material already connected in a broader marketing workspace.

### Feature index

- Concept Slides
- Exact Slides
- Presentation Studio

#### Concept Slides

##### Public definition

Concept Slides is the AI-first slide-deck creator: you describe your topic, audience, and goal, and the AI structures and builds the complete presentation for you. Before any slide is rendered, the AI produces a per-slide Creative Brief in the chat - every slide shown as an editable block you can refine, reorder, add to, or regenerate before committing to full creation. Once you confirm the Creative Brief, the AI builds the finished visual deck slide by slide.

##### Customer problem this solves

Presentation work mixes strategy, writing, structure, design, and formatting. The user may either need AI to help invent the story or need AI to faithfully turn an already-approved story into slides.

##### Why this feature is valuable

This feature is valuable because it reduces manual handoffs around customer jobs such as: build a pitch deck from a brief, prepare a training or workshop deck, create a sales or client presentation. It also matters as part of a larger system: the source, conversation, or finished output can remain connected to the broader IdeoLab project instead of being treated as an isolated one-off task.

##### Why this matters in the IdeoLab experience

Gamma, Beautiful.ai, Pitch, Canva, and Adobe Express all offer substantial AI presentation workflows. IdeoLab’s strongest differentiators are the Concept-versus-Exact distinction and the ability to build from research and source material already connected in a broader marketing workspace. At the feature level, emphasize the workflow around the asset - source context, review, refinement, reuse, publishing, or analytics - not unsupported claims that the raw AI output is universally better.

##### Detailed use-case library

###### Use case 1: Building a pitch deck from a brief

Situation. A founder, product manager, or strategist needs to pitch a new product, service, or initiative to investors, a board, or senior leadership. They have a clear concept and key points in their head but haven’t structured a deck yet. What they want to bring to life. A compelling pitch deck - problem, solution, market, team, financials, ask - structured in a way that makes a coherent argument and looks designed, not assembled. The problem this feature solves. Building a pitch deck from scratch requires either design skills, a design tool, or a designer. The structure of a good pitch follows known patterns (problem → solution → proof → ask) but getting that right from a blank slide is time-consuming even when the content is clear. What it creates. A structured, template-styled pitch deck produced from the prompt - with the AI determining the most persuasive section order, creating content for each slide, and adding speaker notes in the chosen tone. The Creative Brief step lets the founder check the structure and revise any slide before the deck is built. The opportunity. The founder can go from concept to a presentable first draft in minutes rather than days. The Creative Brief review gives them control over the story arc before any design work happens - they can adjust the argument before it’s locked into slides. This produces a first-draft deck from a brief in under three minutes, with the Creative Brief ready to review in under 30 seconds.

###### Use case 2: Preparing a training or workshop deck

Situation. An HR manager, operations lead, trainer, or educator needs to run a workshop, onboarding session, or skills training. They know the topics to cover but need to build the supporting slide deck. What they want to bring to life. A structured training presentation with clear learning objectives, content slides covering each module, and speaker notes that guide the presenter through what to say at each stage. The problem this feature solves. Workshop decks require more structural thought than most presentations - learning objectives need to come before content, modules need to progress logically, and speaker notes need to reinforce what’s on the slide without just repeating it. Designing this from scratch takes significant time. What it creates. A training deck structured with objectives, module content, and summary slides - created in the Template and Tone appropriate for the audience (Educational tone for staff training, Inspirational for team sessions), with speaker notes that provide the spoken narrative for each slide. The opportunity. The training deck goes from a topic to a presentation-ready format in minutes. Speaker notes mean a less experienced presenter can run the session confidently without deep preparation. Updating the deck for future sessions takes seconds - just resend with a revised prompt. This produces the first draft for review in under three minutes.

###### Use case 3: Creating a sales or client presentation

Situation. A salesperson, account manager, or agency is preparing a presentation for a prospect or client - introducing the company, presenting a solution, or proposing next steps. The audience varies; the content needs to be tailored to the specific client situation. What they want to bring to life. A client-facing presentation that opens with the client’s situation, moves through the solution and proof, and closes with a clear call to action - structured persuasively and looking professional. The problem this feature solves. Sales presentations need to be tailored to each client - generic decks don’t perform. Tailoring a new deck for every prospect takes time the sales team doesn’t have. Concept Slides lets the rep describe the specific client context in the prompt and get a tailored starting point immediately. What it creates. A sales deck built from the prompt’s description of the client situation and the solution being proposed - opening with the client’s challenge, moving through the solution and benefits, and closing with next steps. Persuasive Sales tone in the speaker notes means the presenter has a ready script for each slide. The opportunity. Each prospect or client gets a presentation that feels tailored to their situation. The sales team can produce a first-draft tailored deck in minutes rather than adapting a generic template by hand. Conversion rates improve when presentations speak to the specific problem rather than the general category. A tailored 12-slide client deck takes 4-8 hours. This produces a tailored first draft in under three minutes.

###### Use case 4: Building an executive briefing or status update

Situation. A department head, project manager, or senior individual contributor needs to present a status update, quarterly review, or strategic recommendation to senior leadership. The audience is time-constrained; the deck needs to be crisp and decision-oriented. What they want to bring to life. A focused executive briefing: context, current state, key findings or decisions needed, and next steps - no more slides than necessary, language that respects the audience’s time. The problem this feature solves. Executive-level presentations have a different shape from standard presentations - less detail per slide, sharper language, clearer decision points. Getting this right requires practice and good judgement about what to include and what to cut. The AI’s Executive Briefing tone and the Creative Brief review step make it easier to get the structure right. What it creates. A concise briefing deck created from the status update context - structured with the key findings up front, supporting detail in the middle, and a clear recommendation or decision ask at the end. Executive Briefing tone speaker notes provide the precise language for each talking point. The opportunity. The presenter walks into the executive meeting with a deck that matches the audience’s expectations - without spending hours getting the structure right. The Creative Brief review lets them adjust the slide count and emphasis before it’s created, so they don’t waste creation time on slides they’ll never use. A focused 8-slide briefing takes 3-6 hours to get right. This produces the first draft for review in under a minute.

###### Use case 5: Creating educational content for a course or curriculum

Situation. A course creator, instructional designer, or subject-matter expert is building digital learning content - an online course module, a curriculum lesson, or a self-paced learning unit. They need slides that learners can follow independently, not just a presenter’s support material. What they want to bring to life. A structured lesson module with an introduction to the concept, step-by-step explanations, examples, and a summary - designed for learners who are reading and navigating the slides independently. The problem this feature solves. Instructional design for self-paced learning requires more explanation per slide than a live presentation - learners don’t have a presenter to fill the gaps. Getting the right level of depth, the right learning progression, and the right tone for independent learners requires careful writing. What it creates. A lesson module deck created from the topic and learning objective - with an intro slide, concept explanation slides, worked examples, and a summary. Educational tone speaker notes act as the embedded narration that fills the role a presenter would play in a live session. The opportunity. The course creator goes from a learning objective to a reviewable lesson structure in minutes. The Creative Brief step lets them check the learning progression - does each slide build on the last? - before committing to the full creation. The speaker notes become the voiceover script for video versions of the lesson. A fully developed 10-slide lesson module with notes takes 6-12 hours. This produces the first draft for review in under a minute.

###### Use case 6: Producing a marketing or campaign strategy deck

Situation. A marketing manager, strategist, or agency is presenting a campaign brief, channel strategy, or quarterly marketing plan to stakeholders - the brand team, a client, or the leadership team. What they want to bring to life. A strategy presentation that starts with the objective and audience insight, moves through the proposed approach and channel plan, shows expected outcomes, and closes with the timeline and budget overview. The problem this feature solves. Marketing strategy presentations are structurally complex - they need to balance big-picture framing with tactical specifics, and the right structure varies by audience (client vs. internal vs. leadership). Getting the structure right before writing the content is half the work. What it creates. A marketing strategy deck structured from the campaign brief described in the prompt - opening with the audience and objective, moving through the strategy and creative direction, and landing on measurement and timeline. The Analyst tone option produces speaker notes grounded in data and rationale rather than inspiration. The opportunity. The strategist starts the client or leadership review with a structured first draft they can pressure-test - not a blank slide deck. The Creative Brief step lets them confirm the strategy arc before slides are built. Changes to the strategy can be reflected by regenerating from a revised prompt rather than reworking individual slides. This creates a structured first-draft for review in under a minute.

###### Use case 7: Building a conference talk or keynote outline

Situation. A speaker, thought leader, or panellist is preparing a talk for a conference, webinar, or company all-hands. They have a central argument or theme and 20-40 minutes of content to fill. They need a slide structure that supports the talk without becoming a wall of text. What they want to bring to life. A presentation that opens with a hook, builds the argument through a clear narrative arc, and closes with a call to action or memorable takeaway - designed to support a live speaker, not to be read independently. The problem this feature solves. Conference talks have a narrative shape that’s different from a business presentation - they need a story arc, not just sections. Finding the right structure for a compelling talk from a topic and a time slot requires creative thinking that blocks progress before any slides are written. What it creates. A talk structure created from the central theme and audience context - hook, narrative arc, supporting points, examples, and a closing - in the Storyteller tone, with speaker notes that provide the full script for each slide. The opportunity. The speaker has a reviewable first draft to work from within minutes. The Creative Brief step is particularly valuable here - the speaker can check whether the narrative arc feels right before building any slides. Revising the story structure in the Creative Brief is fast; revising it after it’s created means rebuilding the deck. This produces the first-draft structure for review in under a minute.

###### Use case 8: Prototyping a new presentation format quickly

Situation. A creative team, product marketer, or founder needs to test several different presentation approaches for the same idea - a more narrative version, a data-led version, a short punchy version - before deciding which one to develop. Testing three different formats manually means building three decks. What they want to bring to life. Multiple first-draft versions of the same presentation, each with a different structure or angle, so the team can compare approaches and choose the strongest one before investing in polish. The problem this feature solves. Exploration is expensive when each exploration requires building a full deck. Most teams default to refining a single deck rather than comparing multiple structural approaches, which means they often miss the better version. What it creates. A first-draft deck for each structural approach - described differently in the prompt, each producing a different Creative Brief. The Creative Brief review step shows the structure before anything is built, so the team can compare structures without creating multiple full decks; they create only the one they pick. The opportunity. The team evaluates three structural approaches in 20 minutes - reviewing three Creative Briefs - instead of building three decks. The winning structure gets created; the others get discarded at the Creative Brief stage. Better creative decisions get made earlier, without the cost of building to explore.

##### Competitive context

Relevant alternatives: Gamma, Beautiful.ai, Pitch, Canva, Adobe Express. The comparison should be job-specific. A specialist competitor may be stronger for a narrow professional workflow; IdeoLab should be positioned on the connected business workflow and the ability to move from source/context into multiple finished outputs.

##### Claims to emphasize

- Explain the customer job and outcome before naming Concept Slides.
- Show how the feature contributes useful context or finished work that can support another IdeoLab marketing activity.
- Use the detailed use cases above as story material rather than defaulting to generic AI productivity claims.
- When comparing alternatives, acknowledge their real strengths and make a narrower workflow-based differentiation claim.

#### Exact Slides

##### Public definition

Exact Slides lets you write the slide-by-slide content yourself - titles, bullet points, body text, everything - then hands it to the AI to format and design into a finished slide deck. Where Concept Slides starts with a topic and walks through an AI-created Creative Brief before creating anything, Exact skips the Creative Brief step entirely and goes straight to creation. You own the content structure; the AI handles the visual presentation.

##### Customer problem this solves

Presentation work mixes strategy, writing, structure, design, and formatting. The user may either need AI to help invent the story or need AI to faithfully turn an already-approved story into slides.

##### Why this feature is valuable

This feature is valuable because it reduces manual handoffs around customer jobs such as: turn existing training documentation into a slide deck, execut a pre-planned pitch deck structure, build a lecture from prepared academic notes. It also matters as part of a larger system: the source, conversation, or finished output can remain connected to the broader IdeoLab project instead of being treated as an isolated one-off task.

##### Why this matters in the IdeoLab experience

Gamma, Beautiful.ai, Pitch, Canva, and Adobe Express all offer substantial AI presentation workflows. IdeoLab’s strongest differentiators are the Concept-versus-Exact distinction and the ability to build from research and source material already connected in a broader marketing workspace. At the feature level, emphasize the workflow around the asset - source context, review, refinement, reuse, publishing, or analytics - not unsupported claims that the raw AI output is universally better.

##### Detailed use-case library

###### Use case 1: Turning existing training documentation into a slide deck

Situation. An L&D manager, operations lead, or business owner has a written training document - a standard operating procedure, a policy guide, a skills module - that the team needs to go through in a group session. Reading a document is not the same as presenting it; they need a slide deck they can project. What they want to bring to life. A structured slide deck that follows the document’s existing sections exactly - the same headings, the same sequence, the same key points - formatted as slides ready to present, with speaker notes that follow the original language. The problem this feature solves. Converting a written document into a slide deck manually means re-entering content into a presentation tool and reformatting it. The risk is either rewriting things that should stay verbatim, or producing a deck that diverges from the approved document. Both outcomes create additional review cycles. What it creates. A slide deck where each section of the training document becomes a slide - structured the way the document is structured, with the original content preserved. Speaker notes can add narration cues without changing what’s on the slide. The opportunity. The document goes from a file people read alone to a tool a facilitator can use with a group. The training can be delivered consistently because the deck is built from the canonical source rather than someone’s interpretation of it. ##### Use case 2: Executing a pre-planned pitch deck structure

Situation. A founder, consultant, or account manager has already worked out the exact structure and talking points for a pitch or proposal - they know what each slide should say, what the order is, and what the key claims are. They don’t need AI to help them think; they need the slide deck built quickly. What they want to bring to life. The pitch deck they have in their head or in a draft document, formatted and designed without the AI reinterpreting the structure or creating alternative versions of the content. The problem this feature solves. Concept mode creates a Creative Brief that requires review and approval before creating anything - adding time and an extra step when the person already knows what they want. Presentation software requires manually building each slide in a design tool. What it creates. The exact pitch deck as planned - each slide reflecting the pre-written content, formatted visually, with the chosen template applied, ready to send or present. The opportunity. The pitch goes from notes to a complete, professional-looking deck in minutes rather than hours. Time that would have been spent in a design tool or reviewing an AI Creative Brief gets spent on rehearsal and preparation instead. ##### Use case 3: Building a lecture from prepared academic notes

Situation. A lecturer, teacher, or course instructor has detailed notes for a lesson - topic headings, key concepts, definitions, examples, and discussion questions - written in a document. They need a slide deck to project during the class. What they want to bring to life. Slides that follow the lecture plan precisely: the same headings as in the notes, the key concepts as bullet points, examples included, nothing added or reordered by the AI. The problem this feature solves. Using Concept mode would produce an AI-created interpretation of the topic, which may diverge from the course curriculum’s specific framing, vocabulary, or sequencing. Academic content is not a place for AI improvisation. What it creates. A slide deck that mirrors the lecture notes: one slide per topic or section, content matching the prepared material, speaker notes providing additional context or narration cues that can guide the spoken delivery. The opportunity. The lecture has consistent visual support that matches what the instructor has prepared to say. Students can follow along from projected slides that correspond directly to the material rather than a reinterpreted version of it. ##### Use case 4: Preparing a quarterly business review deck from known figures

Situation. A business owner, department head, or finance lead is preparing a quarterly review presentation. The numbers are known, the metrics are decided, and the key messages are clear. The task is converting that structured knowledge into a deck for the board or the team. What they want to bring to life. A QBR deck where each slide covers exactly what was planned: revenue, growth, challenges, next steps - with the figures and language exactly as written, not paraphrased or rewritten by the AI. The problem this feature solves. For a business review, the data and language need to be precise. AI-created Creative Briefs can restructure priorities or rephrase key metrics in ways that change the meaning. A QBR is not the place to review an AI draft. What it creates. A business review slide deck where each slide reflects the pre-written figures, labels, and narrative - formatted visually, template-styled, ready to present to leadership or the team. The opportunity. The review gets from spreadsheet and notes to a presentable deck without an intermediate design step. The presenter can focus on preparing the delivery rather than formatting the slides. ##### Use case 5: Converting a product demo script into a slide deck

Situation. A sales team or product marketer has a written product demo script - step-by-step, matching the exact flow of the demo - that they want to use as a slide deck to guide live demos or to send as a leave-behind after calls. What they want to bring to life. A slide deck that follows the demo script exactly: each step of the demo is a slide, the feature names are as they appear in the product, the talking points are the approved ones from the sales playbook. The problem this feature solves. The demo script is the approved content - it’s been written and reviewed. Creating a new deck from scratch or using Concept mode risks producing content that diverges from the playbook, creating inconsistency in what the team says in demos. What it creates. A slide deck that mirrors the demo script step-by-step: each slide covering one part of the demo flow, content matching the script, speaker notes providing the exact talking points the presenter should use at each stage. The opportunity. New sales reps can use the deck to guide their first demos without memorising the script. Prospects who receive the deck after a call can follow the product’s logic independently. The team presents a consistent story every time. ##### Use case 6: Repurposing a published blog post as slides

Situation. A content marketer, blogger, or business owner has a published article with clearly structured sections - an introduction, three to five main points each with their own heading, a conclusion. They want to repurpose the content as a slide deck for a webinar, a social slideshow, or a LinkedIn document post. What they want to bring to life. A slide version of the article that follows the article’s structure precisely - each section becomes a slide, the main arguments stay intact, the language is recognisably theirs. The problem this feature solves. Copy-pasting an article into Concept mode produces an AI interpretation that often rewrites the structure and the language - the output is a new piece of content, not the article in slide form. Exact mode preserves the original structure and language because the user provides it. What it creates. A slide deck repurposing the article’s content: each section as a slide, the key statements preserved, formatted visually in a template appropriate for the distribution channel. The opportunity. One piece of content becomes two: the article and the slide deck. The slide version can be distributed on LinkedIn, shared in webinars, or turned into a lead magnet without writing new content or restructuring the argument. ##### Use case 7: Creating onboarding slides from company handbook content

Situation. An HR lead, office manager, or founder is building or updating an employee onboarding presentation. The company handbook already has the content - company values, team structure, tools used, policies - but it exists as a document, not as a slide deck a new starter can be walked through. What they want to bring to life. An onboarding slide deck that reflects the handbook’s content exactly: the company values stated as written, the team structure as it actually is, the policies as approved. Nothing invented or paraphrased by the AI. The problem this feature solves. Onboarding content has been approved and needs to be accurate. Creating from a topic prompt risks the AI producing general content rather than the specific things this company actually does and believes. Exact mode ensures the slides match what’s in the handbook. What it creates. An onboarding deck where each slide corresponds to a section of the handbook - values, structure, tools, benefits, next steps - using the language and framing the company has already decided on, formatted to present to new starters. The opportunity. Every new employee gets the same onboarding experience, from the same approved content, in a format that’s easy to present and easy to follow. The deck can be reused every cohort without re-making it, and updated by editing the source content blocks when the handbook changes. ##### Use case 8: Turning a written proposal into a client presentation deck

Situation. An agency, consultant, or freelancer has written a formal proposal for a client - scope, approach, timeline, pricing, team - and needs to present the proposal in a meeting rather than just send the document. They need a deck that walks the client through the proposal in presentation format. What they want to bring to life. A slide deck that mirrors the proposal’s structure and language - the scope as written, the approach as proposed, the pricing as agreed - formatted to present, without the AI rewriting or restructuring anything. The problem this feature solves. The proposal is a legal or contractual document in some cases; the language matters. AI rewrites can shift emphasis, soften commitments, or add detail that contradicts the proposal. Exact Slides takes what’s written and formats it rather than reinterpreting it. What it creates. A proposal presentation deck where each section of the written proposal becomes a slide - scope, approach, timeline, pricing, next steps - in the client’s preferred template style, with speaker notes that guide the presenter through each section’s talking points. The opportunity. The agency or consultant presents a polished, professional deck that matches the written proposal exactly. The client sees the proposal’s logic in a format that’s easier to follow in a meeting than a long document. No discrepancy between what was written and what was presented.
