#### Presentation Studio

##### Public definition

Presentation Studio is the full-screen editor that opens from any created slide deck - whether it was created by Concept Slides or Exact Slides. It gives you three ways to work with slides after they’re created: direct text editing on any element, AI-powered element editing for targeted changes, and an AI chat panel for whole-deck revisions - plus speaker notes editing, slide deletion, version history, and export in four formats.

##### Customer problem this solves

Presentation work mixes strategy, writing, structure, design, and formatting. The user may either need AI to help invent the story or need AI to faithfully turn an already-approved story into slides.

##### Why this feature is valuable

This feature is valuable because it reduces manual handoffs around customer jobs such as: fix specific slides after it’s created, chang the tone of the whole deck, export for distribution. It also matters as part of a larger system: the source, conversation, or finished output can remain connected to the broader IdeoLab project instead of being treated as an isolated one-off task.

##### Why this matters in the IdeoLab experience

Gamma, Beautiful.ai, Pitch, Canva, and Adobe Express all offer substantial AI presentation workflows. IdeoLab’s strongest differentiators are the Concept-versus-Exact distinction and the ability to build from research and source material already connected in a broader marketing workspace. At the feature level, emphasize the workflow around the asset - source context, review, refinement, reuse, publishing, or analytics - not unsupported claims that the raw AI output is universally better.

##### Detailed use-case library

###### Use case 1: Fixing specific slides after it’s created

A deck was created and most slides are strong, but two have phrasing issues - a title that’s too long, a bullet point that’s technically incorrect. Rather than regenerating the whole deck, open Presentation Studio, double-click the offending text on each slide, and correct it directly. The fix takes seconds and leaves all other slides untouched.

###### Use case 2: Changing the tone of the whole deck

A Concept Slides deck was created with Educational tone speaker notes, but the use changed - it’s now going to a senior leadership audience. In the AI chat panel, instruct: “Rewrite all speaker notes in a concise, executive briefing style - no more than three sentences per slide.” The AI rewrites all notes in one pass. A checkpoint is saved automatically so the original version can be restored if needed.

###### Use case 3: Exporting for distribution

The deck is ready to send. The recipient is a client who works in PowerPoint. Open the Download menu, select PowerPoint (PPTX), and the file downloads. For a version the client can edit directly, use PowerPoint Editable instead. For attaching to an email where layout fidelity matters, PDF is the right choice.

###### Use case 4: Updating a slide for a new audience

A slide deck built for one market needs to be adapted for another. Open Presentation Studio, use the AI chat panel: “Update slide 4 to reference the European market instead of North America, and adjust the statistics accordingly.” The AI edits just the relevant slide. The rest of the deck is unchanged.

###### Use case 5: Restoring a previous version after a bad AI edit

An AI chat instruction produced unexpected changes across several slides. Rather than undoing each change manually, open Version History, find the checkpoint created just before the AI edit (labelled automatically), and restore it. The deck returns to its pre-edit state in seconds.

##### Marketing angle library

- Fixing specific slides after it’s created.
- Changing the tone of the whole deck.
- Exporting for distribution.
- Updating a slide for a new audience.
- Restoring a previous version after a bad AI edit.
- Invent the story or preserve the story. Use the Concept-versus-Exact distinction to explain two fundamentally different presentation jobs.
- The deck should start from the source of truth. Build from research, documents, and project context instead of a blank prompt.
- Stop formatting what is already approved. Exact Slides is a strong angle when the content is final and the bottleneck is design.

## 10. AI Mindmaps

### System-level marketing definition

IdeoLab turns a topic, plan, or connected body of context into a structured visual map that can be expanded and refined rather than remaining trapped in linear notes.

### System-level customer problem

Complex plans and ideas are difficult to understand when they live only as paragraphs, chat transcripts, or disconnected notes.

### System-level differentiation

Miro is a far broader visual collaboration and diagramming platform. IdeoLab’s angle is the continuity between research, AI reasoning, mindmap creation, and the other marketing outputs that can follow from the same project.

### Feature index

- AI Mindmaps
- Mindmap Studio

#### AI Mindmaps

##### Public definition

Mindmap turns any topic, information set, or question into a visual mind map - a branching diagram that organises ideas hierarchically and shows how concepts connect. Describe the subject you want mapped, and the AI structures it into a navigable mind map with labeled cards. Every Send produces a Mindmap Studio holding the created map, ready to edit, expand, and export.

##### Customer problem this solves

Complex plans and ideas are difficult to understand when they live only as paragraphs, chat transcripts, or disconnected notes.

##### Why this feature is valuable

This feature is valuable because it reduces manual handoffs around customer jobs such as: brainstorm a new business idea, plan a content strategy, break down a complex project into tasks. It also matters as part of a larger system: the source, conversation, or finished output can remain connected to the broader IdeoLab project instead of being treated as an isolated one-off task.

##### Why this matters in the IdeoLab experience

Miro is a far broader visual collaboration and diagramming platform. IdeoLab’s angle is the continuity between research, AI reasoning, mindmap creation, and the other marketing outputs that can follow from the same project. At the feature level, emphasize the workflow around the asset - source context, review, refinement, reuse, publishing, or analytics - not unsupported claims that the raw AI output is universally better.

##### Detailed use-case library

###### Use case 1: Brainstorming a new business idea

Situation. A founder, entrepreneur, or business owner has an idea for a new product, service, or venture and wants to explore it systematically - mapping out the opportunity, the target market, the competition, the business model, and the risks - before investing time in a formal plan. What they want to bring to life. A structured mind map that captures all the dimensions of the idea in one view - so they can see what’s well-developed, what’s under-explored, and where the biggest gaps are. The problem this feature solves. Brainstorming on a blank page is unfocused. Most idea exploration happens in scattered notes, conversations, or linear documents. A mind map forces the exploration to be comprehensive and shows how the parts connect - but drawing one from scratch is time-consuming. What it creates. A mind map created from the business idea description - branching across the AI’s proposed structure (market, product, revenue model, risks, etc.) with cards populated from the idea as described. The structure selection step lets the founder choose whether to map comprehensively or focus on a specific angle. The opportunity. The founder finishes the brainstorming session with a visual overview of the idea that’s shareable, editable, and complete enough to identify the most important unknowns to research next. This produces the initial structured map in under two minutes, leaving human time for the judgment calls.

###### Use case 2: Planning a content strategy

Situation. A content strategist, marketing manager, or solo creator needs to map out a content strategy - topic clusters, content types, channels, audience segments, publishing cadence - so the team or creator has a single reference view of how everything connects. What they want to bring to life. A content strategy map with the central goal at the core and all the supporting elements branching out - topic pillars, content formats per pillar, target audience segments, distribution channels, and measurement metrics. The problem this feature solves. Content strategies are usually documented in spreadsheets or slide decks - both formats that hide the relationships between elements. A mind map shows the whole strategy in one view and makes it easy to see gaps: a topic pillar with no channel strategy, an audience segment with no assigned content. What it creates. A content strategy mind map created from the described strategy - with branches for topic pillars, formats, channels, and metrics - in the hierarchical or funnel structure the AI proposes. The selection step lets the strategist choose whether to map by topic, by audience, or by channel. The opportunity. The content team has a shared visual reference for the strategy that can be opened in Mindmap Studio, expanded with new topics, and shared with stakeholders who wouldn’t read a strategy doc. A shared mind map that captures the same relationships takes two minutes to create and is editable by anyone on the team.

###### Use case 3: Breaking down a complex project into tasks

Situation. A project manager, team lead, or founder has a complex project that needs to be decomposed into phases, workstreams, tasks, and owners before it can be planned. The structure isn’t obvious and needs to be explored before committing to a project management tool. What they want to bring to life. A project breakdown map - phases at the first level, workstreams at the second, tasks at the third - so the project’s full scope is visible before it’s entered into Notion, Jira, or a Gantt chart. The problem this feature solves. Starting in a project management tool before the structure is clear leads to constant reorganisation. Mapping the project in a mind map first produces a clearer, more complete structure - and the map can be used to review the scope with stakeholders before any tool work is done. What it creates. A project breakdown mind map created from the project description - phases, workstreams, and task buckets branching in the AI’s proposed structure - with enough detail at each level to start assigning owners and estimates. The opportunity. The team enters project planning with a complete, reviewed scope rather than discovering missing workstreams halfway through. The map is exported as PDF for stakeholder review before moving to the project management tool. This creates the initial structure in under two minutes, leaving human time for refinement and sign-off.

###### Use case 4: Structuring a course curriculum

Situation. An online educator, trainer, or L&D professional is designing a new course and needs to map the curriculum - modules, lessons per module, learning objectives, exercises - before writing any content. What they want to bring to life. A course curriculum map with the learning outcome at the core, modules branching from it, and lessons within each module - structured so the progression from foundational to advanced is clear before a single lesson is written. The problem this feature solves. Course outlines in documents or spreadsheets are linear - they don’t show the dependencies between lessons or whether the curriculum covers the topic comprehensively. A mind map shows the full structure in one view and makes it easy to spot a module that’s too thin or a topic that’s been missed. What it creates. A curriculum map created from the course topic and level - with the AI’s proposed structure (module-by-module, or learning-outcome-by-outcome) - populated with lesson topics and objectives. The selection step lets the educator choose the structure that best fits their course design approach. The opportunity. The course has a complete, visualised curriculum before any content is written. The map is shared with a co-creator or L&D reviewer for feedback before writing begins. This creates the initial structure in under two minutes.

###### Use case 5: Mapping a competitive landscape for a strategy session

Situation. A strategist, founder, or marketing lead is preparing for a strategy session and wants to map the competitive landscape - key players, their positioning, strengths, weaknesses, and the gaps in the market - as a shared reference for the team. What they want to bring to life. A competitive landscape map with competitors at the first level, their key attributes (positioning, pricing, target market, strengths, weaknesses) at the second level - organised so patterns and white-space opportunities are visible at a glance. The problem this feature solves. Competitive analysis is usually presented as a table or a list. Tables show data but don’t reveal relationships - which competitors cluster together, which positions are crowded, which are under-served. A mind map shows the landscape as a spatial structure. What it creates. A competitive landscape map created from the described market and competitors - with the AI’s proposed structure (competitor-by-competitor, or attribute-by-attribute) - populated with the competitive details described in the prompt. The opportunity. The strategy session starts with a shared visual map of the competitive landscape rather than a deck of slides. The map can be edited live in the session as new observations arise. This creates a structured map in under two minutes, ready to be reviewed and refined in the session.

###### Use case 6: Explaining a complex concept to a team or audience

Situation. A teacher, trainer, manager, or communicator needs to explain a complex concept - a regulatory framework, a technical architecture, a historical event, a scientific system - to an audience that isn’t already familiar with it. What they want to bring to life. A concept map that breaks the complex topic into digestible components, shows how they relate, and provides a navigable visual that the audience can explore at their own pace. The problem this feature solves. Complex concepts presented as linear text or slides force the audience to build the structure in their heads as they read. A mind map gives the structure visually, reducing cognitive load and making relationships between components visible without explanation. What it creates. A concept map created from the topic description - with the AI’s proposed structure (top-down hierarchical, or relational groupings) - populated with the key components and their relationships. Notes can be enabled to add explanatory text to each card. The opportunity. The trainer or teacher has a shareable visual reference that complements their presentation. Learners use the exported PDF as a study aid after the session. This creates a complete concept map in under two minutes.

###### Use case 7: Outlining a research report or thesis

Situation. A researcher, student, analyst, or writer is starting a large document - a research report, a thesis chapter, a white paper - and needs to map the argument structure and evidence organisation before writing. What they want to bring to life. A report outline map with the central argument or research question at the core, main sections branching from it, and sub-points and evidence buckets at the next level - giving a complete view of the document’s architecture before writing begins. The problem this feature solves. Starting a large document from a blank page produces meandering structure. Mapping the outline first produces a cleaner argument structure and makes it obvious where the evidence is thin or the logic doesn’t flow. What it creates. A report outline map created from the research topic and argument - with the AI’s proposed structure (argument-led, or section-led) - populated with main sections and key points. The structure selection step lets the researcher choose whether to organise by argument, by theme, or by methodology. The opportunity. The writer starts drafting with a complete, visualised outline rather than discovering structural problems midway through the document. The map is exported as PDF for a supervisor or editor to review before writing begins. This creates the initial outline structure in under two minutes.

###### Use case 8: Building an onboarding knowledge map for new team members

Situation. A team lead, HR manager, or operations owner wants to give new team members a visual overview of how the business, team, or role works - the systems, the processes, the key contacts, the tools - so they can orient themselves quickly without reading through dozens of documents. What they want to bring to life. An onboarding knowledge map - the team’s key systems and processes at the first level, how they connect at the second level, and who owns what at the third level - that gives a new joiner a navigable overview on day one. The problem this feature solves. Onboarding documents are linear and long. New team members read them once and remember very little. A mind map that a new joiner can explore interactively is easier to navigate and more memorable than a document. What it creates. An onboarding map created from the team/role description - with the AI’s proposed structure (domain-by-domain, or task-by-task) - populated with the key areas described in the prompt. Notes enabled to add context to each card. The opportunity. New team members arrive with a visual reference they can explore and return to throughout their first weeks. The map is updated in Mindmap Studio as processes change, and shared with each new joiner without needing to rewrite onboarding docs. This creates the structural map in under two minutes, with only the specific details needing human review and editing.

#### Mindmap Studio

##### Public definition

Mindmap Studio is the full-screen editor that opens from any created mind map - created by the Mindmap Skill. It gives you full control over the map’s structure and content: add, delete, and rearrange cards; edit labels and colours; add notes to individual cards; and use the AI chat panel to make wholesale changes across the map. Export as PNG or PDF when it’s ready to share.

##### Customer problem this solves

Complex plans and ideas are difficult to understand when they live only as paragraphs, chat transcripts, or disconnected notes.

##### Why this feature is valuable

This feature is valuable because it reduces manual handoffs around customer jobs such as: expand an ai-created map with custom branches, colour-cod a map by department or category, using AI chat to expand a specific branch. It also matters as part of a larger system: the source, conversation, or finished output can remain connected to the broader IdeoLab project instead of being treated as an isolated one-off task.

##### Why this matters in the IdeoLab experience

Miro is a far broader visual collaboration and diagramming platform. IdeoLab’s angle is the continuity between research, AI reasoning, mindmap creation, and the other marketing outputs that can follow from the same project. At the feature level, emphasize the workflow around the asset - source context, review, refinement, reuse, publishing, or analytics - not unsupported claims that the raw AI output is universally better.

##### Detailed use-case library

###### Use case 1: Expanding an AI-created map with custom branches

The AI created a competitive landscape map with the main competitors as branches. One competitor has three products the team needs to track, but the created map only shows the company name. Open Mindmap Studio, select the competitor card, and add child cards for each product. Type the product names directly. Save the map.

###### Use case 2: Colour-coding a map by department or category

A project breakdown map was created with phases and workstreams. The team wants to colour-code by department: engineering cards in blue, design cards in purple, marketing cards in orange. Multi-select each department’s cards (Shift+Click), then apply the department colour in one step. Save and export as PNG for the project kickoff presentation.

###### Use case 3: Using AI chat to expand a specific branch

A strategy map was created for the top level. One branch - “Distribution Channels” - is sparse. Open AI chat and instruct: “Expand the Distribution Channels branch to include Direct Sales, Partnerships, E-commerce, and Resellers, with 2-3 sub-cards under each.” The AI adds the full expansion. Review the changes and save.

###### Use case 4: Exporting a map for a presentation or report

A research outline map is ready to share with a supervisor for review. Export as PDF - the map renders at full quality for printing and attachment. For including a visual in a slide deck, export as PNG instead, then drag it directly into the presentation.

###### Use case 5: Restoring a previous version after an unwanted change

An AI chat instruction restructured the map in an unexpected way - several branches were merged when they should have stayed separate. Rather than undoing each change manually, open Version History, find the checkpoint created just before the AI edit (labelled automatically), and restore it. The map returns to its pre-edit state.

## 11. AI Sites & Lead Pages

### System-level marketing definition

IdeoLab creates purpose-built marketing sites for lead capture, events, bookings, and broader brand presence, with post-generation editing and publishing controls in Site Studio.

### System-level customer problem

A business often needs a page for a specific commercial job quickly, but traditional website workflows start with themes, blank canvases, or general-purpose builders rather than the conversion purpose itself.

### System-level differentiation

Framer and Wix now pair powerful AI generation with professional site systems, analytics, SEO, and publishing. IdeoLab should not position as categorically more capable. Its marketing advantage is the connection between research, campaign assets, social content, lead-page creation, and automation inside one business workspace, plus purpose-specific starting workflows.

### Feature index

- Free Offer Lead Sites
- Event Registration Sites
- Booking Sites
- Brand Sites
- Site Studio

#### Free Offer Lead Sites

##### Public definition

Free Offer is a single-page lead-magnet site: it offers something of value - a downloadable guide, a checklist, a discount code, early access, or a free tool - in exchange for an email or contact details. It’s the flagship lead-capture page type in the AI Sites Assistant, and the AI builds a focused page whose single goal is the opt-in. Every section the AI lays down is in service of one conversion: getting the visitor to hand over their email to claim the offer.

##### Customer problem this solves

A business often needs a page for a specific commercial job quickly, but traditional website workflows start with themes, blank canvases, or general-purpose builders rather than the conversion purpose itself.

##### Why this feature is valuable

This feature is valuable because it reduces manual handoffs around customer jobs such as: turn a free guide into an email list, capture demand before a product launch, a first-visit discount for a local business. It also matters as part of a larger system: the source, conversation, or finished output can remain connected to the broader IdeoLab project instead of being treated as an isolated one-off task.

##### Why this matters in the IdeoLab experience

Framer and Wix now pair powerful AI generation with professional site systems, analytics, SEO, and publishing. IdeoLab should not position as categorically more capable. Its marketing advantage is the connection between research, campaign assets, social content, lead-page creation, and automation inside one business workspace, plus purpose-specific starting workflows. At the feature level, emphasize the workflow around the asset - source context, review, refinement, reuse, publishing, or analytics - not unsupported claims that the raw AI output is universally better.

##### Detailed use-case library

###### Use case 1: Turning a free guide into an email list

Situation. A coach, consultant, or course creator has expertise to give away - a how-to guide, a playbook, a starter kit - and wants to convert anonymous visitors into named leads they can nurture toward a paid offer. What they want to create. A clean opt-in page that promises the guide, makes the value obvious, and captures an email before the download is handed over. The problem this feature solves. The expertise exists, but there’s nowhere to send people. Building a dedicated lead-capture page in a generic builder means designing a hero, writing benefit copy, and wiring up a form from scratch - work that stalls before the list ever grows. What it enables. A focused page with the offer named in the hero, the benefits framed underneath, an email form, and a single clear CTA - built from one prompt and ready to publish to ideolabsites.ai or a custom domain. The opportunity. Every visitor who wants the guide becomes a known contact in the site’s Contacts, the start of a relationship instead of a one-time pageview. The list compounds while the creator sleeps.

###### Use case 2: Capturing demand before a product launch

Situation. A product or SaaS team is weeks away from launch and wants to build a waitlist so there’s an audience ready on day one - and so they can gauge real interest before they ship. What they want to create. A “be the first to know” opt-in page that offers early access or a launch-day discount in exchange for an email. The problem this feature solves. Launch energy gets wasted if there’s no list to launch to. Standing up a waitlist page usually competes with the engineering work of the launch itself, so it gets skipped or rushed. What it enables. A single page that frames the upcoming product, promises early access, and captures emails through the form - with social-proof framing (early-interest count, founder note) to add momentum. The opportunity. A warm list is waiting when the doors open, and the signup numbers become an early signal of which positioning lands before a dollar is spent on the build.

###### Use case 3: A first-visit discount for a local business

Situation. A salon, gym, restaurant, or clinic wants more first-time customers through the door and is willing to give a first-visit discount to anyone who signs up. What they want to create. A simple page that offers the discount code or voucher and captures an email or phone number to send it to. The problem this feature solves. Foot traffic is invisible - people walk past, scroll past, and leave no trace. There’s no list to remind them to come back, and no easy way to attach a name to the offer. What it enables. A mobile-friendly opt-in page promising “10% off your first visit,” with the form capturing contact details and the code delivered in exchange - a destination for the business’s social bio, flyers, or local ads. The opportunity. Walk-bys and ad clicks turn into a contact list the business can market to for every future promotion, holiday, or slow Tuesday. ##### Use case 4: Gating a calculator, template, or tool

Situation. A B2B company or creator has built something genuinely useful - a pricing calculator, a budget template, a Notion system, a swipe file - and wants to use it to attract qualified leads. What they want to create. An opt-in page that describes the tool, shows a preview of what it does, and unlocks access once the visitor gives their email. The problem this feature solves. A great free tool sitting ungated gets used and forgotten - no lead, no follow-up. But hand-building a gate-and-deliver page is fiddly and rarely worth a developer’s time. What it enables. A page that frames the tool’s payoff, adds proof that others use it, and captures the email through the form before granting access - purpose-built around the single conversion. The opportunity. Each person who values the tool enough to claim it self-identifies as a qualified lead, handing the business a contact already primed by a useful first experience.

###### Use case 5: A high-converting destination for paid traffic

Situation. A marketer or agency is about to run paid ads and needs a dedicated landing page to send clicks to - sending paid traffic to a busy homepage burns budget on people who bounce. What they want to create. A single-focus opt-in page with one offer, one message, and one form - no navigation, no distractions, matched to the ad’s promise. The problem this feature solves. Generic pages leak attention: every menu link and competing CTA is an exit. Paid clicks are expensive, and a distracted page wastes them. What it enables. A deliberately narrow page - hero offer, benefits, proof, a single repeated CTA into the form - exactly the shape a high-converting lead-magnet page should take, ready to publish to a custom domain that matches the campaign. The opportunity. A higher share of paid clicks convert into captured leads, which lowers cost-per-lead and makes the whole campaign math work.

###### Use case 6: Growing a newsletter or content audience

Situation. A writer, creator, or media brand wants to grow a newsletter and needs a dedicated signup page that sells the subscription better than a sidebar form ever could. What they want to create. A page that frames what the newsletter delivers, who it’s for, and what the reader gets - with a single email field to subscribe. The problem this feature solves. Inline signup forms on a blog convert poorly because they ask for attention while the reader is busy elsewhere. A standalone page that makes the case for subscribing rarely exists because it’s tedious to build. What it enables. A focused subscribe page with a benefit-led hero, sample value or a testimonial as proof, and the form as the only action - a link the creator can share everywhere their audience already is. The opportunity. Subscriptions climb because the page does the persuading, turning casual readers into a committed audience the creator owns outside any algorithm. ##### Use case 7: A webinar or workshop registration magnet

Situation. A trainer, B2B marketer, or community lead is running a free webinar or workshop and needs registrations - names and emails so they can send the link, the reminders, and the replay. What they want to create. A registration page that sells the session - the topic, the takeaways, the host - and captures the registrant’s email through the form. The problem this feature solves. Promoting an event with just a calendar link captures no contact and no chance to follow up. Building a real registration page in time for the promo window is a scramble. What it enables. A single page framing the session’s value, adding the host’s credibility as proof, and capturing registrations into Contacts - the destination for every promotional post and email about the event. The opportunity. Registrants become a contact list the host keeps long after the session ends, ready for the replay, the offer, and the next event.

###### Use case 8: Validating an idea before building it

Situation. A founder or solopreneur has an idea for a product, course, or service and wants to test whether anyone actually wants it before investing months building it. What they want to create. A simple opt-in page that describes the not-yet-built offer and captures emails from people who’d want it - a “smoke test” landing page. The problem this feature solves. Building first and validating later is the expensive way around. But spinning up a test page feels like its own project, so the idea either gets built on faith or never tested at all. What it enables. A believable page that frames the concept, promises early access, and captures interested emails through the form - fast enough to launch the same day the idea strikes. The opportunity. Real signups (or the lack of them) give an honest read on demand before a line of product gets built, and the people who did sign up become the first audience if it’s a go.

#### Event Registration Sites

##### Public definition

Event is a single-page event registration site - a focused page whose one job is to turn interest in an event into a confirmed sign-up. You describe the event in plain language and the AI builds a page tuned to drive RSVPs: a webinar, a product launch, a conference, a workshop, a fundraiser, or a local meetup.

##### Customer problem this solves

A business often needs a page for a specific commercial job quickly, but traditional website workflows start with themes, blank canvases, or general-purpose builders rather than the conversion purpose itself.

##### Why this feature is valuable

This feature is valuable because it reduces manual handoffs around customer jobs such as: fill a webinar before the doors even open, build launch-day anticipation instead of hoping people show, sell out a paid workshop with limited seats. It also matters as part of a larger system: the source, conversation, or finished output can remain connected to the broader IdeoLab project instead of being treated as an isolated one-off task.

##### Why this matters in the IdeoLab experience

Framer and Wix now pair powerful AI generation with professional site systems, analytics, SEO, and publishing. IdeoLab should not position as categorically more capable. Its marketing advantage is the connection between research, campaign assets, social content, lead-page creation, and automation inside one business workspace, plus purpose-specific starting workflows. At the feature level, emphasize the workflow around the asset - source context, review, refinement, reuse, publishing, or analytics - not unsupported claims that the raw AI output is universally better.

##### Detailed use-case library

###### Use case 1: Filling a webinar before the doors even open

Situation. A SaaS marketer or coach has a live webinar scheduled in two weeks and needs people to register, not just to “be aware” of it - but the only thing they have is a calendar date and a link to nowhere. What they want to create. A registration page with the talk’s title, the date and time, a countdown to the start, a short “what you’ll learn” list, and a sign-up form that books the attendee in. The problem this feature solves. A webinar with no dedicated page leaks every interested person who can’t immediately act - there’s nowhere to send them, no way to capture their email, and no sense of urgency to register now rather than “later” (which means never). What it enables. A focused page where the countdown makes the deadline real, the agenda makes the hour worth showing up for, and the form captures the email so the organiser can send the join link and the reminders. The opportunity. More of the people who hear about the webinar actually register, the registration list becomes a warm email audience the brand owns, and attendance climbs because the reminders go somewhere real.

###### Use case 2: Building launch-day anticipation instead of hoping people show

Situation. A founder is launching a new product on a fixed date and wants a crowd watching live - but a launch announced only in a tweet and an email evaporates the moment the feed scrolls. What they want to create. An RSVP page for the launch event: the product name, the reveal date, a countdown that builds tension, a teaser of what’s coming, and a “Reserve My Spot” form so people commit to attending. The problem this feature solves. Excitement with nowhere to land dissipates. Without a page that captures the RSVP, the founder reaches launch day with no idea who’s coming and no list to remind - the audience they built has no door to walk through. What it enables. A single anticipation-building page where the countdown does the marketing work between now and launch, and the form turns “I’m interested” into a captured RSVP the founder can email on the day. The opportunity. Launch day opens to a room that already registered and got reminded, the RSVP list becomes the launch-day audience, and the countdown keeps the event top-of-mind for everyone who signed up early.

###### Use case 3: Selling out a paid workshop with limited seats

Situation. A consultant or studio owner is running a hands-on workshop with a hard cap - say 20 seats - and needs the scarcity to do the selling without manually tracking who’s in. What they want to create. A registration page that states what the workshop covers, when and where it runs, who it’s for, and a sign-up form - framed so the limited-seat urgency pushes people to register now. The problem this feature solves. A workshop pitched over DMs and email threads is chaos - there’s no single source of truth for who’s registered, the “limited seats” pressure never lands without a page to anchor it, and qualified people drop off in the gaps. What it enables. A clean page where the agenda and outcomes justify the seat, the countdown to the workshop date adds urgency, and every sign-up is captured in one list instead of scattered across an inbox. The opportunity. Seats fill faster because the scarcity is visible and the sign-up is one tap, the organiser has a single tidy roster in Contacts, and the page is reusable for the next cohort with a date change. ##### Use case 4: Driving conference or summit registrations at scale

Situation. An event team is running a multi-session conference (virtual or in-person) and needs a high-converting registration page that handles a wider audience than a single-speaker webinar. What they want to create. A registration page with the event name and dates, a countdown, agenda highlights and featured speakers, a social-proof line showing registration momentum, and a prominent registration form. The problem this feature solves. A conference is a bigger sell than a webinar - people need to see who’s speaking and what the days hold before they commit, and a thin page that just says “register here” can’t carry that case. Building a proper one usually means a developer and a designer. What it enables. A page that does the persuading - agenda highlights and speakers prove the lineup is worth the time, the “X people registered this week” line signals it’s filling up, and the form captures the registration the moment someone’s convinced. The opportunity. Registration volume rises because the page sells the lineup and the momentum, the team owns the full registrant list for reminders and follow-up, and updates (a new speaker, a fresh agenda slot) take seconds in the editor.

###### Use case 5: Rallying donors to a fundraiser

Situation. A non-profit or community group is hosting a fundraising event - a gala, a charity run, a benefit night - and needs supporters to register and put the date in their calendar. What they want to create. A registration page that tells the cause’s story, shows the date, time, and venue, counts down to the event, and offers a sign-up form so supporters reserve their place. The problem this feature solves. Fundraisers live or die on turnout, and a cause shared only across social posts and group chats has no place to convert a supporter’s good intention into a confirmed, reminder-able registration. Small organisations rarely have a budget for a web team. What it enables. A heartfelt, on-brand page where the mission justifies attending, the countdown keeps the date alive, and the form captures every supporter so the organiser can confirm numbers and send reminders. The opportunity. More supporters register and actually attend, the organiser walks into the event knowing the headcount, and the registration list seeds the relationship for future appeals. ##### Use case 6: Filling a local meetup without a marketing budget

Situation. A community organiser or small-business owner is hosting a local meetup - a networking night, a class, a pop-up - and needs neighbours and regulars to sign up so they can plan numbers. What they want to create. A simple, friendly registration page: the meetup name, the where and when, a line on what to expect, a countdown, and a sign-up form so people RSVP. The problem this feature solves. A meetup promoted only by word of mouth and a flyer gives the organiser no headcount and no way to remind anyone - people mean to come and then forget, and the turnout is a coin flip. What it enables. A shareable page the organiser can drop in a local group or a story, where the form turns a casual “maybe” into a captured RSVP and the countdown nudges people as the night approaches. The opportunity. The organiser knows roughly how many are coming and can cater and set up accordingly, attendees get reminders instead of forgetting, and the same page template serves every future meetup. ##### Use case 7: Standing up a registration page for a client, fast

Situation. An agency or freelancer has a client running an event next week and needs a registration page live now - branded to the client, on the client’s domain, without burning a developer’s hours. What they want to create. A client-branded event page - name, date, countdown, agenda, social proof, registration form - that looks bespoke and captures sign-ups the client can act on. The problem this feature solves. Spinning up a custom event page per client is slow and unprofitable on a tight timeline; the dev queue is full, and the client needs it yesterday. Turning the work down loses the relationship. What it enables. A polished registration page built from a prompt in minutes, refined in the editor to match the client’s brand, with sign-ups landing in Contacts for handoff - published to the client’s own domain. The opportunity. The agency delivers same-day on a job that used to take a week, takes on event work that wasn’t worth quoting before, and hands the client a live page and a registrant list as a clean deliverable.

###### Use case 8: Re-running a recurring event without rebuilding it

Situation. A creator or business runs the same event on a cadence - a monthly webinar, a quarterly workshop, a recurring community call - and re-creating the registration page each time is dead-weight work. What they want to create. A registration page they can stand up once and refresh for each new edition - same structure, new date, new countdown, new agenda - every cycle capturing a fresh batch of sign-ups. The problem this feature solves. Building a page from scratch for every recurrence is a tax on a busy calendar, and skipping the page means losing the registration list and the reminders that drive attendance for that edition. What it enables. A reusable event-page pattern: refresh the date and details in the editor, the countdown re-anchors to the new start, and each edition’s registrations collect cleanly in Contacts - no rebuild required. The opportunity. Every recurrence gets a proper registration page and its own captured audience with minimal effort, attendance stays consistent because the reminders always have somewhere to come from, and the time saved compounds across the year.

Payoff: More of the people who hear about the webinar actually register, the registration list becomes a warm email audience the brand owns, and attendance climbs because the reminders go somewhere real.

Payoff: Seats fill faster because the scarcity is visible and the sign-up is one tap, the organiser has a single tidy roster in Contacts, and the page is reusable for the next cohort with a date change.
- Driving conference or summit registrations at scale. Lead with: A conference is a bigger sell than a webinar - people need to see who’s speaking and what the days hold before they commit, and a thin page that just says “register here” can’t carry that case. Payoff: Registration volume rises because the page sells the lineup and the momentum, the team owns the full registrant list for reminders and follow-up, and updates (a new speaker, a fresh agenda slot) take seconds in the editor.
- Rallying donors to a fundraiser. Lead with: Fundraisers live or die on turnout, and a cause shared only across social posts and group chats has no place to convert a supporter’s good intention into a confirmed, reminder-able registration. Payoff: More supporters register and actually attend, the organiser walks into the event knowing the headcount, and the registration list seeds the relationship for future appeals.
- Filling a local meetup without a marketing budget. Lead with: A meetup promoted only by word of mouth and a flyer gives the organiser no headcount and no way to remind anyone - people mean to come and then forget, and the turnout is a coin flip. Payoff: The organiser knows roughly how many are coming and can cater and set up accordingly, attendees get reminders instead of forgetting, and the same page template serves every future meetup.
- Standing up a registration page for a client, fast. Lead with: Spinning up a custom event page per client is slow and unprofitable on a tight timeline; the dev queue is full, and the client needs it yesterday. Payoff: The agency delivers same-day on a job that used to take a week, takes on event work that wasn’t worth quoting before, and hands the client a live page and a registrant list as a clean deliverable.
- Re-running a recurring event without rebuilding it. Lead with: Building a page from scratch for every recurrence is a tax on a busy calendar, and skipping the page means losing the registration list and the reminders that drive attendance for that edition. Payoff: Every recurrence gets a proper registration page and its own captured audience with minimal effort, attendance stays consistent because the reminders always have somewhere to come from, and the time saved compounds across the year.
- Start with the commercial job, not the template. Free offer, event, booking, and brand sites begin from purpose.
- Campaign continuity. The same research and messaging can feed the page, graphics, social content, and automation.
- From draft to published site in one production environment. Creation and refinement stay connected.

#### Booking Sites

##### Public definition

Booking is a single-page booking / appointment site - it presents one service clearly and captures a booking request or appointment enquiry through a form. Where a Free Offer trades something for an email and an Event drives sign-ups to a dated happening, a Booking page has one job: turn an interested visitor into a request for someone’s time. The whole page is built around a single, unmistakable “Book” or “Request appointment” call to action.

##### Customer problem this solves

A business often needs a page for a specific commercial job quickly, but traditional website workflows start with themes, blank canvases, or general-purpose builders rather than the conversion purpose itself.

##### Why this feature is valuable

This feature is valuable because it reduces manual handoffs around customer jobs such as: turn a coach’s discovery call into a real pipeline, let a salon take appointment requests after hours, give a clinic a clean intake for appointment enquiries. It also matters as part of a larger system: the source, conversation, or finished output can remain connected to the broader IdeoLab project instead of being treated as an isolated one-off task.

##### Why this matters in the IdeoLab experience

Framer and Wix now pair powerful AI generation with professional site systems, analytics, SEO, and publishing. IdeoLab should not position as categorically more capable. Its marketing advantage is the connection between research, campaign assets, social content, lead-page creation, and automation inside one business workspace, plus purpose-specific starting workflows. At the feature level, emphasize the workflow around the asset - source context, review, refinement, reuse, publishing, or analytics - not unsupported claims that the raw AI output is universally better.

##### Detailed use-case library

###### Use case 1: Turning a coach’s discovery call into a real pipeline

Situation. A coach or consultant relies on free discovery calls to win clients, but currently shares a calendar link buried in DMs and email replies, with nothing that explains who they are or why the call is worth taking. What they want to create. A single page that frames the discovery call, establishes credibility, and captures the request - name, goal, and preferred time - so the right people book and arrive prepared. The problem this feature solves. A bare scheduling link converts curiosity into a no-show. Prospects don’t understand the value of the call, so they ghost, or they book without context and waste the slot. The coach burns hours on calls that were never going to convert. What it enables. A focused booking page that sells the call before the form - the outcome they’ll leave with, the credentials behind it, a testimonial or two - then captures the enquiry with a short note field so the coach walks in already knowing the prospect’s situation. The opportunity. Better-qualified people book, fewer ghost, and the coach spends call time closing instead of explaining. The page becomes the single link they share everywhere - bio, email signature, posts.

###### Use case 2: Letting a salon take appointment requests after hours

Situation. A salon or barbershop fields most of its bookings by phone, which means missed calls during a haircut, voicemails nobody returns, and lost appointments every evening and weekend the shop is closed. What they want to create. A simple appointment-request page they can link from Instagram and Google so clients can ask for a slot any time, picking the service and a preferred day without needing anyone to pick up the phone. The problem this feature solves. Phone-only booking caps the business at the hours someone can answer. Every unanswered call is a client who books the salon down the street instead. What it enables. A clean page listing the services, the general hours, and a request form that captures the service, preferred time, and contact details - so requests arrive 24/7 and the front desk confirms them in batches. The opportunity. The shop stops leaking evening and weekend demand, fills quieter days by steering requests, and looks more professional than a competitor whose only option is “call us.” ##### Use case 3: Giving a clinic a clean intake for appointment enquiries

Situation. A dental practice, physiotherapist, or private clinic gets appointment enquiries through a clunky contact form that asks for nothing useful, so reception spends every callback gathering the basics from scratch. What they want to create. A reassuring, professional booking page that captures the patient’s name, the reason for the visit, and a preferred time up front, so the practice can triage and confirm efficiently. The problem this feature solves. A generic “contact us” form produces vague enquiries that take three phone calls to turn into a booked appointment. Patients hesitate when a clinic’s web presence looks careless about something as personal as their health. What it enables. A trust-forward page - credentials, what to expect, a calm tone - wrapped around an intake form that collects exactly the fields reception needs, so the first callback can confirm rather than interrogate. The opportunity. Fewer phone rounds per booking, less reception time wasted, and patients who feel looked after before they’ve even walked in. ##### Use case 4: Helping a trade quote jobs without playing phone tag

Situation. A plumber, electrician, or landscaper gets job enquiries by phone while their hands are full, loses the details by the time they call back, and can’t quote without knowing what the job actually involves. What they want to create. A “request a quote / book a visit” page that captures the job type, location, urgency, and a description, so they can size the work and propose a time without a dozen missed calls. The problem this feature solves. Phone enquiries arrive at the worst moment and lose all their detail. The tradesperson rings back blind, the customer has moved on, and a paying job evaporates. What it enables. A straightforward booking page that explains the service area and rough availability, then captures the job specifics and contact details, so the trade arrives at every callback already knowing what to quote. The opportunity. More enquiries convert because nothing is lost between the first contact and the callback, and the trade looks established next to competitors who only list a mobile number. ##### Use case 5: Booking strategy sessions for an agency without a sales rep

Situation. A small marketing or design agency wants to fill its calendar with paid or free strategy sessions, but the founder is the only salesperson and can’t chase every interested lead personally. What they want to create. A polished session-booking page they can run paid traffic and outbound to, that pre-sells the session’s value and captures the request so the agency follows up with warm, self-selected leads. The problem this feature solves. Without a dedicated page, interest from ads and outreach dies in inboxes. The agency pays for clicks that land on a homepage and bounce, with no clear next step and no capture. What it enables. A conversion-focused booking page - the promise of the session, proof the agency delivers, a crisp request form - so every ad click and cold-email reply has a single place to convert into a booked enquiry. The opportunity. The founder spends time on sessions with people who already raised their hand, ad spend stops leaking, and the agency builds a predictable top of funnel.

###### Use case 6: Scheduling SaaS product demos that actually qualify

Situation. A SaaS team sends “book a demo” traffic to a thin calendar widget that captures only an email, so the sales team walks into demos knowing nothing about company size, use case, or fit. What they want to create. A demo-booking page that frames the product’s value and captures qualifying details - role, company, what they’re trying to solve - alongside the request, so reps prep for the right conversation. The problem this feature solves. A capture-nothing demo link fills the calendar with unqualified calls. Reps waste demos on people who were never a fit, and good leads get the same generic pitch as tyre-kickers. What it enables. A clear demo page that explains who the product is for and what they’ll see, wrapped around a request form with the qualifying fields sales needs, so every booked demo arrives with context. The opportunity. Higher show-to-close rates because reps prepare per lead, fewer wasted demo slots, and marketing can see which segments actually book. ##### Use case 7: Filling a quiet week for a service business on short notice

Situation. A massage therapist, tutor, or freelance professional sees a slow week coming and wants to fill open slots fast by posting a “book now” offer to their list and socials - but has nowhere good to send the traffic. What they want to create. A quick, single-purpose booking page tied to the specific window they want to fill, capturing the requested time and contact details so they can confirm and fill the gap before the week starts. The problem this feature solves. Promoting availability with no dedicated landing spot scatters responses across DMs, replies, and texts that are impossible to track, so half the interest never turns into a booked slot. What it enables. A page they can spin up in minutes, point a one-off campaign at, and use to collect every request in one place - Contacts in Site Studio - so nothing slips through during a time-sensitive push. The opportunity. Empty slots get filled from demand that already exists, and the same page can be reused and reworded the next time a quiet patch looms. ##### Use case 8: Giving a multi-service provider one clean place to be booked

Situation. A provider who offers several distinct services - a wellness practitioner with three treatment types, or a consultant with a few packaged engagements - points clients to a confusing list or makes them email to ask “how do I book?” What they want to create. A single booking page that lays out each service clearly and lets a visitor request the one they want through a form that captures which service, a preferred time, and any notes. The problem this feature solves. When the path to booking is unclear, interested people stall. A wall of services with no obvious “book this one” button makes the visitor do the work, and most won’t. What it enables. A structured page that presents each offering with enough framing to choose, then funnels every choice into one request form that records the selected service - so the provider knows exactly what each enquiry is for. The opportunity. Visitors self-select the right service and book without an email exchange, and the provider gets clean, categorised requests instead of “I’m interested, what’s next?”

#### Brand Sites

##### Public definition

Brand Site is a general single-page brand or business landing page - who you are, what you offer, and a clear call to action, all on one scrollable page. It’s the broadest of the four Sites page types: where Free Offer, Event, and Booking each drive one specific action (opt in, register, book), a Brand Site exists to establish a presence. Pick it when the page is about being known rather than capturing a single conversion - when none of the other three types fit.

##### Customer problem this solves

A business often needs a page for a specific commercial job quickly, but traditional website workflows start with themes, blank canvases, or general-purpose builders rather than the conversion purpose itself.

##### Why this feature is valuable

This feature is valuable because it reduces manual handoffs around customer jobs such as: give a brand-new product a home on day one, turn a portfolio into a page that wins the work, one link that finally holds everything. It also matters as part of a larger system: the source, conversation, or finished output can remain connected to the broader IdeoLab project instead of being treated as an isolated one-off task.

##### Why this matters in the IdeoLab experience

Framer and Wix now pair powerful AI generation with professional site systems, analytics, SEO, and publishing. IdeoLab should not position as categorically more capable. Its marketing advantage is the connection between research, campaign assets, social content, lead-page creation, and automation inside one business workspace, plus purpose-specific starting workflows. At the feature level, emphasize the workflow around the asset - source context, review, refinement, reuse, publishing, or analytics - not unsupported claims that the raw AI output is universally better.

##### Detailed use-case library

###### Use case 1: Giving a brand-new product a home on day one

Situation. A founder or small team is launching a new product or studio and needs a credible web presence the moment they start talking about it - but there’s no website yet, no budget for an agency, and launch can’t wait on a four-week build. What they want to create. A single page that explains what the product is, who it’s for, and why it matters, with one clear next step (join the waitlist, buy, get in touch). The problem this feature solves. Announcing a product with no website to point to kills momentum - links go nowhere, press has nothing to cite, and early interest evaporates because there’s no place to send it. What it enables. From one prompt the AI builds a full brand landing page - hero with the product promise, what-it-does, an about story, early social proof, and a closing CTA - published to an ideolabsites.ai address or a custom domain in minutes. The opportunity. The launch has somewhere to land. Every announcement, DM, and intro now points to a page that looks intentional, so first impressions build belief instead of doubt.

###### Use case 2: Turning a portfolio into a page that wins the work

Situation. A freelancer, consultant, or other solo professional is sending prospects to a scattered LinkedIn profile or a PDF résumé, and it isn’t converting interest into clients. What they want to create. A clean one-page professional landing site - who they are, what they do, selected work or results, and a way to get in touch. The problem this feature solves. A profile on someone else’s platform looks like everyone else’s and can’t carry a story; a PDF feels static and dated. Neither makes a prospect feel they’ve found a professional worth hiring. What it enables. A focused brand page with a personal hero, a services section, an about that establishes credibility, proof (testimonials or selected work), and a contact CTA - all on one ownable link. The opportunity. Prospects arrive at something that feels considered and current, decide faster, and reach out through the page instead of drifting away.

###### Use case 3: One link that finally holds everything

Situation. A creator, coach, or small brand has a single bio link across Instagram, TikTok, and YouTube, and it currently points to just one thing while everything else goes unseen. What they want to create. A “link in bio” hub - one page that gathers the brand promise, the key offers, the latest thing, and every important destination in one place. The problem this feature solves. A bare list of links has no story and no hierarchy - followers land, don’t know what matters most, and bounce. A single destination link wastes every other offer. What it enables. A branded one-page hub with a hero that frames who they are, sections for each offer or channel, social proof, and CTAs that route people to the right place - all behind the one link a bio allows. The opportunity. Followers who tap through actually understand the brand and find more than one reason to stick around, turning passive audience into email signups, buyers, and bookings. ##### Use case 4: A microsite that makes one campaign land

Situation. A marketer is running a campaign - a seasonal push, a partnership, a single big message - and needs a dedicated destination that isn’t buried inside the main corporate site. What they want to create. A focused campaign microsite that carries one message end to end and drives one call to action, separate from everything else the brand is doing. The problem this feature solves. Pointing campaign traffic at a busy homepage scatters attention and dilutes the message; getting a new page added to the main site means a ticket, a queue, and a wait the campaign can’t afford. What it enables. A standalone branded page - hero carrying the campaign message, supporting sections, proof, and a single CTA - spun up independently and published to its own address in minutes. The opportunity. The campaign gets an undivided stage, results are easy to attribute to one page, and the team can spin up a fresh microsite for the next push without touching the main site.

###### Use case 5: A real homepage for a business that never had one

Situation. A small-business owner - a shop, studio, or practice - has been getting by on a Facebook page or a Google listing, and customers keep asking “do you have a website?” What they want to create. A simple, professional business homepage: what they offer, why people choose them, a bit of social proof, and how to reach them. The problem this feature solves. Living only on someone else’s platform looks unestablished and surfaces poorly when people search the business by name. A full website feels like overkill, costs too much, and demands maintenance the owner has no time for. What it enables. A complete one-page homepage - hero, offerings, about, testimonials, and a contact section - that reads as a legitimate business presence and publishes to a custom domain. The opportunity. The business looks established when someone searches for it, walk-ins and callers arrive already convinced, and “do you have a website?” stops being an awkward conversation.

###### Use case 6: A personal-brand page that says who you actually are

Situation. A creator or personal brand has an audience scattered across platforms but no single place that captures their identity, their work, and what they’re about. What they want to create. A personal-brand landing page that introduces them, showcases their work or content, builds credibility, and points people toward following, subscribing, or working with them. The problem this feature solves. Identity spread across social platforms is fragmented and rented - there’s no owned home that tells the full story, and algorithms decide who sees what. Without a hub, the brand has no centre of gravity. What it enables. A branded one-page site with a personal hero, a body of work, an about story, social proof, and clear CTAs - an owned anchor every platform can point back to. The opportunity. New audiences get the whole picture in one scroll and convert into followers or clients, while collaborators and press finally have a serious page to reference.

###### Use case 7: Telling a mission well enough to move people

Situation. A nonprofit or community organisation needs to explain its mission, show its impact, and invite people to get involved - donors, volunteers, partners - but has no web presence beyond a social account. What they want to create. A single mission-driven page: what the cause is, the impact so far, the stories behind it, and a clear way to donate, volunteer, or join. The problem this feature solves. A mission told only in social posts never builds the trust a giving or volunteering decision needs; potential supporters have nowhere to read the full story and quietly move on. What it enables. A brand page with a mission-led hero, an about/impact section, proof in the form of stories and numbers, and a closing call to act - built and published without a developer or agency. The opportunity. Supporters who land grasp the cause, feel the impact, and take the next step, turning fleeting attention into donations, sign-ups, and partnerships.

###### Use case 8: A local service that gets found and trusted

Situation. A local service provider - a plumber, cleaner, trainer, tutor - relies on word of mouth and a directory listing, and loses jobs to competitors who simply look more legitimate online. What they want to create. A straightforward one-page site covering services offered, the area served, reviews or results, and an obvious way to call or request a quote. The problem this feature solves. A bare directory listing makes it impossible to stand out, and prospects who search the business by name find nothing convincing. A full website is more than a local operator needs or can maintain. What it enables. A clean brand page - hero with the service and area, a services breakdown, customer proof, and a prominent contact or quote CTA - published to an ideolabsites.ai address or a custom domain. The opportunity. When a prospect compares options, this provider looks the most established and the easiest to contact, so more searches turn into calls and booked jobs.

##### Marketing angle library

- Giving a brand-new product a home on day one. Lead with: Announcing a product with no website to point to kills momentum - links go nowhere, press has nothing to cite, and early interest evaporates because there’s no place to send it. Payoff: The launch has somewhere to land.
- Turning a portfolio into a page that wins the work. Lead with: A profile on someone else’s platform looks like everyone else’s and can’t carry a story; a PDF feels static and dated. Payoff: Prospects arrive at something that feels considered and current, decide faster, and reach out through the page instead of drifting away.
- One link that finally holds everything. Lead with: A bare list of links has no story and no hierarchy - followers land, don’t know what matters most, and bounce. Payoff: Followers who tap through actually understand the brand and find more than one reason to stick around, turning passive audience into email signups, buyers, and bookings.
- A microsite that makes one campaign land. Lead with: Pointing campaign traffic at a busy homepage scatters attention and dilutes the message; getting a new page added to the main site means a ticket, a queue, and a wait the campaign can’t afford. Payoff: The campaign gets an undivided stage, results are easy to attribute to one page, and the team can spin up a fresh microsite for the next push without touching the main site.
- A real homepage for a business that never had one. Lead with: Living only on someone else’s platform looks unestablished and surfaces poorly when people search the business by name. Payoff: The business looks established when someone searches for it, walk-ins and callers arrive already convinced, and “do you have a website?” stops being an awkward conversation.
- A personal-brand page that says who you actually are. Lead with: Identity spread across social platforms is fragmented and rented - there’s no owned home that tells the full story, and algorithms decide who sees what. Payoff: New audiences get the whole picture in one scroll and convert into followers or clients, while collaborators and press finally have a serious page to reference.
- Telling a mission well enough to move people. Lead with: A mission told only in social posts never builds the trust a giving or volunteering decision needs; potential supporters have nowhere to read the full story and quietly move on. Payoff: Supporters who land grasp the cause, feel the impact, and take the next step, turning fleeting attention into donations, sign-ups, and partnerships.

#### Site Studio

##### Public definition

Site Studio is the full-screen editor that opens from any created site - whether it was built directly or from a Vision Board. It’s where you refine the AI’s first draft into a finished, publishable site. You can work three ways - chat with the AI, click an element and edit it visually, or edit text inline - and the same studio handles publishing, lead capture, analytics, and SEO/domain settings.

##### Customer problem this solves

A business often needs a page for a specific commercial job quickly, but traditional website workflows start with themes, blank canvases, or general-purpose builders rather than the conversion purpose itself.

##### Why this feature is valuable

This feature is valuable because it reduces manual handoffs around customer jobs such as: fix the first draft fast, restyle without touching content, targeted tweak on one section. It also matters as part of a larger system: the source, conversation, or finished output can remain connected to the broader IdeoLab project instead of being treated as an isolated one-off task.

##### Why this matters in the IdeoLab experience

Framer and Wix now pair powerful AI generation with professional site systems, analytics, SEO, and publishing. IdeoLab should not position as categorically more capable. Its marketing advantage is the connection between research, campaign assets, social content, lead-page creation, and automation inside one business workspace, plus purpose-specific starting workflows. At the feature level, emphasize the workflow around the asset - source context, review, refinement, reuse, publishing, or analytics - not unsupported claims that the raw AI output is universally better.

##### Detailed use-case library

###### Use case 1: Fixing the first draft fast

The AI’s first build is 90% there but the headline is weak and a button points nowhere. Double-click the headline to rewrite it inline, click the button to open its link control and set the destination, and the page is fixed in under a minute - no full regeneration.

###### Use case 2: Restyling without touching content

A founder likes the copy but wants a darker, more premium look. In the AI chat: “Switch to a dark theme with gold accents and a serif headline font.” The AI restyles the whole page while leaving the text intact.

###### Use case 3: Targeted tweak on one section

One section’s spacing feels cramped. The user clicks it, the sidebar shows its Spacing controls, and they adjust the padding by hand - affecting only that section.

###### Use case 4: Capturing and reviewing leads

An event page goes live; registrations come in through its form. The organiser opens the Contacts tab to see the captured leads and follow up.

###### Use case 5: Publishing to a client’s own domain

An agency finishes a client’s landing page, sets the client’s custom domain in Settings → Publish Domain, fills in the SEO fields, and clicks Publish - handing over a live, search-indexed site on the client’s own domain.

##### Marketing angle library
