## 12. Automations

### System-level marketing definition

IdeoLab Automations helps teams make recurring marketing and business work happen more consistently with less manual repetition. It can support recurring content, follow-up, reporting, notifications, and other repeatable work while keeping the results connected to the broader IdeoLab project.

### System-level customer problem

Most recurring content and operations work depends on people remembering to pull inputs, create assets, route them, and report what happened.

### System-level differentiation

Zapier and Make are broad automation platforms with extensive ecosystems. IdeoLab should not compete on ecosystem size. The stronger marketing story is that recurring work can support the same environment where teams research, create, review, and improve marketing assets.

### Feature index

- Workflow Automation Studio
- Automated IdeoLab Content Creation
- Connected Business Tools
- Automation Activity History

#### Workflow Automation Studio

##### Public definition

Workflow Automation Studio helps a business turn recurring work into reusable routines. Instead of rebuilding the same content, reporting, follow-up, or coordination process every time, the team can create a repeatable automation, review it, and rely on it when that work needs to happen again.

##### Customer problem this solves

Most recurring content and operations work depends on people remembering to pull inputs, create assets, route them, and report what happened.

##### Why this feature is valuable

This feature is valuable because it reduces manual handoffs around customer jobs such as: the morning content drop that fills itself, turn a flood of inbound interest into organized follow-up, a weekly report that assembles itself. It also matters as part of a larger system: the source, conversation, or finished output can remain connected to the broader IdeoLab project instead of being treated as an isolated one-off task.

##### Why this matters in the IdeoLab experience

Zapier and Make are broad automation platforms with extensive ecosystems. IdeoLab should not compete on the size of an integration catalog. The stronger marketing story is that recurring business work can connect directly to the same environment where teams research, create, review, and improve marketing assets.

##### Detailed use-case library

###### Use case 1: The morning content drop that fills itself

Situation. Someone runs the public face of a small operation that has to show up online every single day, and the daily scramble for “what do we post?” eats the first hour of every morning. What they want to bring to life. A steady stream of on-brand posts pulled from the topics they already track in a spreadsheet, without sitting down to make each one. The problem this feature solves. Consistency dies when every post is a manual task; skipped days mean a feed that looks abandoned and an audience that drifts. What it creates. A workflow on a daily schedule that reads the next topics from a sheet, turns each into a finished social post with caption and image, and drops the batch into a lab for a quick glance before anything goes out. The opportunity. The feed stays alive and regular, the audience keeps seeing them, and the hour that used to vanish into “what do we post” goes back to the actual business. ##### Use case 2: Turning a flood of inbound interest into organized follow-up

Situation. A growing service business gets enquiries through a web form, and the notes pile up faster than anyone can route them, so good leads go cold. What they want to bring to life. Every new enquiry instantly captured, logged where the team can see it, and surfaced to the right person while the interest is still warm. The problem this feature solves. Manual triage is slow and uneven; the cost of a missed lead is a lost customer who simply went elsewhere. What it creates. An automation that responds to each new enquiry by keeping the customer information organized and making sure the right people know about it quickly. The opportunity. Nothing slips through, response times drop from hours to seconds, and the team trusts a single source instead of scattered inboxes. ##### Use case 3: A weekly report that assembles itself

Situation. Someone has to send leadership the same recap every week, and rebuilding it by hand is the chore they most resent. What they want to bring to life. The week’s numbers gathered, shaped into a clean visual, and delivered to the inbox of the people who read it - without the Friday-afternoon copy-paste. The problem this feature solves. Hand-built reports are late, inconsistent, and a magnet for small errors that undermine trust in the figures. What it creates. A scheduled workflow that pulls the metrics, generates a tidy slide deck or graphic from them, and emails it to the distribution list every Monday at 8am. The opportunity. Leadership gets a reliable, professional recap on time, and the person who used to dread Fridays gets the afternoon back. recurring cost disappears.

###### Use case 4: Welcoming every new customer the same warm way

Situation. A shop or membership keeps gaining customers, and the founder wishes each one got the same friendly welcome the very first ones did. What they want to bring to life. An automatic, personal-feeling welcome the moment someone signs up or makes a first purchase - a note, a useful link, a sense of being seen. The problem this feature solves. Manual welcomes stop happening once volume rises, so newer customers quietly get a colder experience than the early ones. What it creates. A workflow started by a new customer in the store or CRM that sends a personalized welcome email and posts a quiet heads-up to the team so a human can follow up if it’s a big account. The opportunity. Every customer starts the relationship feeling valued, churn-at-the-door drops, and the brand’s warmth scales past the founder’s personal attention. a two-step workflow.

###### Use case 5: Briefing the team on what the market is saying

Situation. A founder wants to start each day knowing what’s being discussed in their corner of the internet, but doomscrolling for it is a time sink. What they want to bring to life. A short, curated digest of the most relevant conversations, waiting for them and the team first thing. The problem this feature solves. Staying informed manually is unreliable and swallows attention; falling behind on the conversation means missing trends and openings. What it creates. A scheduled workflow that gathers the day’s notable discussions, has AI clean and summarize them, and delivers a crisp briefing to email or a team channel. The opportunity. The team starts aligned and informed, spots opportunities earlier, and reclaims the time that used to disappear into feeds. replaces the routine pull.

###### Use case 6: One brief, a whole launch kit

Situation. A solo founder is about to launch something and faces the wall of assets a launch needs - and no team to make them. What they want to bring to life. A single description of the idea expanded into the assets a launch actually requires, all at once. The problem this feature solves. Producing a deck, a plan, and a set of posts by hand takes days the founder doesn’t have, so launches get delayed or shipped half-dressed. What it creates. A workflow that takes the one-line brief and fans it out into a pitch deck, a strategy mind map, and a starter set of social posts, all landing in a lab ready to refine. The opportunity. The founder goes from idea to a credible launch package in minutes, moves while the moment is hot, and looks far bigger than a team of one. today.

###### Use case 7: Keeping two systems in sync without a human in the middle

Situation. An operations lead juggles tools that don’t talk to each other, and the daily copy-paste between them is both boring and error-prone. What they want to bring to life. Data flowing automatically from where it’s captured to where it needs to live, with no manual re-entry. The problem this feature solves. Manual transfer is slow and introduces mistakes; bad data downstream costs hours of cleanup and erodes confidence in the numbers. What it creates. A workflow started when a record changes in one tool that reshapes the data and writes it into the other - a sheet, a CRM, a project board - every time, identically. The opportunity. The two systems stay in lockstep, the team stops babysitting a spreadsheet, and reporting finally rests on data everyone trusts. a simple reusable automation.

###### Use case 8: A campaign that kicks off on command

Situation. A marketer wants to fire a coordinated burst - content, notifications, follow-ups - the instant a campaign is greenlit, instead of manually nudging each task into motion. What they want to bring to life. A single button that sets a whole sequence running in the right order. The problem this feature solves. Manually orchestrating a launch means steps happen late or out of order, and the campaign loses its punch. What it creates. An on-demand automation that starts a coordinated campaign routine when the team is ready to launch, helping the work happen in the intended order without rebuilding the process. The opportunity. Launches go out clean and coordinated, the marketer runs more of them with less effort, and the playbook gets reused instead of rebuilt. workflow does the mechanical part on demand.

##### Detailed use-case library

###### Use case 1: A daily visual that’s ready before the day starts

Situation. Someone responsible for a brand’s presence needs a fresh, on-brand image every day and is tired of opening a design tool each morning to make one. What they want to bring to life. A new image keyed to the day’s theme, sitting in their workspace before they log on, ready to post or tweak. The problem this feature solves. Making a daily visual by hand is a small task that never ends; miss a few and the feed starts to look neglected. What it creates. A scheduled workflow whose IdeoLab step generates the day’s image from a prompt and delivers it onto a lab canvas, attached to a chat where it can be refined in a sentence. The opportunity. The daily visual is handled, the brand looks consistently cared-for, and the person spends their attention on the message, not the mechanics. a single step.

###### Use case 2: Turning a topic list into a week of posts in one run

Situation. A small team plans content as a list of topics but stalls at the part where each topic has to become an actual post. What they want to bring to life. That list expanded into a batch of finished posts - captions and images - landing somewhere they can review together. The problem this feature solves. The gap between “we know what to say” and “it’s made” is where content plans quietly die. What it creates. A workflow that reads the topics and runs Create Social Posts to produce a batch and places the finished content into the appropriate IdeoLab workspace so the week reviews at a glance. The opportunity. A week of content is produced in minutes, the team reviews instead of producing, and the plan actually ships. the first draft for free.

###### Use case 3: A deck built from the numbers, not by hand

Situation. Someone has to present results regularly and dreads rebuilding the same slide deck from a spreadsheet every time. What they want to bring to life. A presentation that assembles itself from the latest figures, ready to open and present. The problem this feature solves. Hand-built decks are slow and inconsistent, and the effort scales with how often you report. What it creates. A workflow that gathers the data and runs Create Slides to generate a populated deck - outline, content, and design done - delivered into a lab for a final pass. The opportunity. Reporting goes from an afternoon to a review, the deck looks polished every time, and the cadence becomes effortless. build is free.

###### Use case 4: Making sense of a tangle as a clear map

Situation. A founder’s plan lives as a messy pile of notes, and they wish they could see it laid out before sharing it. What they want to bring to life. Their thinking turned into a structured mind map that others can actually follow. The problem this feature solves. Ideas trapped as prose are hard to grasp and harder to align a team around. What it creates. A workflow that takes a brief and runs Create Mind Map to produce a structured map, delivered into a lab where it can be expanded into the rest of the plan. The opportunity. The plan becomes legible at a glance, the team aligns faster, and the founder looks organized instead of scattered. is a step.

###### Use case 5: A flyer for every new offer, automatically

Situation. A local business launches promotions often, and each one needs a flyer that nobody has time to design. What they want to bring to life. A ready-to-share promotional graphic the moment a new offer is defined. The problem this feature solves. Promotions go out as plain text because the visual never gets made, and they land softer for it. What it creates. A workflow started when a new offer is added that runs Create Graphics to produce a flyer and delivers it into a lab, ready to post or print. The opportunity. Every offer gets a proper visual, promotions look professional, and the owner never touches a design tool. trigger.

###### Use case 6: Dropping finished work right where the team already talks

Situation. A team does its thinking inside IdeoLab labs, and they want automated results to arrive there - in context - not buried in an email. What they want to bring to life. Generated assets appearing on the right lab canvas, attached to the relevant conversation, ready to build on. The problem this feature solves. Outputs that land elsewhere get lost; context-switching to go fetch them kills momentum. What it creates. An automation that places each finished image, deck, or post shows up as a card next to the chat it belongs to, visually connected. The opportunity. Work arrives in context, the team picks it up without hunting, and the lab stays the single place where everything lives. ##### Use case 7: Kicking off a fresh chat with an automated brief

Situation. An operator wants each new project to start with a consistent brief already waiting in its lab, instead of typing the same setup every time. What they want to bring to life. A prepared prompt or brief delivered straight into a lab chat to seed the conversation. The problem this feature solves. Starting from a blank chat every time is repetitive, and the setup drifts from project to project. What it creates. An automation that places a prepared brief into the appropriate chat, so the project begins from a consistent, ready-made starting point. The opportunity. Every project starts the same strong way, the operator stops re-typing setup, and the team’s output gets more consistent. ##### Use case 8: Repurposing a video into a content set

Situation. A creator publishes long videos and knows each one could become a week of smaller posts, but the chopping-up never happens. What they want to bring to life. A set of social posts spun out of a single video URL, ready to schedule. The problem this feature solves. Repurposing is high-value and low-priority, so it’s perpetually skipped and reach is left on the table. What it creates. A workflow that feeds the video URL into Create Social Posts to produce a batch of captioned posts, delivered into a lab grouped for review. The opportunity. One video becomes many touchpoints, the creator’s reach multiplies, and the back-catalog finally works for them.

#### Connected Business Tools

##### Public definition

Connected Business Tools help IdeoLab work alongside the software a business already uses for communication, email, spreadsheets, customer management, scheduling, forms, commerce, storage, and project coordination. The marketing value is less copy-paste work and fewer manual handoffs between systems.

##### Customer problem this solves

Most recurring content and operations work depends on people remembering to pull inputs, create assets, route them, and report what happened.

##### Why this feature is valuable

This feature is valuable because it reduces manual handoffs around customer jobs such as: tell the team the moment something matters, a spreadsheet that fills itself, capture every form lead in the crm. It also matters as part of a larger system: the source, conversation, or finished output can remain connected to the broader IdeoLab project instead of being treated as an isolated one-off task.

##### Why this matters in the IdeoLab experience

Zapier and Make are broad automation platforms with extensive ecosystems. IdeoLab should not compete on the size of an integration catalog. The stronger marketing story is that recurring business work can connect directly to the same environment where teams research, create, review, and improve marketing assets.

##### Detailed use-case library

###### Use case 1: Telling the team the moment something matters

Situation. A small team misses important events because the signal is buried in a tool nobody watches all day. What they want to bring to life. An instant heads-up in the place the team actually lives - their chat - whenever something noteworthy happens. The problem this feature solves. Checking five tools for changes is nobody’s job, so things slip until it’s too late to act. What it creates. An automation that surfaces important updates in the team communication space where people are most likely to see and act on them. The opportunity. The team reacts in seconds instead of hours, and nothing important sits unseen. and a message step.

###### Use case 2: A spreadsheet that fills itself

Situation. An operator keeps a master sheet by hand, copying data in from other tools, and the re-entry is endless. What they want to bring to life. New records flowing into the sheet automatically, the instant they appear elsewhere. The problem this feature solves. Manual data entry is slow and error-prone, and the sheet is always a little out of date. What it creates. An automation that keeps a shared tracking sheet current as new leads, orders, or submissions arrive. The opportunity. The master sheet stays current on its own, and the operator stops being a copy-paste machine.

###### Use case 3: Capturing every form lead in the CRM

Situation. A service business collects enquiries through a form, but getting them into the CRM is a manual step that gets skipped when things are busy. What they want to bring to life. Each submission landing in the CRM as a contact, automatically, with no one re-typing it. The problem this feature solves. Leads stuck in form responses don’t get followed up, and follow-up is the whole game. What it creates. A workflow started by a Typeform or Calendly event that creates or updates a contact in HubSpot or Salesforce with the submitted details. The opportunity. Every lead is in the system the moment it arrives, ready for follow-up, and the pipeline reflects reality.

###### Use case 4: Sending the right email without lifting a finger

Situation. A shop wants to email customers at key moments but can’t staff someone to send each one. What they want to bring to life. Timely, personal-feeling emails that go out automatically on the right moment. The problem this feature solves. Manual sending doesn’t scale, so the emails that drive repeat business simply don’t happen. What it creates. An automation that sends the appropriate customer email - such as a thank-you, receipt, or follow-up - at the right moment. The opportunity. Customers get the right message at the right time, repeat business rises, and no one is glued to an inbox.

###### Use case 5: Pulling fresh facts from the web before acting

Situation. A marketer wants workflows that react to what’s actually on a page or across the web, not just to internal events. What they want to bring to life. Fresh public web information available when the work needs it. The problem this feature solves. Acting on stale or hand-copied web data is slow and unreliable. What it creates. An automation that can use fresh public web information as context before creating a summary, briefing, or marketing asset. The opportunity. Workflows respond to the real world, and research that took hours happens inside the run.

###### Use case 6: Letting any system kick off a workflow

Situation. A technical operator has a tool with no native integration but wants it to start an IdeoLab workflow. What they want to bring to life. A simple, universal way for an outside system to start the automation. The problem this feature solves. Waiting for a purpose-built connector blocks automation that could happen today. What it creates. A connected workflow that lets activity in another business system start the right follow-up work automatically. The opportunity. Almost anything can plug in, and the operator isn’t limited to pre-built tools.

###### Use case 7: Keeping the calendar and the team in sync

Situation. A coach or consultant books sessions through a scheduling tool but has to manually prep and notify around each one. What they want to bring to life. Each new booking automatically reflected wherever the team and the prep live. The problem this feature solves. Manual handoffs around bookings are easy to forget, and a missed prep step means a rough session. What it creates. An automation that helps a new booking reach the team, update the appropriate tracking, and prepare the supporting material consistently. The opportunity. Every booking is handled consistently end to end, and the human just shows up prepared.

###### Use case 8: Bridging into the tools you already automate with

Situation. A team already runs automations in Zapier or Make and doesn’t want to rebuild everything to adopt IdeoLab. What they want to bring to life. IdeoLab slotting into their existing automation stack rather than replacing it. The problem this feature solves. Ripping out working automation to switch platforms is costly and risky. What it creates. A workflow that can complement existing automation systems so teams do not have to abandon established processes simply to add IdeoLab creation capabilities. The opportunity. The team keeps its investment and gains IdeoLab’s output, with no rebuild.

#### Automation Activity History

##### Public definition

Automation Activity History gives teams a clear way to confirm that recurring work happened, see when something needs attention, and build confidence in the routines they rely on.

##### Customer problem this solves

Most recurring content and operations work depends on people remembering to pull inputs, create assets, route them, and report what happened.

##### Why this feature is valuable

This feature is valuable because it reduces manual handoffs around customer jobs such as: confirm the overnight run actually happened, find the step that broke, prove the automation’s value. It also matters as part of a larger system: the source, conversation, or finished output can remain connected to the broader IdeoLab project instead of being treated as an isolated one-off task.

##### Why this matters in the IdeoLab experience

Zapier and Make are broad automation platforms with extensive ecosystems. IdeoLab should not compete on the size of an integration catalog. The stronger marketing story is that recurring business work can connect directly to the same environment where teams research, create, review, and improve marketing assets.

##### Detailed use-case library

###### Use case 1: Confirming the overnight run actually happened

Situation. Someone relies on a workflow that runs while they sleep and needs to trust it without watching it. What they want to bring to life. A quick, reliable way to confirm the automation did its job overnight. The problem this feature solves. Silent automation is unnerving - without proof it ran, people go back to doing it by hand “just in case.” What it creates. A run history they can glance at each morning to see the scheduled workflow executed and finished. The opportunity. Confidence to actually rely on the automation, and the hand-done backup work goes away. ##### Use case 2: Finding the step that broke

Situation. A workflow stopped producing its usual result and someone has to figure out why, fast. What they want to bring to life. A clear view of which run failed and where, instead of guessing. The problem this feature solves. Debugging blind wastes time and leaves the automation broken longer. What it creates. A run record showing the failed execution, so the person can trace the problem to the responsible step and fix it. The opportunity. Faster recovery, less downtime, and a workflow that gets more reliable over time. ##### Use case 3: Proving the automation’s value

Situation. A founder wants to know how much work a workflow is actually doing before investing more in it. What they want to bring to life. A tangible record of how often the automation has run and delivered. The problem this feature solves. Without evidence, it’s hard to justify leaning on automation or building more of it. What it creates. A run history (and per-workflow run counts) that show the volume of work handled automatically. The opportunity. A clear case for expanding automation where it pays off and trimming what doesn’t. ##### Use case 4: Re-running after a quick fix

Situation. A run failed because of a small, fixable issue, and the operator wants to run again it once corrected rather than wait for the next scheduled fire. What they want to bring to life. A simple path from “found the failed run” to “fixed and ran it again.” The problem this feature solves. Waiting a full cycle for the next scheduled run delays the work that was supposed to happen now. What it creates. Visibility of the failed run alongside the workflow, so after a fix in the Studio they can run it again and confirm success in the history. The opportunity. Problems get closed out the same day instead of lingering until the next cycle.

- Finding the step that broke. Lead with: Debugging blind wastes time and leaves the automation broken longer. Payoff: Faster recovery, less downtime, and a workflow that gets more reliable over time.

## 13. Cross-Product Concepts

### System-level marketing definition

These concepts explain how IdeoLab turns AI generation into a controllable production system: Creations are the outputs, Creative Briefs are review checkpoints before selected multi-step creations, Studios are the post-creation refinement environments, and canvas cards keep sources and outputs visible in the project.

### System-level customer problem

One-shot AI output is fast but often misaligned, hard to refine systematically, and detached from the source material that produced it.

### System-level differentiation

The value is the production pattern: source context, review checkpoints, creation, and structured refinement remain connected instead of disappearing into an isolated chat response.

### Feature index

- Creative Brief Review Step
- Studios: Persistent Refinement Workspaces
- Creation: Finished AI Outputs
- Canvas Cards / Nodes

#### Creative Brief Review Step

##### Public definition

The Creative Brief is a review checkpoint used in selected creation workflows. It gives the user a chance to confirm or adjust direction before the finished asset is created.

##### Customer problem this solves

One-shot AI output is fast but often misaligned, hard to refine systematically, and detached from the source material that produced it.

##### Why this feature is valuable

#### Studios: Persistent Refinement Workspaces

##### Public definition

A Studio is the organizational unit that groups together related items produced by a creation. Every time you create something with an Assistant that supports the Studio pattern, the output isn’t a loose item - it’s a new Studio that contains one or more items (one for each variation you asked for in your prompt). Edits and upscales you make inside the Studio editor later are also added to the same Studio, so over time a Studio can grow.

##### Customer problem this solves

One-shot AI output is fast but often misaligned, hard to refine systematically, and detached from the source material that produced it.

##### Why this feature is valuable

This feature is valuable because it reduces manual handoffs around customer jobs such as: refine AI output after the first generation, keep variations and edits together, move from first draft to finished asset without starting over. #### Creation: Finished AI Outputs

#### Canvas Cards / Nodes

##### Public definition

A canvas card is a single item that sits on a lab’s canvas. Everything that lives on the canvas is a card - whether it was brought in as source material or created by an Assistant. Cards are the building blocks of a lab: you arrange them spatially, wire them into chats, group them, and edit them.

##### Customer problem this solves

One-shot AI output is fast but often misaligned, hard to refine systematically, and detached from the source material that produced it.

## 14. Cross-Feature Workflow Library

These workflows are not new feature claims. They are combinations of documented capabilities designed to help content agents tell the platform story. Use them as article structures, video demonstrations, social threads, webinar segments, sales examples, and campaign concepts.

### Workflow 1: Website research → messaging → presentation

Crawl a prospect or client website, connect the research card to a chat, identify messaging gaps, then build a client-facing presentation from the same research.

### Workflow 2: Website research → conversion map → lead page

Research a website, identify the weakest conversion path, then create a purpose-built free-offer, event, or booking page around the strongest opportunity.

### Workflow 3: Website research → social campaign

Use deep website research as the source for a batch of social posts that reflect the business’s actual products, services, and language.

### Workflow 4: Website research → infographic

Extract a complex offer, process, or comparison from the site and convert it into an infographic for education or sales.

### Workflow 5: Google Business research → review intelligence → ad angle

Pull a local listing and recent reviews, identify the language customers repeatedly use, then turn those themes into ad or social angles.

### Workflow 6: Google Business research → service-gap discovery → offer page

Use review-driven service insights to identify an unmet need, then create a dedicated offer or lead page around it.

### Workflow 7: Local lead discovery → qualification → outreach

Find local businesses, connect the lead set to chat, qualify the best fits, then generate personalized outreach angles.

### Workflow 8: Local lead discovery → audit → presentation

Find prospects, research the strongest lead in depth, then create a pitch deck that shows the specific opportunities you found.

### Workflow 9: YouTube channel scan → pattern analysis → content plan

Import several videos from a creator or competitor, compare themes and performance signals, then build a content plan from the patterns.

### Workflow 10: YouTube video → article → social posts

Import a video transcript, develop a long-form article from the ideas, then create a batch of social posts from the same source.

### Workflow 11: YouTube tutorial → slide deck

Import a tutorial transcript, structure its teaching points, and create a presentation without manually outlining the video.

### Workflow 12: Customer testimonial video → social proof system

Upload a testimonial, pull exact customer language, then create landing-page proof, social posts, quote graphics, and sales slides.

### Workflow 13: Product demo video → launch assets

Analyze the demo, extract the strongest features and outcomes, then create launch posts, slides, a flyer, and a lead page.

### Workflow 14: Training recording → SOP → presentation

Bring in a training recording, use the transcript/visual analysis to create an SOP, then turn the process into a training deck.

### Workflow 15: Meeting audio → decisions → action map

Record or upload a meeting, extract decisions and action items, then create a mindmap of owners, dependencies, and next steps.

### Workflow 16: Voice brief → campaign assets

Dictate a campaign brief by voice, convert it into project context, then create social posts, graphics, slides, and a landing page.

### Workflow 17: Client brief document → reusable project context

Upload the client brief, connect it to a dedicated chat, and keep every subsequent creation anchored to the same requirements.

### Workflow 18: Spreadsheet → results summary → executive deck

Upload campaign or operational data, summarize the findings, then create an executive presentation from the same figures.

### Workflow 19: Product spreadsheet → social content

Use a catalog spreadsheet as source context to produce product descriptions, comparison graphics, and post batches without re-keying specs.

### Workflow 20: Competitor page → comparison → sales content

Import a competitor page, analyze its real claims, then build a differentiated comparison page or sales deck grounded in evidence.

### Workflow 21: Social post swipe file → pattern analysis → original content

Import strong public posts, compare hooks and structures, then create original posts using the patterns without copying the wording.

### Workflow 22: Real photos → social posts

Upload real business photography, create one post per photo, then review and schedule the batch in Social Studio.

### Workflow 23: Event photos → recap campaign

Turn a photo gallery into a multi-post event recap while the event is still fresh.

### Workflow 24: Before/after photos → proof campaign

Use transformation images to create story-driven proof posts and related graphics.

### Workflow 25: Existing image → edit → campaign reuse

Take a strong existing photo, remove distractions or update context, then reuse the improved asset across social, graphics, sites, and slides.

### Workflow 26: Product photo → lifestyle blend → flyer

Blend a clean product image into a relevant lifestyle setting, then use the result as the hero for a promotional flyer.

### Workflow 27: Brand reference → image family

Keep a brand visual or reference connected while creating multiple images so the campaign direction stays coherent.

### Workflow 28: Photo Shoot → social calendar

Create a coordinated people/product/service/location gallery, then turn the selected images into a social post batch.

### Workflow 29: Photo Shoot → website refresh

Generate a consistent gallery for a person, product, service, or location and use the set across site sections instead of isolated visuals.

### Workflow 30: Topic → social batch → schedule

Create a batch from a theme, review the Creative Brief, refine the posts, and schedule approved items.

### Workflow 31: Webpage → social campaign

Use a live article, product page, case study, event page, or offer page as the direct source for a social campaign.

### Workflow 32: Video → social campaign

Use a video as the source for multiple social angles instead of manually rewatching and rewriting it.

### Workflow 33: Social analytics → next content brief

Review performance, identify what message patterns are working, then use those insights to shape the next content batch.

### Workflow 34: Infographic → social series

Create an infographic from a complex idea, then break the same information into smaller social posts and graphics.

### Workflow 35: Flyer → lead page

Create a promotional flyer and a matching landing page from the same offer and project context.

### Workflow 36: Thumbnail → video distribution

Create a video thumbnail, then use the video/transcript to create supporting social posts and an article.

### Workflow 37: Concept Slides → article

Build a conceptual deck from research, then turn the approved structure into a long-form article or webinar script.

### Workflow 38: Exact Slides → approved content deck

Take already-approved content and use Exact Slides to design it without asking AI to reinvent the message.

### Workflow 39: Presentation → social snippets

Use a finished deck as the source for a series of posts, quote graphics, and follow-up content.

### Workflow 40: Research → mindmap → execution plan

Turn a large body of research into a map of themes, priorities, and opportunities before creating assets.

### Workflow 41: Meeting → mindmap → project brief

Convert a discussion into a visual structure, then use the map to brief the next set of assets.

### Workflow 42: Free-offer site → social promotion

Create a lead magnet page, then create social posts and graphics that drive traffic to it.

### Workflow 43: Event site → promotion sequence

Create the registration page, build the promotional social batch, and automate reminders or follow-up.

### Workflow 44: Booking site → local campaign

Build a booking page for a service, create supporting local social/graphic assets, and route leads into connected systems.

### Workflow 45: Brand site → campaign system

Use the same brand context to create the website, presentation, social campaign, and supporting graphics.

### Workflow 46: Scheduled topic feed → social creation

Use an automation to pull planned topics on a cadence, create posts, and deliver them into a Lab for review.

### Workflow 47: New offer → automatic flyer

When a new offer is defined, automatically prepare a matching flyer and place it with the campaign work for review.

### Workflow 48: New data → recurring presentation

Pull updated metrics on a schedule, create or refresh a presentation workflow, and route the result for review.

### Workflow 49: Form lead → CRM → team alert

Use connected business tools to capture a new lead, keep the information organized, and alert the team.

### Workflow 50: Content workflow → human review

Automate research and first-draft creation but intentionally land the result in a Lab or Studio for human judgment before publishing.

### Workflow 51: Idea → full launch kit

Start from one brief and create a presentation, mindmap, social content, graphics, and a lead page as a coordinated launch package.

### Workflow 52: Client onboarding → research pack

Create a repeatable onboarding workflow that collects the client website, listing, documents, and brief into one Lab before strategy begins.

### Workflow 53: Agency template → remix per client

Use a proven Lab/template as the starting structure for a repeatable client workflow, then replace the sources with the new client’s context.

### Workflow 54: Winning post → derivative formats

Import a high-performing owned social post and expand it into an article, deck, graphic, and new platform-specific posts.

### Workflow 55: Published article → complete distribution kit

Import the article URL and create social posts, an infographic, a presentation, and a lead-page CTA around the same piece.

### Workflow 56: Research insight → sales enablement kit

Take a validated market insight and turn it into a sales deck, one-page graphic, social proof post, and follow-up page.

### Workflow 57: Customer reviews → positioning language

Mine review language for repeated value themes, then use those exact themes to refine headlines, social messaging, and presentation copy.

### Workflow 58: Analytics insight → cross-format campaign

Use performance learning from social as the seed for a new article, video angle, graphic, and lead page rather than merely tweaking the next post.

## 15. Master Marketing Angle Framework

The individual feature profiles contain specific angles. This chapter provides a reusable classification system so content agents can intentionally vary the story instead of repeating “save time with AI.”

| Angle class | How to use it |
| --- | --- |
| Pain angle | Start with the frustrating old workflow or bottleneck the customer already recognizes. |
| Time angle | Quantify or dramatize repeated manual effort without inventing unsupported hard savings. |
| Context-switch angle | Show the hidden cost of moving source material among separate tools and re-briefing AI. |
| Existing-assets angle | Show how websites, documents, videos, photos, reviews, recordings, and past content can become new marketing. |
| Authenticity angle | Use real business material - customer words, real photos, real product information - as the starting point. |
| Evidence-before-opinion angle | Lead with research, reviews, live webpages, or analytics before recommending the message. |
| One-source-many-formats angle | Show a single source turning into slides, social, graphics, sites, mindmaps, or other outputs. |
| Review-control angle | Show where the user can approve a Creative Brief, edit a Studio, or keep human judgment in the loop. |
| Specialist-without-the-handoff angle | Explain how a business can get a credible first draft or workflow without coordinating several separate tools/people. |
| Anti-blank-canvas angle | Start from the source, message, or business goal instead of a generic blank prompt or template search. |
| Consistency angle | Keep the same brief, research, visual references, and business context across several outputs. |
| Repurposing angle | Make previously one-use material work repeatedly across channels and formats. |
| Speed-to-test angle | Create enough quality variations to test direction before committing larger production resources. |
| Small-team leverage angle | Show what a founder or small marketing team can execute without adding a separate specialist for each format. |
| Agency scalability angle | Show repeatable client structures, source-of-truth Labs, templates, and automations. |
| Local-business angle | Connect GBP/review/lead research to marketing output that a local operator can actually use. |
| Closed-loop learning angle | Create, publish, measure, learn, update the message, and create again. |
| Old way vs connected way | Contrast multiple disconnected handoffs with one connected project workflow. |
| Tool-consolidation angle | Show where a workflow can happen with fewer exports/imports, while avoiding claims that every specialist tool is eliminated. |
| Contrarian angle | Challenge a common assumption, e.g. “The problem isn’t that you need more AI images; it may be that you are not using the real photos you already have.” |
| Proof angle | Use testimonials, reviews, real source material, or performance evidence to make the content more credible. |
| Control angle | Emphasize that the AI first draft can be reviewed, edited, refined, versioned, or published intentionally. |

## 16. Content-Agent Operating Instructions

An AI agent using this source should follow this sequence before producing public content:

- Identify the exact customer job. Start with the problem the person is trying to solve, not a list of platform features.
- Choose the most relevant IdeoLab capability. Use only capabilities clearly described in this source.
- Choose the buyer stage. Awareness, Consideration, and Decision content should sound different and answer different questions.
- Choose one primary use case. Prefer a concrete Situation → Problem → Opportunity story over generic claims about AI productivity.
- Choose one marketing angle. Use pain, time, authenticity, existing assets, repurposing, control, consistency, proof, or another angle from this source.
- Use the search-intent and FAQ libraries. Treat them as topic and language intelligence, not as claimed keyword-volume data.
- Use competitor context only when it helps the buyer. Acknowledge what alternatives do well and explain the narrower IdeoLab difference relevant to the customer job.
- Explain the customer experience. Describe what the user can accomplish, what becomes easier, and what business outcome the capability supports.
- Connect the story to the broader marketing journey. When supported by this source, show how the result can become another useful marketing asset or inform the next campaign activity.
- Keep changing facts current. Re-verify platform availability, network support, counts, limits, and other details that may change before making precise claims.
- Never invent proof. Do not invent customer results, performance lifts, savings, market share, testimonials, or superiority.
- Stay entirely customer-facing. This source is for marketing communication, not technical explanation.

### Format guidance for articles

- Use one primary search intent and one secondary intent.
- Open with the customer problem, not a product overview.
- Use at least one detailed use case and one broader workflow example.
- For Consideration content, explain category tradeoffs before introducing IdeoLab.
- For Decision content, include concrete customer-facing capability and objection handling.
- Named comparisons should acknowledge real competitor strengths rather than declaring a universal winner.

### Format guidance for videos

- Hook with the pain, misconception, surprising possibility, or before/after workflow.
- Show the real business material whenever possible; transformation stories are stronger when viewers understand what the work started from.
- Demonstrate one meaningful customer journey rather than rapidly listing features.
- Use a detailed use case as the narrative spine.
- End on the business outcome and next action rather than AI novelty.

### Format guidance for social posts

- Use one idea per post.
- Start with a recognizable problem, surprising observation, customer question, or useful demonstration.
- Favor specific examples over broad claims.
- Use real business scenarios, existing-asset stories, and before/after workflows to make the post credible.
- Match the call to action to the buyer stage: learn at Awareness, explore at Consideration, act at Decision.

## 17. Public Marketing Safety Standard

This source is intentionally written for outward-facing marketing use. Content agents should stay within the following boundary:

- Explain what customers can accomplish, not the behind-the-scenes method used to produce it.
- Explain benefits, outcomes, user-facing choices, use cases, and market context.
- Keep all content focused on customer-facing capabilities, outcomes, use cases, and verified market context.
- When a detail is not necessary to explain customer value, leave it out.
- Treat changing availability, platform support, counts, and limits as dynamic and re-verify them before making precise public claims.
- When a needed fact is not stated in this source, omit it or verify it rather than inventing it.

## 18. Content Maintenance Rule

IdeoLab evolves quickly. Review this source whenever a major capability launches, changes materially, or is retired. Competitive sections should also be refreshed before publishing direct comparison content. The marketing principle remains stable: preserve customer truth, update changing product facts, and keep all behind-the-scenes operating detail out of public content.

- AI workflow automation software

- AI website builder for small business
